This series goes from a logistic regression to a language-model system that
is served, evaluated and guarded. It is written for **engineers who can code
and want to understand these systems from the inside**. It assumes Python,
dot products and matrix multiplication, derivatives, and probability at the
level of "a distribution sums to one". Everything else, including tools like
eigenvalues, is explained where it's first needed.

Each post explains one idea from the problem that made it necessary: how it
works step by step, the math with a worked example, what it costs with the
arithmetic shown, runnable code, and where it fails. Each concept has one
home. Every other post that needs it links back with a one-sentence recap
rather than teaching it again. The full list of posts, by part, is on the
[series page](/series/ai-engineering/).

## Table of contents

## Six words, six layers

Six words come up in every conversation about AI systems. They aren't six
separate topics. They are layers of one system, roughly from the model
outward:

| Word | What it is | Where it lives in this series |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **LLM** | A model that predicts the next token, trained to minimize a loss | Parts I–VI build one from a logistic regression upward |
| **Quantization** | Storing the model in fewer bits so it's cheaper to run | Part XII |
| **Fine-tuning** | Continuing that training on your own data or preferences | Part VII |
| **RAG** | Retrieving relevant text and putting it in the prompt | Part IX |
| **Agent** | The model in a loop, calling tools until a task is done | Parts X–XI |
| **MCP** | A protocol for giving an agent tools and context | Part X |

The rest of the series is the machinery around those layers:

- how text is generated (Part IV);
- how models are served (Part XII);
- how they're evaluated (Part XIII) and attacked (Part XIV);
- what else they can see (Part XV);
- the hardware and system design underneath them (Part XVI).

## The map

The seventeen parts fall into six stages. Each builds on the ones before it,
and the arrows show the main dependencies. Serving a model (Part XII)
depends directly on how the model is built, not only on what's built with
it.

Within each stage, the order of the parts matters less than the arrows.
Every post links back to the specific earlier ideas it uses, so you can start
at any stage and follow the links back to what it depends on.

## The running example: the Notebook Assistant

Every idea in the series is applied to one system: an assistant that answers
readers' questions about this blog, in English and Vietnamese. It's a good
test case because its data is real and ours to use, it needs every layer in
the table above, and being bilingual makes it a harder engineering problem.
Tokenization, retrieval and evaluation all behave differently across the two
languages.

It grows one step per post:

| Part | The Notebook Assistant gains… |
| --- | --- |
| I | a router that sends each question to the right part of the blog, first as a logistic regression, then as a small neural network |
| II | embeddings of every post section, and a first language model trained on the blog |
| III–V | a tiny GPT trained on the blog's own text, then modernized with the design choices of current models |
| VI–VII | a real small open model, fine-tuned with LoRA on questions and answers from the posts, then aligned with preference data |
| VIII–IX | a system prompt and retrieval over the blog, with hybrid English/Vietnamese search and a reranker |
| X–XI | tools and memory: it can search the posts, run a post's code, and expose the blog through an MCP server |
| XII | a real server with a measured memory budget, plus a quantized build that runs on a laptop |
| XIII–XIV | a bilingual evaluation set, an LLM judge checked against human labels, and defenses against a deliberately poisoned post |
| XV–XVI | the ability to answer questions about the blog's diagrams, and a decision about where it should run |

## What one answer costs

It helps to know from the start roughly what the finished assistant costs to
run, because so many later decisions exist to change this number. Take one
question answered by an 8-billion-parameter model shaped like Llama-3-8B
(32 layers, 8 key/value heads of dimension 128), stored in BF16 at 2 bytes
per parameter, on one H100 SXM GPU. That GPU does 989 TFLOPS of dense BF16
math and reads memory at 3.35 TB/s. Retrieval puts 2,000 tokens of posts in
the prompt, and the answer is 300 tokens long.

- **Reading the prompt (prefill)** costs about $2 \times$ parameters FLOPs per
  token: $2 \times 8 \times 10^9 \times 2{,}000 = 3.2 \times 10^{13}$ FLOPs.
  At 989 TFLOPS that is **32 ms**.
- **Writing the answer (decode)** produces one token per step, and each step
  has to read all $8 \times 10^9 \times 2$ bytes $= 16$ GB of weights from
  memory. At 3.35 TB/s that's 4.78 ms a step, so 300 tokens take **1.43 s**.

That's about 1.5 GPU-seconds for one reader's answer, and almost all of it is
spent moving weights, not doing math.

Now serve 64 readers at once. Each decode step still reads the weights once,
now shared by all 64 answers. It also reads every reader's own cache of past
tokens. That cache is $32 \text{ layers} \times 8 \text{ heads} \times 128
\text{ dims} \times 2\ (K \text{ and } V) \times 2 \text{ bytes} = 128$ KiB
per token, or 0.30 GB for a 2,300-token conversation.

- **One step:** $16 + 64 \times 0.30 \approx 35$ GB of reads, which takes
  **10.5 ms**.
- **Per answer:** 300 steps produce 64 answers, so decode costs $300 \times
  10.5 / 64 \approx 49$ ms per answer. Add each answer's own 32 ms of
  prefill, which batching doesn't share, and it comes to about **0.08
  GPU-seconds**, 18 times cheaper.

At an assumed \$2.50 per H100-hour (rental prices vary widely), that's about
\$0.001 per answer served alone and \$0.00006 at a batch of 64: roughly 1,000
versus 18,000 answers per dollar.

Two caveats keep these numbers honest.

- **They are ideal lower bounds.** Real kernels reach only a fraction of the
  GPU's peak compute and bandwidth, and Part XII measures how much.
- **Memory caps the batch.** With 80 GB on the card and 16 GB of weights,
  only about $(80 - 16) / 0.30 \approx 212$ conversations' caches fit at
  once, so the batch can't grow without limit.

Every number in this section is derived later in the series.

- **The FLOP count** comes from how a Transformer is built (Part III), and
  post 3.1 derives why training costs three times as much per token as this
  forward pass.
- **Decode's memory bound, and the cache that caps the batch size**, are the
  [KV cache](/posts/kv-cache-explained/) post.
- **What changes each of these numbers:** smarter memory management, reusing
  shared prompt prefixes, speculative decoding and quantization, all in
  Part XII.

## Notation

The same symbols mean the same things in every post:

| Symbol | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| $x$, $X$ | an input vector; a batch or sequence stacked as rows, so a layer computes $Y = XW$ |
| $W$, $b$ | a weight matrix and a bias |
| $\theta$ | all trainable parameters together |
| $z$ | logits, the scores before a sigmoid or softmax |
| $p_\theta(\cdot)$ | the model's probability distribution |
| $\mathcal{L}$ | a loss |
| $\eta$ | the learning rate |
| $\tau$ | a temperature, one symbol wherever temperature appears |
| $B$, $S$, $T$ | batch size; tokens already in context; tokens processed in this pass |
| $D$, $F$ | model width; the MLP's hidden width |
| $L$ | number of layers |
| $N$, $K$, $H$ | query heads; key/value heads; head dimension, with group size $G = N/K$ |
| $V$ | vocabulary size |

Later parts add a few more ($E$ and $k$ for mixture-of-experts, $r$ for LoRA
rank, $\beta$ and $\pi$ for alignment), each introduced where it's first
used. Symbols that belong to a single post, like $n$ and $d$ for a dataset's
size and dimension or a Hessian and its eigenvalues, are defined where they
appear.

A few terms are used strictly.

- A **token** is a unit of the tokenizer, not a word.
- **Logits** are always the scores before the sigmoid or softmax.
- **Throughput** is tokens per second across a whole batch.
- **Latency** is time per generated token (TPOT, time per output token).
- **TTFT**, time to first token, is how long before the first token appears.

## How to read the series

In order, if you're starting from scratch. Every post links back to what it
depends on, so you can also start anywhere and follow the links back. Some
posts that already existed on this blog before the series, like the ones on
[attention](/posts/transformer-attention-patterns/), the
[KV cache](/posts/kv-cache-explained/) and
[BPE tokenization](/posts/bpe-tokenizer-explained/), are part of it. They
are the home for their concepts, and the new posts link to them rather than
repeating them.

[The first post](/posts/learning-is-optimization/) starts with the most basic
question: what does it mean for a model to learn?

## References

- NVIDIA, [_H100 Tensor Core GPU_](https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/data-center/h100/)
  (datasheet) — 989 TFLOPS dense BF16 and 3.35 TB/s of HBM3 bandwidth for
  the SXM variant; the PCIe card is lower.
- Grattafiori et al. (Meta AI), [_The Llama 3 Herd of Models_](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.21783)
  (2024) — the 8B model's shape: 32 layers, 8 key/value heads, head
  dimension 128.
[The attention post](/posts/transformer-attention-patterns/) ends with a
warning: the cost is quadratic in sequence length. [The KV cache
post](/posts/kv-cache-explained/) shows the other half of the bill — a cache
that grows linearly with every token generated and has to be re-read on
every step. Both posts treat those costs as facts of life and go on to
manage them: page the cache, share it, quantize it.

This post asks the other question. What if you change the attention
mechanism itself?

That question has produced a decade of research and, more recently, real
production models that ship something other than plain softmax attention.
The answers fall into three families, and they are not competing versions of
the same idea — they attack different parts of the bill and give up
different things. This post derives each family from the algebra that makes
it work, and then reports, honestly, what survived contact with production.

## Table of contents

## First, be precise about what is expensive

"Attention is quadratic" is true and too vague to design against. There are
two separate costs, they dominate at different times, and an alternative can
fix one while leaving the other untouched.

**Cost 1: prefill compute.** Processing a prompt of $n$ tokens computes the
score matrix $QK^\top$, which is $n \times n$ per head. Each matmul of an
$a \times b$ by a $b \times c$ matrix costs $2abc$ floating-point operations
(one multiply and one add per term), so per head:

$$
\underbrace{2n^2 d}_{QK^\top} + \underbrace{2n^2 d}_{(\cdot)V} = 4n^2 d
\quad\text{FLOPs}
$$

Double the prompt, quadruple the work. With $d = 128$, a 4,096-token prompt
costs 8.6 GFLOPs per head; a 131,072-token prompt costs 8.8 TFLOPs per head —
1,024× more work for 32× the tokens.

**Cost 2: decode memory and bandwidth.** Once generation starts, the model
processes one token at a time and the quadratic term stops mattering. What
matters is the KV cache: every token ever seen contributes a key and a value
in every layer, all of which must be *stored* and *re-read* on every single
step. For a Llama-3-8B-shaped model — 32 layers, 8 KV heads, $d_\text{head}$
of 128, BF16 — that is 128 KB per token, so:

| Context | KV cache |
| --- | --- |
| 4,096 tokens | 0.54 GB |
| 32,768 tokens | 4.3 GB |
| 131,072 tokens | 17.2 GB |
| 1,048,576 tokens | 137 GB |

The second column is per sequence. Serving 20 concurrent 128K-token
conversations needs 344 GB of KV cache — more than four H100s hold — before
a single model weight is loaded. And as [the KV cache
post](/posts/kv-cache-explained/#what-it-costs-the-memory-formula) derives,
decode is memory-bandwidth-bound, so every one of those bytes is re-read
per token generated.

Keep the two apart, because they have different fixes. A method that makes
prefill cheaper may leave the cache untouched. A method that shrinks the
cache to a constant may make training slower. The interesting question about
any alternative is always: *which bill does this pay, and with what?*

## The baseline that is not an alternative: FlashAttention

Before replacing attention, it is worth knowing how good exact attention
already is, because the bar moved a lot in 2022 and a great many "efficient
attention" papers published before that quietly stopped being faster.

Standard attention materializes the full $n \times n$ score matrix in GPU
high-bandwidth memory. At $n = 32{,}768$ that is 2.1 GB *per head* in BF16 —
written out, read back for the softmax, written again, read again for the
multiply by $V$. The arithmetic was never the problem; the memory traffic
was.

**FlashAttention** (Dao, Fu, Ermon, Rudra & Ré, 2022) is an *exact*
attention algorithm — bit-for-bit the same output, no approximation — that
never materializes that matrix. It tiles $Q$, $K$ and $V$ into blocks that
fit in on-chip SRAM, computes the softmax incrementally over blocks using a
running maximum and running normalizer, and writes only the final $n \times
d$ output back. The authors describe it as "an IO-aware exact attention
algorithm that uses tiling to reduce the number of memory reads/writes
between GPU high bandwidth memory (HBM) and GPU on-chip SRAM." Reported
speedups: 15% end-to-end on BERT-large, 3× on GPT-2, 2.4× on long-range
arena. FlashAttention-2 (Dao, 2023) roughly doubled that again, reaching
50–73% of an A100's theoretical peak FLOPs.

This matters for the rest of the post in two ways.

**It changes the constant and the memory, not the exponent.** FLOPs are
still $4n^2d$ per head. What FlashAttention removes is the quadratic
*memory* and most of the traffic. So the quadratic wall still exists — it
just sits much further out than it did, and any alternative claiming a
speedup has to beat a well-optimized exact kernel, not a naive one.

**It is why "efficient attention" needs an asterisk.** A method with better
asymptotics can easily be slower in practice at the lengths people actually
run, because it gives up the dense matmul that GPUs are built for. Several
of the approaches below are honest about exactly this.

With that baseline set, here are the three ways out.

## Way one: attend to less

The first family keeps softmax attention exactly as it is and simply refuses
to compute most of the pairs. The motivation is empirical: in a trained
model, most of the $n^2$ attention weights are near zero. If a query only
ever attends meaningfully to a few hundred keys, computing all $n$ of them
is waste.

The design question is *which* pairs to keep, and the answers split into two
eras.

### Fixed patterns

The 2020 generation chose the pattern in advance, by hand.

**Longformer** (Beltagy, Peters & Cohan, 2020) composes three: a **sliding
window**, where each token attends to $w/2$ neighbours on each side, costing
$O(n \cdot w)$ and therefore linear in $n$; a **dilated** window with gaps,
which widens the reach at no extra cost and is applied to a couple of heads
in higher layers; and **global attention** on a handful of task-chosen
tokens that attend to everything and are attended by everything — the
question tokens in question answering, the classification token otherwise.
Because the global set is $O(1)$ in size, the total stays linear.

**BigBird** (Zaheer et al., 2020) uses window plus global plus **random**
keys, and adds the theory: it "is a universal approximator of sequence
functions and is Turing complete, thereby preserving these properties of the
quadratic, full attention model." The proof leans on the pattern containing
a star graph, which is exactly why those $O(1)$ global tokens are
load-bearing rather than decorative.

That theorem is reassuring and, read carefully, it is also the honest limit
of the whole family. The same paper proves there is a task that full
attention solves in **one layer** but which needs $\tilde{\Omega}(n)$ layers
for any sparse mechanism with $\tilde{O}(n)$ edges. The authors call it "no
free lunch." Sparse attention does not buy its savings for nothing — it pays
in **depth**. Universal approximation says the sparse model can represent
the function; it does not say it can do so at the depth you were planning to
train.

**Mistral 7B** (Jiang et al., 2023) is the production version of the idea,
and it makes the depth argument explicit rather than hiding it. With a
window $W = 4096$, "after $k$ attention layers, information can move forward
by up to $k \times W$ tokens," so 32 layers give "a theoretical attention
span of approximately 131K tokens." Note *theoretical*: this is an upper
bound on how far information can propagate, not a demonstration that the
model retrieves reliably at that range. The practical prize is the **rolling
buffer cache** — because no token is ever attended beyond $W$ steps, the
cache is a fixed-size ring buffer storing position $i$ at index $i \bmod W$,
which cuts cache memory 8× on 32K sequences with no quality loss.

### Attention sinks, and a lesson about softmax

Sliding windows come with a failure that is worth its own subsection,
because the explanation is a nice piece of mechanism.

Naively evicting the oldest tokens from the cache — the obvious way to run a
window — destroys quality the moment the very **first** tokens go, even
though those tokens are usually semantically irrelevant boilerplate. **Xiao
et al. (2023)** diagnosed why. Recall that softmax weights must sum to
exactly 1, a property [derived in the residual stream
post](/posts/residual-stream-explained/#what-softmax-actually-computes).
That constraint has an awkward consequence: when a head finds *nothing* in
the context worth attending to, it still has to put its probability mass
somewhere. Models solve this by dumping it on the first few positions, which
every query can see — a garbage bin the authors call an **attention sink**.
Evict the bin and every head's distribution is forced to renormalize over
tokens it never wanted, and the model breaks.

The fix is almost embarrassingly simple: keep the first few tokens' keys and
values pinned alongside the rolling window. With that, Llama-2, MPT and
Falcon stream to "up to 4 million tokens" without fine-tuning, at up to
22.2× the speed of recomputing a sliding window.

The idea then graduated from a patch to an architectural feature. OpenAI's
gpt-oss models alternate full-context layers with **128-token** sliding
window layers, and give each head a **learned attention sink**: not a token
at all, but an extra additive value in the softmax denominator, letting a
head attend to nothing at all by dumping mass into a bias it controls.

### Learned sparsity

The 2025 generation stopped hand-designing the pattern and learned it.

**Native Sparse Attention** (DeepSeek, 2025) runs three branches per query
and blends them with learned gates: **compression**, which aggregates blocks
of tokens into coarse summaries through a small network; **selection**, which
ranks blocks by importance and reads the top ones at full fidelity; and a
**sliding window** of 512 tokens for local context. The word doing the work
in the name is *native* — the sparsity is present during pretraining, so the
model learns around its own access pattern rather than having a mask imposed
on it after the fact. Reported speedups at 64K context: up to 9.0× forward,
6.0× backward, 11.6× decoding.

**MoBA** (Moonshot, 2025) applies mixture-of-experts routing to attention
instead: split the context into blocks and let a gate route each query to a
subset of them. Its stated design principle is "less structure" — letting
"the model determine where to attend autonomously, rather than introducing
predefined biases," which is a direct rebuke of the hand-built window-plus-
global patterns above. Usefully, it can fall back to full attention, so the
same weights serve both regimes.

**DeepSeek-V3.2-Exp** (2025) is where this shipped at frontier scale. Its
sparse attention adds a **lightning indexer** — a lightweight scorer that
ranks previous tokens — and then attends to only the top 2,048 of them per
query. That takes the core attention from $O(L^2)$ to $O(Lk)$ with
$k = 2048$. The indexer itself still scores every earlier token, so it is
still $O(L^2)$, but each of its scores is far cheaper than a full attention
score. DeepSeek
reported roughly 50% lower long-context API prices on the back of it, and
both vLLM and SGLang shipped support on day zero.

Keep one property of this whole family in view, because it is what separates
it from everything below: **every token stays in the cache.** Sparse
attention chooses what to *read*. Nothing is thrown away at write time, so
any token remains exactly retrievable if the model decides it matters.

## Way two: drop the softmax

The second family goes after the exponent itself, and the argument starts by
noticing that the softmax is the only thing standing in the way.

### Attention as a similarity kernel

Katharopoulos, Vyas, Pappas & Fleuret's *Transformers are RNNs* (ICML 2020)
begins by writing attention with an arbitrary similarity function instead of
a specific one:

$$
V'_i = \frac{\sum_{j=1}^{N} \operatorname{sim}(Q_i, K_j)\, V_j}{\sum_{j=1}^{N} \operatorname{sim}(Q_i, K_j)}
$$

Standard attention is the special case
$\operatorname{sim}(q, k) = \exp\!\left(q^\top k / \sqrt{d}\right)$ — the
numerator and denominator together are exactly a softmax. Written this way,
the only property the mechanism actually needs from $\operatorname{sim}$ is
that it be **non-negative**, so the weights form a valid distribution.

That opens a door. Pick any similarity that factors through a feature map
$\phi$, so that $\operatorname{sim}(q, k) = \phi(q)^\top \phi(k)$, and the
formula becomes:

$$
V'_i = \frac{\phi(Q_i)^\top \sum_{j=1}^{N} \phi(K_j) V_j^\top}{\phi(Q_i)^\top \sum_{j=1}^{N} \phi(K_j)}
$$

Look at what happened to the sums: they no longer depend on $i$. The same
$\sum_j \phi(K_j) V_j^\top$ serves every query. Compute it once and every
query reads from it.

### The one algebraic move

Stated as matrices, that is nothing more than moving the parentheses:

$$
\left(\phi(Q)\,\phi(K)^\top\right) V = \phi(Q) \left(\phi(K)^\top V\right)
$$

Matrix multiplication is associative, so both sides are equal. But they cost
wildly different amounts, because the *intermediate* differs. On the left,
$\phi(Q)\phi(K)^\top$ is $n \times n$ — the score matrix. On the right,
$\phi(K)^\top V$ is $d \times d$ — a state matrix whose size does not depend
on the sequence at all.

Softmax is precisely what forbids the right-hand side. $\exp(q^\top k)$
does not factor into a product of a function of $q$ and a function of $k$,
and the normalizer has to see the whole row before it can divide. Remove the
softmax and the reordering is legal — that is the entire content of "linear
attention."

The saving has a pleasantly simple form. Both orders cost two matmuls, so
the ratio is

$$
\frac{4n^2 d}{4n d^2} = \frac{n}{d}
$$

— the speedup is exactly the sequence length divided by the head dimension.
At $d = 128$ that means break-even at 128 tokens, 32× at 4K, and 1,024× at
128K. It also means linear attention is *slower* on short sequences, which
is worth remembering before reaching for it.

The complexity goes from $O(N^2 \max(D, M))$ to $O(NDM)$: linear in sequence
length. Katharopoulos et al. use the feature map

$$
\phi(x) = \operatorname{elu}(x) + 1
$$

chosen because it is positive everywhere (satisfying the non-negativity
constraint) without an exponential's overflow behavior.

### The same model is also an RNN

Now make it causal. Token $i$ may only attend to tokens $1 \dots i$, so the
sums become *prefix* sums:

$$
S_i = \sum_{j=1}^{i} \phi(K_j) V_j^\top, \qquad
Z_i = \sum_{j=1}^{i} \phi(K_j), \qquad
V'_i = \frac{\phi(Q_i)^\top S_i}{\phi(Q_i)^\top Z_i}
$$

And a prefix sum has an obvious incremental form. Writing $s_i$ and $z_i$
for the running state:

$$
s_i = s_{i-1} + \phi(x_i W_K)\,(x_i W_V)^\top, \qquad
z_i = z_{i-1} + \phi(x_i W_K)
$$

$$
y_i = \frac{\phi(x_i W_Q)^\top s_i}{\phi(x_i W_Q)^\top z_i}
$$

That is a recurrent neural network. It reads one token, updates a fixed-size
state, emits one output, and forgets the token. Hence the paper's title: an
autoregressive Transformer with linear attention *is* an RNN, and the
authors report up to **4000× faster** autoregressive generation on very long
sequences.

The state $s_i$ is an outer product of a $d$-dimensional feature vector with
a $d$-dimensional value, so it is $d \times d$ **per head**, and it never
grows. It is built from keys and values alone, so heads that share them share
one state: the same 32-layer model with 8 KV heads keeps
$32 \times 8 \times 128 \times 128$ values, 8.4 MB in BF16. Compare that
with the KV cache directly:

| Context | KV cache | Linear-attention state |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 4,096 tokens | 0.54 GB | 8.4 MB |
| 32,768 tokens | 4.3 GB | 8.4 MB |
| 131,072 tokens | 17.2 GB | 8.4 MB |
| 1,048,576 tokens | 137 GB | 8.4 MB |

That is the prize: constant memory per sequence, constant work per token, no
cache to page or evict. Everything [the paged
attention](/posts/paged-attention-explained/) and
[RadixAttention](/posts/sglang-radixattention-explained/) posts work so hard
to manage simply stops existing.

### What it costs: the recall wall

If linear attention were free, this post would end here. It is not, and the
reason is the same fixed-size state that makes it fast.

A KV cache is lossless. Every token's key and value sit there individually,
so a query at step 10,000 can pick out token 3's value exactly. A $d \times
d$ state is a lossy summary: every token has been *added into* the same
matrix. Once more than $d$ roughly-orthogonal keys have been written, they
start colliding, and retrieving any one of them cleanly stops being possible.

The literature has measured this precisely, and it is worth quoting the
numbers because they are stark.

- **Zoology** (Arora et al., 2023) found gated-convolution models trailing
  attention by up to 2.1 perplexity points on the Pile, and traced **82% of
  that gap to a single skill**: associative recall, meaning "look up
  something stated earlier in the context." Their sharpest data point is a
  70M-parameter attention model beating a **1.4B-parameter** gated
  convolution model at associative recall — a 20× parameter disadvantage
  overturned by architecture.
- **Based** (Arora et al., 2024) states the tradeoff directly: there is "a
  key tradeoff between a model's state size and recall ability." Bigger
  state, better recall, more memory. It is a dial, not a defect.
- **Repeat After Me** (Jelassi et al., 2024) proves the separation
  theoretically: a two-layer Transformer can copy strings of length
  exponential in its width, while any model with a fixed-size state cannot,
  because the state is an information bottleneck. Empirically, pretrained
  Transformers "dramatically outperform state space models at copying and
  retrieving information from context."

So the honest summary is not "linear attention is worse." It is: **a
fixed-size state buys $O(1)$ inference and pays for it in in-context
recall**, and the exchange rate is set by the state size.

### The delta rule: writing over instead of piling on

One fix deserves its own paragraph because it is a genuinely better
recurrence rather than a bigger one. Plain linear attention updates by pure
accumulation, $S_t = S_{t-1} + v_t k_t^\top$ — nothing is ever removed, so
writing the same key twice leaves both values superimposed. **DeltaNet**
(Schlag et al. 2021; parallelized by Yang et al., 2024) replaces that with
the classic delta rule:

$$
S_t = S_{t-1}\left(I - \beta_t k_t k_t^\top\right) + \beta_t v_t k_t^\top
$$

Read it as *erase, then write*: the first term subtracts whatever was
previously stored at key $k_t$, scaled by a learned, input-dependent write
strength $\beta_t \in (0,1)$, before the second term writes the new value
there. It is memory assignment rather than memory accumulation, and it fixes
the key-collision problem at its root. **Gated DeltaNet** (Yang et al., 2024)
adds a decay gate on top, combining the delta rule's targeted edits with
gating's ability to clear the whole state quickly — the combination that,
as the next section shows, several 2025 production models chose.

## Way three: replace attention with a recurrence

The third family arrives at a similar place from the opposite direction. Instead
of starting with attention and removing the softmax, it starts with classical
control theory and adds what a language model needs.

### The state space model

A continuous-time linear state space model is a pair of equations that any
control-theory textbook opens with — a hidden state $x(t)$ driven by an
input $u(t)$, and an output read off from it:

$$
x'(t) = A\,x(t) + B\,u(t), \qquad y(t) = C\,x(t)
$$

To use this on a token sequence it has to be discretized, which turns the
derivative into a step of size $\Delta$ and produces a plain recurrence:

$$
h_t = \bar{A}\,h_{t-1} + \bar{B}\,x_t, \qquad y_t = C\,h_t
$$

with the zero-order hold discretization
$\bar{A} = \exp(\Delta A)$ and
$\bar{B} = (\Delta A)^{-1}(\exp(\Delta A) - I)\cdot \Delta B$.

Two things make this promising and one makes it hard. Promising: inference
is a fixed-size recurrence, exactly like linear attention, and because $A$,
$B$ and $C$ are fixed the recurrence is also a *convolution*, which can be
evaluated in parallel across the whole sequence during training. Hard: a
naive choice of $A$ makes the model forget almost immediately or blow up —
the same vanishing-gradient problem that killed RNNs.

**HiPPO** (Gu et al., 2020) is the principled answer to that, and it is why
the $A$ matrix in these models is a specific matrix rather than a random
one. HiPPO poses the question as *online function approximation*: given a
signal arriving one step at a time, keep a fixed-size summary that best
reconstructs its history, by projecting that history onto a polynomial basis
under a measure that says how much each past moment matters. Solving that
optimization gives a specific $A$. The HiPPO-LegS variant weights all of
history equally, which removes any built-in timescale prior and yields
bounded gradients.

**S4** (Gu, Goel & Ré, 2021) made that computationally tractable by
conditioning $A$ as a normal-plus-low-rank matrix so it can be stably
diagonalized, reducing the convolution kernel to a Cauchy-kernel
computation. The payoff was the benchmark that had defeated everything
before it: S4 set state of the art on every Long Range Arena task, including
solving Path-X at sequence length 16,000, which all prior models had failed
outright.

### Mamba: making the recurrence input-dependent

S4's efficiency comes from $A$, $B$, $C$ and $\Delta$ being *fixed* — that
is what makes it a convolution. It is also its weakness: the same summary
rule applies to every token, so the model cannot decide that one token
matters and another is filler. Attention does that natively; that is what
the softmax weights *are*.

**Mamba** (Gu & Dao, 2023) fixes this with one change, called **selection**:
make the parameters functions of the input. Precisely which ones matter:

| Parameter | S4 | Mamba (S6) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $A$ | static | **static** |
| $B$ | static | input-dependent, $s_B(x) = \operatorname{Linear}_N(x)$ |
| $C$ | static | input-dependent, $s_C(x) = \operatorname{Linear}_N(x)$ |
| $\Delta$ | static | input-dependent, via softplus |

$B$, $C$ and $\Delta$ become functions of the current token; $A$ stays a
learned constant. Intuitively $\Delta$ is a per-token gate on how much the
state should move at all — a large $\Delta$ writes the token in firmly, a
small one lets it slide past — while input-dependent $B$ and $C$ decide what
goes in and what comes out.

The cost of that is severe and worth stating plainly: **it destroys the
convolution.** A convolution requires linear time-invariant dynamics, and
the moment $\Delta$, $B$ and $C$ vary per token, the system is time-varying.
The efficient parallel training path S4 depended on is simply gone.

Mamba's answer is engineering rather than math, and it is the same playbook
FlashAttention used. The **selective scan** loads parameters from HBM into
SRAM, performs discretization and the recurrence there, and writes back only
the outputs; it uses a work-efficient parallel scan instead of a sequential
loop, so the recurrence still parallelizes across the sequence; and it
recomputes intermediate states in the backward pass rather than storing
them, keeping memory comparable to an optimized Transformer. Reported
results: 3× faster than prior scan implementations on an A100, 5× higher
generation throughput than a Transformer, and a 3B Mamba matching
Transformers twice its size. The default state dimension is a striking
$N = 16$.

### Mamba-2: the two families turn out to be one

The most interesting result in this area is that the two ways out of this
post converge. **Mamba-2** (Dao & Gu, 2024) proves a **structured state space
duality**: causal linear attention *is* a restricted state space model.
Specifically, masked attention with 1-semiseparable structure is a special
case of a diagonal SSM whose diagonal is a scalar multiple of the identity.

The duality's two computation paths should look familiar. The paper notes
that the dual form is "equivalent to naive (quadratic-time) multiplication
by the matrix $M$," while the recurrent form is "a particular efficient
(linear-time) algorithm" — which is exactly the $(QK^\top)V$ versus
$Q(K^\top V)$ choice from the widget above, arrived at from the other side.
Quadratic for parallel training, linear for inference, same computation.

Mamba-2 then spends that insight on hardware. Restricting $A$ from a
diagonal matrix to a scalar times the identity makes the algorithm express
itself as block decompositions built from **matrix multiplications**, which
means tensor cores — the units Mamba's custom scan could not use. The result
is a core layer 2–8× faster while "simultaneously allowing for much larger
recurrent state sizes (8× the size of Mamba or even higher)," with the head
dimension moving to 64 or 128 to match Transformer conventions.

That is the recall dial from the previous section, turned up: bigger state,
better recall, and now fast enough to be worth having.

## What actually shipped: nobody picked one

Read the three families as a competition and you would expect production
models to pick a winner. None did. Essentially every model that ships
something other than plain attention **mixes**, and the dominant pattern is
a small minority of full-attention layers doing exact recall while a large
majority of cheap layers carries the rest. (DeepSeek-V4 mixes along a
different axis — two *sparse* attention variants rather than sparse against
dense — but the principle is the same: keep an expensive path for what needs
it.)

| Model | Year | Structure |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Jamba | 2024 | 1 attention : 7 Mamba, plus MoE — 52B total, 12B active |
| Samba | 2024 | Mamba + sliding-window attention, interleaved — 3.8B |
| Zamba2 | 2024 | Mamba-2 backbone with two *shared* attention blocks, interleaved |
| Nemotron-H | 2025 | ~8% of layers are attention — 4 of 52 at 8B, 10 of 118 at 56B |
| Falcon-H1 | 2025 | Parallel hybrid: attention and Mamba-2 heads side by side *within* a block |
| Qwen3-Next | 2025 | 3:1 — 36 Gated DeltaNet layers to 12 attention, over 48 layers |
| Kimi Linear | 2025 | 3:1 — Kimi Delta Attention to global attention |
| DeepSeek-V4 | 2026 | Hybrid sparse: Compressed Sparse Attention + Heavily Compressed Attention |

Two details in that table are worth pausing on.

**Qwen3-Next and Kimi Linear independently landed on exactly 3:1.** Two
labs, different linear mechanisms — Gated DeltaNet in one, a delta rule with
fine-grained gating in the other — converging on the same ratio is a
reasonably strong signal about where the quality cliff sits. Nemotron-H
pushes considerably further, to roughly one attention layer in twelve, which
suggests the cliff is not a sharp constant so much as a tradeoff surface
that different teams price differently.

**The savings are real and large.** Jamba reports a 4 GB KV cache at 256K
context where Mixtral needs 32 GB and Llama-2-70B needs 128 GB:

Kimi Linear reports up to 75% KV cache reduction and roughly 6× faster
per-token decoding at 1M context. Nemotron-H reports about 3× faster
inference from replacing ~92% of its attention layers. DeepSeek-V4-Pro
reports needing "only 27% of single-token inference FLOPs and 10% of KV
cache compared with DeepSeek-V3.2" at one-million-token context.

## The honest scorecard

A post that stopped at that table would be selling something. The most
useful evidence in this whole area is a lab publicly reversing itself.

**MiniMax shipped a linear hybrid, then went back to full attention.**
MiniMax-Text-01 used lightning attention in a hybrid stack. MiniMax-M2 uses
full attention in every layer. Their engineering write-up on why is
unusually candid: "the truth is that efficient attention still has some way
to go before it can definitively beat full attention." The failure was
specific — at larger scale the hybrid showed "clear deficits in complex,
multi-hop reasoning tasks."

And the reason nobody caught it earlier is the part worth internalizing.
Under the heading **"Benchmarks are a Leaky Abstraction,"** they report that
the hybrid looked *just as good* as full attention on MMLU, BBH, MATH and
LongBench. The standard evaluation suite could not see the thing that
eventually mattered. Multi-hop reasoning is precisely chained in-context
retrieval — exactly the capability Zoology, Based and *Repeat After Me*
identify as the fixed-state weakness — and it is exactly what a benchmark
average hides.

The story has a third act that sharpens rather than reverses it: MiniMax
subsequently shipped **sparse** attention in MiniMax-M3. So the trajectory
runs hybrid-linear → full → sparse. They did not reject cheap attention;
they rejected **throwing information away at write time** and kept the
approach that keeps every token addressable and merely chooses what to read.

That distinction is, in the end, the most useful thing in this post:

- **Sparse attention** keeps the full KV cache and selects a subset to read.
  Recall degrades only if the selector misses something; the information is
  still there.
- **Linear attention and SSMs** compress the past into a fixed-size state as
  it arrives. Recall degrades because the information is *gone*, and no
  amount of later cleverness recovers it.

BigBird's "no free lunch" theorem in 2020 and MiniMax's multi-hop deficit in
2025 are two measurements of one fact: you can spend less per token, but the
budget comes out of some capability, and you should find out which one on
purpose rather than by surprise.

The most systematic study, **The Sparse Frontier** (Nawrot et al., 2025),
cuts both ways and is worth reading in full. In favour: "larger sparse
models outperform smaller dense ones at equivalent cost, improving the
Pareto frontier" — at a fixed compute budget and long enough sequences,
sparsify-and-scale genuinely wins. Against uniform application: "sparsity
tolerance varies dramatically across tasks," with some degrading at even
modest sparsity, and no method wins everywhere — "fine-grained token
selection excels at retrieval, chunk-based methods suit reasoning and
aggregation." One asymmetry is directly actionable for anyone serving
models: prefill and decode have different tolerances, and decoding takes
substantially more compression than prefill before quality moves.

## Choosing, in practice

Putting the whole post in one place. Match the method to the bill you are
actually paying:

| Your problem | What to reach for |
| --- | --- |
| Attention is slow, quality must not move | FlashAttention. Exact, and the bar every alternative must clear. |
| Prompts are long, prefill dominates | Sparse attention. Keeps every token retrievable. |
| KV cache does not fit, or dominates cost | A hybrid with a linear/SSM majority — accepting a recall cost. |
| Context is effectively unbounded (streaming) | Sliding window plus attention sinks. |
| Recall over a long context is the product | Full or sparse attention. Do not compress the state. |

And two rules that fall out of the evidence rather than the theory. First,
if you evaluate a cheaper attention mechanism, **test in-context retrieval
explicitly** — multi-hop questions, needle-in-a-haystack, copying — because
the aggregate benchmarks demonstrably do not surface the failure. Second,
prefer methods that keep the cache addressable when you can afford them: the
production trend, from every lab that has published a reversal, runs toward
selecting rather than compressing.

## Takeaways

- "Attention is quadratic" hides two separate bills: $4n^2d$ FLOPs per head
  at prefill, and a KV cache that grows linearly and is re-read every decode
  step. Alternatives pay one or the other, rarely both.
- FlashAttention is exact, not an alternative. It removes the quadratic
  *memory* and most of the traffic while leaving $O(n^2)$ FLOPs — and it
  raised the bar that every approximate method now has to clear.
- Linear attention is one algebraic move: without a softmax in the way,
  $(QK^\top)V = Q(K^\top V)$, and the right-hand order builds a $d \times d$
  state instead of an $n \times n$ score matrix. That makes the model an RNN
  with $O(1)$ inference.
- State space models reach the same recurrence from control theory. Mamba's
  contribution is making it input-dependent, which destroys the convolution
  and forces a hardware-aware scan; Mamba-2 then proves that causal linear
  attention *is* a restricted SSM, and its two computation paths are the
  same association-order choice.
- The fixed-size state is not a bug to be engineered away — it is the thing
  being bought. It caps in-context recall at roughly the state size, which
  is measurable, theoretically provable, and the reason no frontier model is
  purely linear.
- Everything in production is a hybrid. Two labs independently chose 3:1;
  one lab shipped linear, publicly reverted to full attention over multi-hop
  reasoning, then returned with sparse attention instead.

- Beltagy, Peters & Cohan, [_Longformer_](https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.05150) (2020) — sliding, dilated and global attention.
