  (ICML, 2006) — why precision–recall is the right view when positives are
  rare.
- Elkan, [_The Foundations of Cost-Sensitive Learning_](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.5555/1642194.1642224)
  (IJCAI, 2001) — the cost-based optimal threshold.
- Brier, [_Verification of Forecasts Expressed in Terms of Probability_](https://doi.org/10.1175/1520-0493(1950)078%3C0001:VOFEIT%3E2.0.CO;2)
  (Monthly Weather Review, 1950) — the Brier score.
- Platt, _Probabilistic Outputs for Support Vector Machines and Comparisons
  to Regularized Likelihood Methods_ (in Advances in Large Margin
  Classifiers, MIT Press, 1999) — Platt scaling.
- Niculescu-Mizil and Caruana, [_Predicting Good Probabilities with Supervised Learning_](https://doi.org/10.1145/1102351.1102430)
  (ICML, 2005) — reliability diagrams, and which models are over- or
  under-confident.
- Naeini, Cooper and Hauskrecht, [_Obtaining Well Calibrated Probabilities Using Bayesian Binning_](https://ojs.aaai.org/index.php/AAAI/article/view/9602)
  (AAAI, 2015) — expected calibration error.
- Guo, Pleiss, Sun and Weinberger, [_On Calibration of Modern Neural Networks_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.04599)
  (ICML, 2017) — temperature scaling, and why deep networks are overconfident.
- Quiñonero-Candela, Sugiyama, Schwaighofer and Lawrence (eds.),
  [_Dataset Shift in Machine Learning_](https://doi.org/10.7551/mitpress/9780262170055.001.0001)
  (MIT Press, 2009) — the kinds of distribution shift, including a change
  in the class balance.
The [KV cache](/posts/kv-cache-explained/) makes decoding fast, but it creates a
memory-management problem: the cache grows unpredictably (you don't know how
long a generation will be), and naive systems reserve a huge contiguous block
per request "just in case." The result is that most of the reserved memory sits
empty. **Paged attention**, introduced with [vLLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.06180),
fixes this by borrowing the oldest trick in operating systems — virtual memory
paging. This post shows exactly where the waste comes from, how paging removes
it, and how the same mechanism enables memory *sharing* between sequences.

If the KV cache itself isn't familiar — why keys and values are cached, how it
grows one token per step — read [the KV cache post](/posts/kv-cache-explained/)
first.

## Table of contents

## The problem: reserved but empty

The KV cache for one sequence grows by one token's worth of keys and values per
decode step, and you don't know in advance how many steps there will be. The
simplest way to store a growing thing is a contiguous array — so early systems
reserved a single contiguous chunk sized to the model's **maximum** sequence
length (say 2048 tokens) for *every* request, then filled it in as generation
proceeded.

That reservation is almost entirely wasted. Here are two requests that reserve
12 slots each (a scaled-down "max length") but only actually use 9 and 6 tokens:

Everything colored dark is reserved-but-empty — memory that no other request is
allowed to touch, because it "belongs" to this sequence in case it keeps
generating. This is **internal fragmentation** (space reserved inside a
sequence's chunk that it never uses). There's also **external fragmentation**:
the leftover gaps between chunks are often too small or oddly-sized to fit
another request's max-length reservation. The vLLM paper measured that existing
systems waste **60–80%** of KV cache memory this way. Since the KV cache is
often what fills the GPU (see [the memory formula](/posts/kv-cache-explained/#what-it-costs-the-memory-formula)),
wasting 60–80% of it directly caps how many requests you can serve at once.

## Borrowing an idea from operating systems

Operating systems solved exactly this problem decades ago. A process thinks it
has one big contiguous address space, but physically its memory is scattered
across fixed-size **pages** that the OS maps wherever there's room, via a **page
table**. Nothing needs a big contiguous reservation, so there's no
over-reservation and no external fragmentation.

Paged attention applies the same idea, with a clean analogy:

| Operating system | Paged attention |
| ---------------- | --------------- |
| page             | KV **block** (keys/values for a fixed number of tokens) |
| byte             | one token's KV |
| process          | a sequence (request) |
| page table       | **block table** (per sequence) |

The KV cache for each sequence is split into fixed-size **blocks**, each holding
the keys and values for a fixed number of tokens (vLLM's default block size is
16; call it $B$). Blocks live anywhere in a global pool of GPU memory — they do
**not** have to be contiguous — and a per-sequence **block table** records where
each logical block physically landed.

> [!note] Paging is an old idea, twice over
> Virtual memory itself isn't new, either — the Atlas computer at the
> University of Manchester introduced paging in 1962, decades before it became
> the default in every general-purpose OS. PagedAttention is the second time
> the idea gets reused: first to let a process address more memory than it
> physically has, and now to let a KV cache grow without a contiguous
> reservation.

## The block table

A sequence's tokens are numbered 0, 1, 2, … and grouped into logical blocks of
$B$ tokens each. Logical block 0 is tokens $1 \dots B$, logical block 1 is
tokens $B+1 \dots 2B$, and so on. The block table maps each **logical** block to
whatever **physical** block the allocator handed out — which can be scattered
all over the pool.

For request X (9 tokens, toy block size $B = 4$, so 3 logical blocks):

The number of blocks a sequence of $L$ tokens needs is just:

$$
\text{blocks} = \left\lceil \frac{L}{B} \right\rceil
$$

and the **only** wasted space is in the last block, if $L$ isn't a multiple of
$B$. That waste is at most $B - 1$ tokens — **per sequence**, and crucially
**independent of the maximum sequence length**. Compare the two waste models:

- **Contiguous:** wastes $\text{max\_len} - L$ per sequence (e.g. $2048 - 9 = 2039$ slots for a 9-token request).
- **Paged:** wastes at most $B - 1$ per sequence (e.g. $\leq 15$ slots with $B = 16$), no matter how long the sequence *could* have grown.

For the toy requests above with $B = 4$: request X (9 tokens) needs
$\lceil 9/4 \rceil = 3$ blocks and wastes 3 slots in its last block; request Y
(6 tokens) needs $\lceil 6/4 \rceil = 2$ blocks and wastes 2. For realistic
sequence lengths the last-block waste is amortized to almost nothing — the vLLM
paper reports **under 4%** waste in practice, versus the 60–80% above.

The formula behind both of those numbers is small enough to just run:

```python

def waste(seq_len, block_size, max_len):
    blocks = math.ceil(seq_len / block_size)
    paged_waste = blocks * block_size - seq_len
    contiguous_waste = max_len - seq_len
    return blocks, paged_waste, contiguous_waste

for name, seq_len, block_size, max_len in [
    ("req X, toy",  9, 4, 12),
    ("req Y, toy",  6, 4, 12),
    ("req X, real", 9, 16, 2048),
]:
    blocks, paged_waste, contiguous_waste = waste(seq_len, block_size, max_len)
    print(f"{name}: {blocks} block(s), paged waste={paged_waste}, contiguous waste={contiguous_waste}")
```

```text
req X, toy: 3 block(s), paged waste=3, contiguous waste=3
req Y, toy: 2 block(s), paged waste=2, contiguous waste=6
req X, real: 1 block(s), paged waste=7, contiguous waste=2039
```

Note the last row: with a realistic block size of 16, the same 9-token request
that wasted 3 slots in the toy example wastes 7 out of a single 16-slot block
— still bounded by $B - 1 = 15$, and utterly dwarfed by the 2,039 slots a
2048-token contiguous reservation would have wasted on it.

The contiguous bar is the measured **60–80%** (drawn at 70%); paging drops it
under **4%**, and almost all of *that* is the single partially-filled last block.
Reclaiming that memory is what lets many more sequences share the GPU at once.

## The physical layout

Here's where X's and Y's blocks actually land in a pool of 8 physical blocks.
X holds physical blocks 5, 1, 7 (from its block table above); Y holds physical
blocks 3 and 0; the rest are free:

The blocks for a single sequence are deliberately **not** contiguous, and that's
fine — the block table knows where each one is. Free blocks (dark) can be handed
to *any* request, so there's no external fragmentation: a new request grabs
blocks one at a time from the global pool as it generates, and returns them when
it finishes. Nothing is reserved ahead of time.

## Attention over scattered blocks

If the keys and values for one sequence are now spread across non-contiguous
blocks, how does attention — which needs to score the query against *all* past
keys — still work? This is what the **PagedAttention kernel** handles. The math
is exactly the scaled dot-product attention from before:

$$
Y = \operatorname{softmax}\!\left(\frac{q\,K^\top}{\sqrt{d_k}}\right)V
$$

The only change is *how* $K$ and $V$ are read. Instead of assuming one contiguous
$K$ matrix, the kernel walks the sequence's block table, gathers the key blocks
in logical order, computes the scores block by block, and accumulates the
softmax-weighted values. The output is bit-identical to contiguous attention —
paging changes the memory layout, not the computation. It's the same "one query
against the whole cached history" shape from the KV cache post, just with the
history assembled from scattered blocks.

That reconstruction step is the whole trick, and it's simple enough to verify
directly — scatter an 8-row $K$ into two blocks at arbitrary physical
locations, then gather them back through the block table:

```python

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
K = rng.normal(size=(8, 2)).round(2)   # "K" for an 8-token sequence
B = 4                                   # block size
logical_blocks = [K[i:i + B] for i in range(0, len(K), B)]

# scatter: a physical pool of 8 block slots; logical blocks land anywhere
physical_pool = [None] * 8
block_table = [5, 1]                    # logical block 0 -> physical 5, block 1 -> physical 1
for logical_idx, physical_idx in enumerate(block_table):
    physical_pool[physical_idx] = logical_blocks[logical_idx]

# gather: walk the block table in logical order, concatenate
gathered_K = np.concatenate([physical_pool[p] for p in block_table])
print("reconstructs the original K exactly:", np.array_equal(gathered_K, K))
# reconstructs the original K exactly: True
```

Nothing about *where* the two blocks physically sit — 5 and 1, or any other
pair of slots — changes the reconstructed $K$. The block table is the only
thing that has to be correct; the physical layout underneath it is free to be
whatever the allocator found available.

## Memory sharing: copy-on-write

The block-table indirection unlocks something the contiguous layout couldn't do:
**two sequences can point at the same physical block**. This matters whenever
sequences share a prefix — parallel sampling (generating several completions for
one prompt), beam search, or many requests that share a long system prompt. The
shared prompt's KV blocks are stored **once**, and every sequence's block table
points at them.

To stay correct when one sequence needs to diverge, vLLM uses reference counts
and **copy-on-write**, exactly like an OS sharing memory pages between forked
processes:

As long as both sequences only *read* the shared block, it stays shared. The
moment one needs to write (append its own diverging token), that block is copied
so the write can't corrupt the other sequence's view. The vLLM paper reports
this sharing cuts memory use by up to **55%** for parallel-sampling and beam-search
workloads, translating to up to **2.2×** more throughput.

## Why it adds up

Paging the KV cache does three things at once, all from the same block-table
indirection:

- **Near-zero waste** — under 4% instead of 60–80%, because nothing is
  over-reserved and blocks are the same fixed size (no external fragmentation).
- **Higher batch sizes** — reclaimed memory means more sequences resident at
  once, which is what actually drives serving throughput.
- **Sharing** — identical prefixes cost memory once.

Together these are why vLLM reported up to **24× higher throughput than
HuggingFace Transformers** and up to **3.5× over HuggingFace TGI** at the time
of publication — not from a faster kernel, but from fitting far more concurrent
sequences into the same GPU memory.

Note what *isn't* here: a faster matmul. Every bar runs the same attention math —
vLLM wins purely by wasting less memory and therefore keeping far more sequences
resident in the batch. (HF TGI shown at ≈7×, implied by vLLM's reported 3.5× lead
over it.)

## Limitations: what paging doesn't automatically fix

**Block size is a tradeoff, not a free parameter.** A smaller block wastes
less in the last, partially-filled block (at most $B - 1$ tokens) but means
more blocks per sequence — more entries in the block table and more gather
steps for the kernel to walk. A larger block cuts that management overhead
but raises the worst-case last-block waste. vLLM's default of 16 is a
balance point tuned for typical sequence lengths, not a universal answer —
the "real" row in the table above already shows a 9-token request wasting
nearly half of its single 16-slot block.

**Reuse is still exact-match, block-aligned reuse.** The content-hash sharing
mentioned above only fires when two sequences' tokens agree **and** land on
the same block boundaries — a shared system prompt that's one token longer
in one request than another shifts every subsequent block's contents, and
with it every hash, breaking the match entirely from that point on. Paging
solves *fragmentation*; it doesn't give the engine any notion of *how much*
two unaligned sequences overlap, which is exactly the gap the next post's
radix tree is built to close.

**Indirection has a real, if small, cost.** Reading $K$ and $V$ through a
block table is a gather, not a flat read — the scatter/gather demo above is
cheap for 8 rows, but the PagedAttention kernel has to do this for every
block of every sequence in the batch, on every step. vLLM's kernel is written
specifically to make that gather fast; the cost isn't eliminated, it's paid
inside a custom kernel instead of at the memory-allocation layer.

## Where this leads

Paged attention is really a memory-management layer under the KV cache, and it
composes with the other inference-serving techniques in this notebook:

- **Continuous batching** relies on being able to add and evict sequences from a
  running batch cheaply — trivial when each sequence is just a set of blocks to
  allocate or free.
- **Prefix caching** is the sharing mechanism above, made persistent: keep a
  popular prompt's blocks resident and let every new request that starts with it
  reuse them.
- **KV cache quantization** shrinks each block, so paging and quantization stack
  — smaller blocks, packed with no waste.

The KV cache post asked "the cache is what makes decoding fast, so how do we
manage its memory?" Paged attention is the load-bearing answer the rest build on.
For the rest of that machine — the scheduler doing the adding and evicting,
chunked prefill, preemption, and distributed serving — see
[vLLM Architecture: Continuous Batching and Serving at Scale](/posts/vllm-architecture-explained/).

## Takeaways

- The waste in contiguous KV allocation is proportional to $\text{max\_len} - L$
  per sequence; the waste in paging is bounded by $B - 1$ regardless of how
  long a sequence *could* have grown — that bound, not a faster kernel, is
  what turns 60–80% waste into under 4%.
- The block table is the only thing that has to be correct; the physical
  layout of blocks underneath it can be anything the allocator finds free,
  because attention gathers blocks in logical order before scoring them.
- Sharing a physical block between sequences (copy-on-write) is the same
  free lunch OS forked processes get from shared pages — it costs nothing
  until one side actually writes.
- Paging fixes fragmentation, not overlap detection: two sequences only
  share a block when their tokens match **and** align on a block boundary,
  which is why block-level reuse still misses cases a shared radix tree
  catches automatically.

## References

- Kwon et al., [_Efficient Memory Management for Large Language Model Serving with PagedAttention_](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.06180) (SOSP 2023) — the paper that introduced PagedAttention and vLLM.
- [_vLLM: Easy, Fast, and Cheap LLM Serving with PagedAttention_](https://blog.vllm.ai/2023/06/20/vllm.html) — the announcement blog, with the memory-waste and throughput figures and the OS-paging analogy.
- Vaswani et al., [_Attention Is All You Need_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.03762) (2017) — the underlying attention the kernel computes.
- [Operating Systems: Three Easy Pieces — Paging](https://pages.cs.wisc.edu/~remzi/OSTEP/) — the classic free textbook on the virtual-memory paging this borrows from, if the OS analogy is new.
Everything in the series so far was written in NumPy, with backward passes
by hand. That was the point: every gradient was a rule we derived. From here
on the models get too big for that, and the series switches to PyTorch. This
post opens it up first, so that nothing it does later is magic.

A deep-learning framework is three things, and most surprises, especially
performance surprises, come from mixing them up:

- **A tensor library.** Arrays of numbers with a shape, a data type and a
  layout in memory, and fast operations on them.
- **An autograd tape.** A record of the operations that produced a result,
  so the framework can run post 3.1's backward rules automatically.
- **A device model.** Where the numbers live, CPU or GPU, and the fact that
  work sent to a GPU runs later, asynchronously.

Every number below was measured on the machine this post was written on, an
Apple M4 laptop, using PyTorch 2.11 on its CPU and on its GPU through Apple's
Metal backend (`mps`). The data-center numbers in the
[series map](/posts/ai-engineering-series-map/) are from an H100's
datasheet; the lessons transfer, the absolute numbers don't.

## Table of contents

## A tensor is a view of memory

A tensor is a block of memory plus a description of how to read it: a
shape, a data type, an offset, and a **stride** for each dimension, the
number of elements to jump to move one step along it.

```python

x = torch.arange(12.0).reshape(3, 4)
print(f"x: shape {tuple(x.shape)}, strides {x.stride()}, contiguous {x.is_contiguous()}")
t = x.T                                            # a transpose is a view: new strides, same memory
print(f"x.T: shape {tuple(t.shape)}, strides {t.stride()}, contiguous {t.is_contiguous()}, "
      f"same memory {t.data_ptr() == x.data_ptr()}")
row, col = x[1], x[:, 1]                           # slicing is a view too, with an offset
print(f"x[1]: offset {row.storage_offset()}, strides {row.stride()};  "
      f"x[:, 1]: offset {col.storage_offset()}, strides {col.stride()}")
t[0, 1] = 100.0                                    # writing through a view writes the original
print(f"after t[0, 1] = 100: x[1, 0] = {x[1, 0].item()}")
c = t.contiguous()                                 # a real copy, laid out row by row
print(f"t.contiguous(): strides {c.stride()}, same memory {c.data_ptr() == x.data_ptr()}")
try:
    t.view(12)
except RuntimeError as e:
    print(f"t.view(12) fails: {str(e).split('.')[0]}")
print(f"t.reshape(12) works (it copies when it must): {t.reshape(12)[:4].tolist()}")
```

```text
x: shape (3, 4), strides (4, 1), contiguous True
x.T: shape (4, 3), strides (1, 4), contiguous False, same memory True
x[1]: offset 4, strides (1,);  x[:, 1]: offset 1, strides (4,)
after t[0, 1] = 100: x[1, 0] = 100.0
t.contiguous(): strides (3, 1), same memory False
t.view(12) fails: view size is not compatible with input tensor's size and stride (at least one dimension spans across two contiguous subspaces)
t.reshape(12) works (it copies when it must): [0.0, 100.0, 8.0, 1.0]
```

The same twelve numbers in memory, read three ways (each dot's number is
the order in which the view visits that memory cell):

    <circle cx="95.0" cy="108" r="7" fill="currentColor" fill-opacity="0.5" />
    <text x="95.0" y="111.5" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="var(--color-background)">0</text>
    <circle cx="191.0" cy="108" r="7" fill="currentColor" fill-opacity="0.5" />
    <text x="191.0" y="111.5" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="var(--color-background)">1</text>
    <circle cx="287.0" cy="108" r="7" fill="currentColor" fill-opacity="0.5" />
    <text x="287.0" y="111.5" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="var(--color-background)">2</text>
    <text x="352" y="112" font-size="10" fill="currentColor" fill-opacity="0.75">offset 1, stride 4</text>
  </svg>
</figure>

- **A row-major $3 \times 4$ tensor has strides $(4, 1)$.** Moving down a
  row skips 4 elements; moving along a row skips 1.
- **A transpose copies nothing.** `x.T` is the same memory with the strides
  swapped to $(1, 4)$. That's why it's instant for any size, and why
  writing through it changes `x`.
- **A slice is a view with an offset.** Row 1 starts 4 elements in; column
  1 starts 1 element in and strides by 4.
- **Some operations need the layout to be simple.** `view` only
  reinterprets memory: it works whenever the new shape can be expressed with
  strides, and fails on the transposed tensor, whose elements aren't in an
  order `view(12)` can describe. `contiguous()` makes a real copy in row
  order. `reshape` returns a view when it can and a copy when it must, so
  never write through a `reshape` result expecting the original to change.

(PyTorch's strides count elements; NumPy's `.strides` count bytes.)

This matters for speed later. A GPU reads memory fastest in long
consecutive runs (post 6), so a non-contiguous tensor can make the next
operation slower, or force a hidden copy.

## Autograd is a tape

Post 3.1 derived backpropagation as local rules applied from the loss
backward. An autograd system automates exactly that: during the forward
pass, record every operation and its inputs; during the backward pass, walk
the record in reverse and apply each operation's rule. Here is the whole
idea in 55 lines, enough to run post 3.1's XOR network:

```python

class Tensor:
    """A NumPy array that remembers how it was made, so it can run backward."""
    def __init__(self, data, parents=(), backward=None):
        self.data, self.grad = np.asarray(data, dtype=float), None
        self.parents, self._backward = parents, backward   # the tape: one node per operation

    def __add__(self, other):
        out = Tensor(self.data + other.data, (self, other))
        def backward(g):                                    # sum over broadcast dimensions
            return unbroadcast(g, self.data.shape), unbroadcast(g, other.data.shape)
        out._backward = backward
        return out

    def __matmul__(self, other):
        out = Tensor(self.data @ other.data, (self, other))
        out._backward = lambda g: (g @ other.data.T, self.data.T @ g)   # post 3.1's rules
        return out

    def relu(self):
        out = Tensor(np.maximum(self.data, 0), (self,))
        out._backward = lambda g: (g * (self.data > 0),)
        return out

    def bce_with_logits(self, y):
        """Mean log-loss of sigmoid(self) against labels y, as one operation.
        (Not stable for very large logits; PyTorch's uses log-sum-exp, post 3.2.)"""
        p = 1 / (1 + np.exp(-self.data))
        out = Tensor(-(y * np.log(p) + (1 - y) * np.log(1 - p)).mean(), (self,))
        out._backward = lambda g: (g * (p - y) / y.size,)             # p - y, post 1
        return out

    def backward(self):
        order, seen = [], set()
        def visit(t):                                        # topological order of the tape
            if id(t) not in seen:
                seen.add(id(t))
                for p in t.parents:
                    visit(p)
                order.append(t)
        visit(self)
        self.grad = np.ones_like(self.data)
        for t in reversed(order):                            # from the loss back to the inputs
            if t._backward:
                for p, g in zip(t.parents, t._backward(t.grad)):
                    p.grad = g if p.grad is None else p.grad + g   # a tensor used twice sums its gradients

def unbroadcast(g, shape):
    while g.ndim > len(shape):                               # dimensions added in front
        g = g.sum(0)
    for i, s in enumerate(shape):                            # dimensions stretched from size 1
        if s == 1:
            g = g.sum(i, keepdims=True)
    return g
```

Each operation creates a new `Tensor` that remembers its parents and a
function mapping the gradient arriving at the output to the gradients for
its inputs. `backward` puts every tensor in an order where each comes after
its inputs, then runs the functions in reverse. Two details carry most of
the weight:

- **Gradients add up.** A tensor used in two places gets a gradient from
  each, and they sum. That's the chain rule for a quantity with several
  paths to the loss (post 3.1).
- **Broadcasting has a backward too.** Adding a bias vector to every row of
  a matrix uses the bias once per row, so its gradient is the sum over rows:
  `unbroadcast`.

Run it on post 3.1's worked step and compare with PyTorch:

```python

from tiny_autograd import Tensor

# Post 3.1's worked example: one XOR input through the 2-2-1 network.
x, y = np.array([[1.0, 0.0]]), np.array([[1.0]])
W1, b1 = np.array([[0.5, -0.4], [0.3, 0.8]]), np.array([0.0, 0.1])
w2, b2 = np.array([[0.7], [-0.6]]), np.array([0.1])

ours = [Tensor(a) for a in (W1, b1, w2, b2)]
loss = ((Tensor(x) @ ours[0] + ours[1]).relu() @ ours[2] + ours[3]).bce_with_logits(y)
loss.backward()

theirs = [torch.tensor(a, requires_grad=True) for a in (W1, b1, w2, b2)]
h = torch.relu(torch.tensor(x) @ theirs[0] + theirs[1])
ref = torch.nn.functional.binary_cross_entropy_with_logits(h @ theirs[2] + theirs[3], torch.tensor(y))
ref.backward()

print(f"loss: ours {loss.data:.4f}, PyTorch {ref.item():.4f}")
for name, a, b in zip(["W1", "b1", "w2", "b2"], ours, theirs):
    print(f"dL/d{name}: {np.round(a.grad, 4).ravel().tolist()}   largest gap to PyTorch "
          f"{np.abs(a.grad - b.grad.numpy()).max():.1e}")

# Beyond the worked example: random inputs, a weight used twice, a (1, n) bias.
worst = 0.0
for seed in range(200):
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    X, Y = rng.normal(size=(8, 5)), rng.integers(0, 2, (8, 1)).astype(float)
    W, b = 0.5 * rng.normal(size=(5, 5)), rng.normal(size=(1, 5))
    wo = 0.5 * rng.normal(size=(5, 1))
    ours = [Tensor(a) for a in (W, b, wo)]
    h = ((Tensor(X) @ ours[0] + ours[1]).relu() @ ours[0]).relu()       # W used twice
    (h @ ours[2]).bce_with_logits(Y).backward()
    theirs = [torch.tensor(a, requires_grad=True) for a in (W, b, wo)]
    h = torch.relu(torch.relu(torch.tensor(X) @ theirs[0] + theirs[1]) @ theirs[0])
    torch.nn.functional.binary_cross_entropy_with_logits(h @ theirs[2], torch.tensor(Y)).backward()
    worst = max(worst, max(np.abs(a.grad - t.grad.numpy()).max() for a, t in zip(ours, theirs)))
print(f"200 random cases, shared weight and (1, 5) bias: largest gap to PyTorch {worst:.1e}")
```

```text
loss: ours 0.4932, PyTorch 0.4932
dL/dW1: [-0.2726, 0.0, 0.0, 0.0]   largest gap to PyTorch 0.0e+00
dL/db1: [-0.2726, 0.0]   largest gap to PyTorch 0.0e+00
dL/dw2: [-0.1947, 0.0]   largest gap to PyTorch 0.0e+00
dL/db2: [-0.3894]   largest gap to PyTorch 0.0e+00
200 random cases, shared weight and (1, 5) bias: largest gap to PyTorch 1.1e-16
```

The same numbers post 3.1 derived by hand ($-0.2726$, $-0.1947$,
$-0.3894$), and identical to PyTorch's here. On 200 random networks, with a
weight used twice and a bias broadcast from shape $(1, 5)$, the two agree
to about $10^{-16}$, the last bit or two: the same arithmetic done in a
slightly different order. (The one thing this tape doesn't do that
PyTorch's does is free itself: PyTorch discards the graph after `backward`
unless you pass `retain_graph=True`, and walks it with a work queue instead
of recursion, which a very deep graph would overflow.)

## The graph PyTorch records

PyTorch keeps the same kind of record, one node per operation, and you can
walk it:

```python

x = torch.tensor([[1.0, 0.0]])
W1 = torch.tensor([[0.5, -0.4], [0.3, 0.8]], requires_grad=True)
w2 = torch.tensor([[0.7], [-0.6]], requires_grad=True)
z = torch.relu(x @ W1) @ w2

# The graph PyTorch recorded while computing z, walked from the output back.
def walk(fn, depth=0):
    if fn is None:
        return
    print("  " * depth + type(fn).__name__)
    for nxt, _ in fn.next_functions:
        walk(nxt, depth + 1)
walk(z.grad_fn)

# The graph is rebuilt on every forward pass, so ordinary Python control flow
# decides its shape: here, how many times a layer is applied.
def count(fn, seen=None):
    seen = set() if seen is None else seen
    if fn is None or fn in seen:
        return 0
    seen.add(fn)
    return 1 + sum(count(nxt, seen) for nxt, _ in fn.next_functions)

W = torch.eye(2, requires_grad=True)
for repeats in (1, 3):
    h = x
    for _ in range(repeats):
        h = torch.relu(h @ W)
    print(f"layer applied {repeats}x: {count(h.grad_fn)} nodes in the graph")

# Under no_grad nothing is recorded: no graph, and no saved activations.
with torch.no_grad():
    print(f"under no_grad: grad_fn = {torch.relu(x @ W1).grad_fn}")
```

```text
MmBackward0
  ReluBackward0
    MmBackward0
      AccumulateGrad
  AccumulateGrad
layer applied 1x: 3 nodes in the graph
layer applied 3x: 7 nodes in the graph
under no_grad: grad_fn = None
```

- **The graph mirrors the forward pass, backward.** From the output: a
  matrix multiply, a ReLU, another matrix multiply, and an `AccumulateGrad`
  node for each weight, which is where `.grad` gets written.
- **It's rebuilt on every forward pass.** Applying a layer three times in a
  Python loop gives a graph with three copies of the layer's nodes (7
  instead of 3; the shared weight has one accumulator). Ordinary Python
  control flow, `if` and `for`, decides the graph's shape. That's what
  "define-by-run" or **eager** execution means.
- **`torch.no_grad()` turns recording off.** No graph is built, and nothing
  is saved for a backward pass. For inference, `torch.inference_mode()` is a
  stricter, faster version. Neither switches dropout or batch statistics to
  their test-time behavior; that's `model.eval()` (post 4.2).

Two practical consequences follow from the tape.
- **Gradients accumulate.** `.grad` is added to, not overwritten, which is
  what the summing rule needs inside one backward pass. Between steps it's
  usually a bug, which is why every training loop calls `zero_grad()` (which
  by default sets the gradients to `None`). Sometimes it's the point:
  **gradient accumulation** runs several small batches, calls `backward` on
  each, and steps once, to train with a batch too big for memory.
- **The tape holds memory.** To run post 3.1's rules, the backward pass
  needs what each operation saved: its inputs for a matrix multiply, its
  output for a ReLU or a sigmoid. The graph keeps them alive until
  `backward` runs. That's the activation memory post 3.1 described. `no_grad`
  avoids it for inference, and activation checkpointing,
  `torch.utils.checkpoint.checkpoint(fn, x, use_reentrant=False)`, trades
  it for recomputation during training.

## Devices, and why GPU timings lie

A tensor lives on a device, and operations run where their inputs are.
Moving data between devices is an explicit copy (`.to("mps")`, `.cpu()`),
and it's slow relative to computing, so the rule is to move once and stay.

The less obvious part is timing. A GPU call returns as soon as the work is
*queued*, not when it's done. That's what keeps the GPU busy while Python
prepares the next operation, and it means a naive timer measures the wrong
thing:

```python

def timed(fn, device, trials=10):
    """Median seconds per call. GPU work is queued, not finished, when a call returns,
    so wait for the device before reading the clock."""
    sync = torch.mps.synchronize if device == "mps" else (lambda: None)
    fn(); sync()                                        # warm up
    times = []
    for _ in range(trials):
        t0 = time.perf_counter(); fn(); sync()
        times.append(time.perf_counter() - t0)
    return sorted(times)[trials // 2]

cases = [("cpu", torch.float32), ("mps", torch.float32), ("mps", torch.float16), ("mps", torch.bfloat16)]
for n in (256, 1024, 4096):
    row = [f"n = {n:4d}:"]
    for device, dt in cases:
        a = torch.randn(n, n, device=device, dtype=dt)
        b = torch.randn(n, n, device=device, dtype=dt)
        s = timed(lambda: a @ b, device)
        row.append(f"{device} {str(dt).split('.')[1]:8s} {2 * n ** 3 / s / 1e12:5.2f}")
    print("  ".join(row) + "  TFLOP/s", flush=True)

# The same GPU matmul, timed without waiting for it: the clock only sees the launch.
a = torch.randn(4096, 4096, device="mps")
a @ a; torch.mps.synchronize()
t0 = time.perf_counter(); a @ a; t_launch = time.perf_counter() - t0
torch.mps.synchronize()
print(f"4096 matmul on mps, no synchronize: {t_launch * 1e3:.2f} ms 'measured', "
      f"{timed(lambda: a @ a, 'mps') * 1e3:.2f} ms real")

# Synchronizing after every call also measures the round trip to the GPU.
# Queue many calls, synchronize once, and the GPU can overlap them.
a = torch.randn(256, 256, device="mps")
a @ a; torch.mps.synchronize()
t0 = time.perf_counter()
for _ in range(200):
    a @ a
torch.mps.synchronize()
queued = (time.perf_counter() - t0) / 200
print(f"256 matmul on mps: {timed(lambda: a @ a, 'mps') * 1e6:.0f} us per call waiting each time, "
      f"{queued * 1e6:.0f} us per call when 200 are queued ({2 * 256 ** 3 / queued / 1e12:.2f} TFLOP/s)")
```

```text
n =  256:  cpu float32   1.36  mps float32   0.12  mps float16   0.14  mps bfloat16  0.13  TFLOP/s
n = 1024:  cpu float32   1.88  mps float32   1.52  mps float16   2.38  mps bfloat16  2.46  TFLOP/s
n = 4096:  cpu float32   1.45  mps float32   2.99  mps float16   3.43  mps bfloat16  3.42  TFLOP/s
4096 matmul on mps, no synchronize: 0.05 ms 'measured', 47.58 ms real
256 matmul on mps: 230 us per call waiting each time, 30 us per call when 200 are queued (1.12 TFLOP/s)
```

- **An unsynchronized timer is wrong by orders of magnitude.** The 4096
  matrix multiply "took" 0.05 ms when the clock stopped at the call's
  return, and 47.58 ms when it waited for the result, about 1,000 times
  longer here. The first number is the time to queue the work. A benchmark
  needs a synchronize before each clock reading: `torch.cuda.synchronize()`
  on NVIDIA, `torch.mps.synchronize()` here. (`torch.utils.benchmark.Timer`
  handles this and the warm-up for you.)
- **But don't synchronize inside the work.** Timed one call at a time, a
  $256 \times 256$ multiply takes 230 µs on this GPU, 0.12 TFLOP/s, because
  every measurement includes a full round trip to the GPU and back. Queue
  200 of them and synchronize once, and each takes 30 µs, 1.1 TFLOP/s: the
  GPU overlaps the calls. Real training queues work the same way, which is
  why anything that forces a wait in the loop, `.item()`, `.cpu()`,
  printing a tensor, branching on a tensor's value, costs more than it looks.
- **Small work still doesn't pay for the GPU.** Even queued, the $256
  \times 256$ multiply is slower on this GPU than on this laptop's CPU (1.4
  TFLOP/s). At $4096 \times 4096$ the GPU is ahead, 3.4 TFLOP/s in BF16
  against the CPU's 1.5 in FP32. This CPU is unusually fast at matrix math
  because Apple's chips have matrix units that PyTorch reaches through
  Apple's Accelerate library; a typical server CPU is much further behind a
  data-center GPU.
- **Scale matters more than the chip.** The H100 in the series map does
  989 TFLOP/s of dense BF16, about 300 times this laptop's GPU, and its BF16
  tensor cores are about 15 times its own FP32 rate (989 against 67). Here
  BF16 is only about 15% faster than FP32. (On an H100, PyTorch's
  `torch.set_float32_matmul_precision("high")` runs FP32 matrix multiplies
  in TF32 on the tensor cores, 494 TFLOP/s dense, which narrows that gap to
  2×.) The habits transfer, move data
  once, batch work into big operations, synchronize before timing, but
  absolute speeds and precision trade-offs don't.

## Numbers, bit by bit

A floating-point number stores a sign, an **exponent**, which sets its
range, and a **mantissa**, which sets its precision. The three formats deep
learning uses split their bits differently:

```python

bits = {torch.float32: (8, 23), torch.float16: (5, 10), torch.bfloat16: (8, 7)}
print(f"{'':9s} {'exponent':>8s} {'mantissa':>8s} {'largest':>10s} {'smallest normal':>16s} "
      f"{'smallest subnormal':>19s} {'step at 1.0':>12s}")
for dt, (e, m) in bits.items():
    f = torch.finfo(dt)                                   # a subnormal is the normal minimum's last bits
    print(f"{str(dt).split('.')[1]:9s} {e:8d} {m:8d} {f.max:10.3g} {f.tiny:16.3g} "
          f"{f.tiny * f.eps:19.3g} {f.eps:12.3g}")

show = lambda v, dt: torch.tensor(v, dtype=torch.float32).to(dt).item()
print()
for v in (1e-8, 70_000.0, 1.001):
    print(f"{v:>9g} stored as  float16: {show(v, torch.float16):<12.6g} bfloat16: {show(v, torch.bfloat16):.6g}")

# Adding many small updates to one number, as an optimizer does to a weight.
for dt in (torch.float32, torch.bfloat16):
    w = torch.tensor(1.0, dtype=dt)
    for _ in range(10_000):
        w = w + torch.tensor(1e-4, dtype=dt)
    print(f"1 + 10,000 steps of 0.0001 in {str(dt).split('.')[1]:8s} = {w.item():.4f} (should be 2.0000)")

# FP16's narrow range: small gradients vanish unless the loss is scaled up first.
g = torch.tensor([1e-8, 2e-7, 1e-4])
fmt = lambda t: "[" + ", ".join(f"{v:.3g}" for v in t.tolist()) + "]"
print(f"gradients {fmt(g)} in float16: {fmt(g.half().float())}")
print(f"scaled by 1024, stored, unscaled: {fmt((g * 1024).half().float() / 1024)}")
```

```text
          exponent mantissa    largest  smallest normal  smallest subnormal  step at 1.0
float32          8       23    3.4e+38         1.18e-38             1.4e-45     1.19e-07
float16          5       10   6.55e+04          6.1e-05            5.96e-08     0.000977
bfloat16         8        7   3.39e+38         1.18e-38            9.18e-41      0.00781

    1e-08 stored as  float16: 0            bfloat16: 1.00117e-08
    70000 stored as  float16: inf          bfloat16: 70144
    1.001 stored as  float16: 1.00098      bfloat16: 1
1 + 10,000 steps of 0.0001 in float32  = 2.0002 (should be 2.0000)
1 + 10,000 steps of 0.0001 in bfloat16 = 1.0000 (should be 2.0000)
gradients [1e-08, 2e-07, 0.0001] in float16: [0, 1.79e-07, 0.0001]
scaled by 1024, stored, unscaled: [1e-08, 2e-07, 0.0001]
```

    <rect x="114" y="60" width="109" height="20" rx="2" fill="currentColor" fill-opacity="0.18" />
    <text x="169.0" y="74" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="currentColor">10</text>
    <text x="0" y="108" font-size="12" fill="currentColor">BF16</text>
    <rect x="48" y="94" width="10" height="20" rx="2" fill="currentColor" fill-opacity="0.75" />
    <rect x="59" y="94" width="87" height="20" rx="2" fill="currentColor" fill-opacity="0.45" />
    <text x="103.0" y="108" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="currentColor">8</text>
    <rect x="147" y="94" width="76" height="20" rx="2" fill="currentColor" fill-opacity="0.18" />
    <text x="185.5" y="108" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="currentColor">7</text>
  </svg>
</figure>

- **FP32** has 8 exponent bits and 23 mantissa bits: a range up to $3.4
  \times 10^{38}$ and a step of about $10^{-7}$ near 1.
- **FP16** has 5 exponent bits and 10 mantissa bits, three more than BF16.
  Its range stops at 65,504, so 70,000 becomes infinity. Below its smallest
  *normal* number, $6.1 \times 10^{-5}$, it keeps going with **subnormal**
  numbers, which give up precision bit by bit, down to $6 \times 10^{-8}$.
  So $2 \times 10^{-7}$ comes back 10% off, and $10^{-8}$ is past the end:
  it becomes 0.
- **BF16** ("brain float") keeps FP32's 8 exponent bits and cuts the
  mantissa to 7. Its range is FP32's, so $10^{-8}$ is fine and 70,000 is in
  range, stored as 70,144. Its precision is poor: near 1, the next number is
  1.0078, so 1.001 is stored as 1.

**Why BF16 won for training.** Gradients span a huge range, and many are
tiny. In FP16 the small ones fall into the subnormals or underflow to zero,
as the last lines show. The fix is **loss scaling**: multiply the loss by a
large factor, so by the chain rule every gradient is that much larger and
out of the danger zone, then divide it back out in FP32 before the update
(Micikevicius et al., 2017). The demo scales the gradients directly, which
is the same thing. PyTorch's `torch.amp.GradScaler` does it adaptively: it
starts the scale at $2^{16}$, skips any step whose gradients overflow and
halves the scale, and doubles it again after 2,000 clean steps. (Unscale
before clipping gradients, with `scaler.unscale_(opt)`.) FP16's 65,504
ceiling can also overflow in the forward pass, in large activations or
attention scores, which loss scaling can't fix. BF16 has FP32's range, so it
needs no loss scaling at all. And its low precision matters little for
matrix multiplications because the hardware multiplies BF16 inputs but adds
up the products in FP32, and autocast keeps numerically sensitive
operations, softmax, norms, the loss, in FP32 (Kalamkar et al., 2019). BF16
spread with the hardware that supports it, first Google's TPUs, then
NVIDIA's GPUs from the A100 on. FP16 is still common for inference, and
newer GPUs train with 8-bit formats for the matrix math, still with FP32
weights and higher-precision accumulation (Part XII).

**Why the weights stay in FP32 anyway.** The accumulation line is the
reason. Adding $10^{-4}$ to 1, ten thousand times, should give 2. In BF16
it gives 1: each addition is smaller than half the gap between 1 and the
next representable number, so it rounds away, every time. (Even FP32
drifts a little, to 2.0002.) An optimizer does exactly this. Each update is
typically $10^{-3}$ to $10^{-5}$ of the weight it changes, below BF16's
half-step of about 0.4%, so in BF16 most updates would round away. So the
weights and the optimizer's state are kept in FP32, and only the heavy math
runs in BF16. That's **mixed precision**. The same reason is why the
matrix hardware accumulates in FP32: a long sum in BF16 would stall the same
way.

## The router, in PyTorch

Here is post 3.2's four-way router trained the way post 4.1 trained it,
with PyTorch doing the work, and post 4.1's hand-written `optim.py` running
alongside on the same mini-batches:

```python

from router_data import split
from router4 import four_way
from softmax_basics import softmax
from optim import Adam

((Xtr, ytr), (Xva, yva), _), _ = four_way(*split(0))
X, y = torch.tensor(Xtr, dtype=torch.float64), torch.tensor(ytr)

# The four-way router as a PyTorch module, trained the way post 4.1 trained it.
model = torch.nn.Linear(X.shape[1], 4, dtype=torch.float64)
torch.nn.init.zeros_(model.weight); torch.nn.init.zeros_(model.bias)
opt = torch.optim.AdamW([{"params": [model.weight], "weight_decay": 0.3},
                         {"params": [model.bias], "weight_decay": 0.0}],   # no decay on biases
                        lr=0.01)

# The same run with post 4.1's hand-written AdamW, on the same mini-batches.
W, b, ours = np.zeros((Xtr.shape[1], 4)), np.zeros(4), Adam(0.01, decay=0.3)

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
for step in range(1, 3001):
    i = rng.choice(len(ytr), 16, replace=False)
    loss = torch.nn.functional.cross_entropy(model(X[i]), y[i])   # takes logits (post 3.2)
    opt.zero_grad()                                               # gradients accumulate otherwise
    loss.backward()
    opt.step()
    G = (softmax(Xtr[i] @ W + b) - np.eye(4)[ytr[i]]) / 16
    ours.step(W, Xtr[i].T @ G, "W"); ours.step(b, G.sum(0), "b")
    if step in (1, 10, 3000):
        gap = np.abs(model.weight.detach().numpy().T - W).max()
        print(f"step {step:4d}: loss {loss.item():.4f}, largest gap between PyTorch and optim.py weights {gap:.1e}")

with torch.no_grad():
    acc = (model(torch.tensor(Xva)).argmax(1).numpy() == yva).mean()
print(f"validation accuracy {acc:.3f}")
```

```text
step    1: loss 1.3863, largest gap between PyTorch and optim.py weights 1.7e-18
step   10: loss 1.1757, largest gap between PyTorch and optim.py weights 2.8e-17
step 3000: loss 0.0428, largest gap between PyTorch and optim.py weights 1.1e-15
validation accuracy 0.750
```

The standard training loop is four lines: compute the loss, `zero_grad`,
`backward`, `step`. Two details are worth noticing.

- **Weight decay by parameter group.** `AdamW` takes groups of parameters
  with their own settings; here the bias gets no decay, as post 4.1
  recommended.
- **The hand-written version matches.** After 3,000 steps the two sets of
  weights differ by $10^{-15}$, which is floating-point rounding. Post 4.1's
  `optim.py` is PyTorch's AdamW, and PyTorch's AdamW is post 4.1's
  equations.

## What a training step keeps in memory

Post 4.1 counted 16 bytes per parameter for mixed-precision Adam, using the
ZeRO paper's accounting: BF16 weights and gradients, plus an FP32 master
copy and Adam's two FP32 averages. PyTorch's usual mixed precision,
`torch.autocast`, arrives at the same total differently. Measured:

```python

# Count the bytes a training step actually keeps, for a small MLP trained
# with AdamW, the way mixed precision usually runs in PyTorch: FP32 weights,
# BF16 math inside autocast.
model = torch.nn.Sequential(torch.nn.Linear(1024, 4096), torch.nn.ReLU(), torch.nn.Linear(4096, 1024))
opt = torch.optim.AdamW(model.parameters(), lr=1e-3)
x = torch.randn(64, 1024)
saved = []                                              # what autograd keeps for backward
weights = {p.shape for p in model.parameters() if p.dim() == 2}
with torch.autograd.graph.saved_tensors_hooks(lambda t: saved.append(t) or t, lambda t: t):
    with torch.autocast("cpu", dtype=torch.bfloat16):
        loss = model(x).float().pow(2).mean()
loss.backward()
opt.step()

size = lambda ts: sum(t.numel() * t.element_size() for t in ts)
dtype = lambda ts: str(next(iter(ts)).dtype).split(".")[1]
n = sum(p.numel() for p in model.parameters())
state = [t for s in opt.state.values() for t in s.values() if t.dim() > 0]
parts = {"weights": list(model.parameters()),
         "gradients": [p.grad for p in model.parameters()],
         "Adam m and v": state}
for name, ts in parts.items():
    print(f"{name:13s} {size(ts) / n:4.1f} bytes/param  ({dtype(ts)})")
print(f"{'total':13s} {sum(size(ts) for ts in parts.values()) / n:4.1f} bytes/param for {n:,} parameters")
copies = [t for t in saved if t.dtype == torch.bfloat16 and t.shape in weights]
print(f"during the step: {len(copies)} BF16 weight copies saved for backward, "
      f"{size(copies) / n:.1f} more bytes/param")
```

```text
weights        4.0 bytes/param  (float32)
gradients      4.0 bytes/param  (float32)
Adam m and v   8.0 bytes/param  (float32)
total         16.0 bytes/param for 8,393,728 parameters
during the step: 1 BF16 weight copies saved for backward, 1.0 more bytes/param
```

Under autocast the FP32 weights *are* the master copy. Autocast casts each
one to BF16 when a matrix multiply first uses it, reuses that cast for the
rest of the forward pass, and the multiply keeps the BF16 copy for
backward. The gradients flow back through the cast and land in FP32. What
persists between steps is $4 + 4 + 8 = 16$ bytes per parameter, the same
total as ZeRO's accounting (which uses FP16, the same size as BF16), split
differently. During the step, the saved BF16 copies add up to 2 more bytes
per parameter in a deep network. Here it's 1, because the first layer's
input needs no gradient, so its weight's copy isn't saved. The split
matters at scale: FP32 gradients mean a data-parallel all-reduce moves 4
bytes per parameter instead of 2, which is why sharded setups keep 16-bit
gradients.

## Graphs, eager, and compilation

Eager execution, running each operation as Python reaches it, is easy to
debug and lets Python control flow shape the model. It has a cost: each
operation is launched separately, reads its inputs from memory and writes
its output back, and the framework can't see the next operation coming.
Post 4.2 showed that simple elementwise work is bound by memory traffic,
so a chain of four such operations pays for four round trips.

**TensorFlow** started from the other end (Abadi et al., 2016). In its first
version you built a static graph first and ran it afterwards, which let
TensorFlow optimize and plan the whole graph (and later compile it with
XLA), but made debugging and dynamic models awkward. TensorFlow 2 made
eager the default and kept graphs behind `tf.function`. PyTorch started
eager (Paszke et al., 2019), following Chainer, which coined
"define-by-run", and added compilation later: **`torch.compile`** (Ansel et
al., 2024) watches the Python code run, captures the tensor operations into
a graph, compiles the backward pass too, and generates fused kernels,
Triton on NVIDIA GPUs and C++ on CPUs. Code it can't capture falls back to
eager at a "graph break", and a new input shape can trigger a recompile, so
the compilation cost is paid once only if shapes stay stable.
`mode="reduce-overhead"` also replays whole sequences of GPU calls at once
(CUDA graphs), which attacks the per-call cost measured above. The two
frameworks converged on the same design from opposite ends.

What fusion buys, on the CPU:

```python

def gelu_scale_shift(x, g, b):
    """Four elementwise steps. Eager PyTorch runs each as its own pass over memory."""
    return torch.nn.functional.gelu(x) * g + b + x

def scale_shift(x, g, b):
    """The same without GELU: three steps, almost no arithmetic."""
    return x * g + b + x

x = torch.randn(4096, 4096)
g, b = torch.randn(4096), torch.randn(4096)

def timed(fn, trials=20):
    fn(); times = []
    for _ in range(trials):
        t0 = time.perf_counter(); fn(); times.append(time.perf_counter() - t0)
    return sorted(times)[trials // 2]

for name, fn in [("GELU, scale, shift, add", gelu_scale_shift), ("scale, shift, add", scale_shift)]:
    t0 = time.perf_counter()
    fast = torch.compile(fn)
    fast(x, g, b)
    compile_s = time.perf_counter() - t0
    eager_s, fused_s = timed(lambda: fn(x, g, b)), timed(lambda: fast(x, g, b))
    same = torch.allclose(fast(x, g, b), fn(x, g, b), atol=1e-5)
    print(f"{name:24s} eager {eager_s * 1e3:5.1f} ms   compiled {fused_s * 1e3:4.1f} ms "
          f"({eager_s / fused_s:.1f}x)   first call {compile_s:.1f} s   same result: {same}")
t = timed(lambda: x.clone())
print(f"{'one read + one write':24s} {t * 1e3:5.1f} ms  (x.clone(), the floor for any one-pass kernel)")
```

```text
GELU, scale, shift, add  eager   9.7 ms   compiled  4.0 ms (2.4x)   first call 1.6 s   same result: True
scale, shift, add        eager   6.7 ms   compiled  1.8 ms (3.7x)   first call 0.0 s   same result: True
one read + one write       1.6 ms  (x.clone(), the floor for any one-pass kernel)
```

Across runs the compiled versions are 2.4 to 3.7 times faster, with the
same results. Count the memory traffic to see why, and why the two chains
differ.
- **Eager runs each step as its own pass.** For the GELU chain, GELU reads
  `x` and writes a temporary, the multiply reads that and writes another,
  and so on: 9 full-array reads and writes. The compiled kernel reads the
  inputs once and writes the result once, 2 transfers.
- **Without GELU, fusion reaches the floor.** The compiled three-step chain
  takes 1.8 ms, close to the 1.6 ms of `x.clone()`, which does nothing but
  read the array once and write it once. It's purely memory-bound, and
  fusion removed every pass it could.
- **With GELU, the math becomes the limit.** The compiled GELU chain takes
  about two and a half times that floor. Once the passes are fused, the kernel is no
  longer waiting on memory: GELU's arithmetic, an `erf` per element, is now
  the bottleneck on this CPU.

That's post 4.2's lesson from the other side: for memory-bound work, fewer
passes is the whole game, until the work stops being memory-bound. The price
is the first call, which spent 1.6 seconds compiling. For a training run of
thousands of steps with stable shapes that's nothing; for a function called
once it's a loss.

## Where this leads

- **[Post 6](/posts/how-a-gpu-runs-deep-learning/)** goes inside the GPU: what a kernel is, why reading memory in
  long consecutive runs matters, the roofline that decides whether an
  operation is bound by math or by memory, and a first kernel written by
  hand.
- **Part III** builds a Transformer in PyTorch, using everything here:
  views for reshaping into heads, autograd for training, BF16 autocast, and
  compilation.
- **Part XII** comes back to precision from the other side: quantization,
  storing a trained model in 8 or 4 bits for serving.

> [!note] The Notebook Assistant, step 8
> The assistant's code now runs in PyTorch. The router trains with
> `torch.optim.AdamW`, biases exempt from decay, and matches the
> hand-written version to $10^{-15}$. From Part II on, the models are
> PyTorch modules trained in BF16 mixed precision, with the backward passes
> left to autograd.

## Takeaways

- A framework is a tensor library, an autograd tape and a device model.
- A tensor is memory plus shape, strides and an offset. Transposes and
  slices are views that copy nothing; `contiguous()` copies.
- Autograd records each operation during the forward pass and applies its
  backward rule in reverse order. Gradients from different uses of a tensor
  add up, and broadcast inputs get summed gradients. A 55-line version
  matches PyTorch to about $10^{-16}$.
- PyTorch rebuilds the graph on every forward pass, so Python control flow
  shapes it. `no_grad` skips it; `zero_grad` is needed because gradients
  accumulate.
- GPU calls return before the work is done. Synchronize before reading a
  clock, but not inside the work: queued calls overlap, and every hidden
  synchronize (`.item()`, `.cpu()`) costs a round trip.
- BF16 has FP32's range and little precision, so it needs no loss scaling.
  FP16 needs loss scaling to keep small gradients. Weights and optimizer
  state stay in FP32 because small updates vanish in BF16.
- Mixed-precision training with Adam keeps 16 bytes per parameter, whether
  counted ZeRO's way or measured under `torch.autocast`.
- `torch.compile` captures eager code into a graph and fuses it, which pays
  off for chains of memory-bound operations.

  (NeurIPS, 2019) — eager execution and PyTorch's autograd.
- Abadi et al., [_TensorFlow: A System for Large-Scale Machine Learning_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1605.08695)
  (OSDI, 2016) — the static-graph design.
- Ansel et al., [_PyTorch 2: Faster Machine Learning Through Dynamic Python Bytecode Transformation and Graph Compilation_](https://doi.org/10.1145/3620665.3640366)
  (ASPLOS, 2024) — `torch.compile`.
- Micikevicius et al., [_Mixed Precision Training_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.03740)
  (ICLR, 2018) — FP16 training, FP32 master weights and loss scaling.
- Kalamkar et al., [_A Study of BFLOAT16 for Deep Learning Training_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1905.12322)
  (2019) — BF16 as a drop-in for FP32 training.
- Stanford CS336, [_Language Modeling from Scratch_](https://stanford-cs336.github.io/spring2025/),
  lecture 2 (2025) — resource accounting: tensors, FLOPs
  and memory for training.
Every post on this blog is, underneath its own topic, a story about one
object: a single vector, per token position, that starts as an embedding and
ends as a prediction. This blog is named after it. Almost nothing else about
a Transformer makes sense until that object does, so before any attention
math, any KV cache, any serving engine — this is the floor.

Picture a relay race where every runner, instead of handing off the baton
and stepping aside, *keeps running alongside it*, adding their own stride to
its path without ever taking it away from anyone else. Nothing is ever
overwritten; every layer just adds its contribution to a path that started
at the very first token embedding and keeps accumulating all the way to the
end. That accumulating path is the **residual stream**, and this post is
about what lives on it, why it's shaped that way, and how it turns into a
next-token prediction.

## Table of contents

## Tokens become vectors: embedding

A model never sees text. A tokenizer first splits it into a sequence of
discrete IDs from a fixed vocabulary — word pieces, not necessarily whole
words — and an **embedding matrix** $W_E$ turns each ID into a learned
vector: row $i$ of $W_E$ *is* the vector for token $i$.

Take a toy 4-token vocabulary and a 2-dimensional embedding, small enough to
write out in full:

$$
\text{vocab} = [\texttt{the},\ \texttt{cat},\ \texttt{sat},\ \texttt{.}],
\qquad
W_E = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 \\ -1 & 1 \end{bmatrix}
$$

Embedding the token `cat` just reads off row 1:

$$
x_0 = W_E[\texttt{cat}] = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}
$$

That's it — no computation yet, just a table lookup. (Real models also add
**positional information** at this step, so a token's vector encodes *where*
it sits, not just *what* it is. Exactly how — sinusoidal functions, learned
position embeddings, rotary embeddings — is its own topic; the [attention
patterns post](/posts/transformer-attention-patterns/#limitations-what-attention-doesnt-give-you-for-free)
already flags *why* it's needed. Here, treat $x_0$ as already carrying it.)

$x_0$ is the residual stream's very first value — for this one token
position, at layer 0, before anything else has touched it.

## The residual stream: one running vector, read and written by everything

Here's the idea this whole post is named after, stated precisely. Every
component of a Transformer — the embedding, every attention sublayer, every
MLP sublayer, the final unembedding — reads from and writes to the *same*
vector, at the *same* position, by **addition**:

$$
x_{l+1} = x_l + F_l(x_l)
$$

$F_l$ is whatever that layer computes (attention, or an MLP — both below).
Layers never replace $x_l$ outright; they each read it, compute an update,
and add that update back in. After $L$ layers, $x_L$ is the *sum* of the
original embedding plus every single layer's contribution:

$$
x_L = x_0 + \sum_{l=0}^{L-1} F_l(x_l)
$$

This is exactly the framing Anthropic's interpretability team made precise
in 2021, and it's worth reading in their own words:

> [!note] Where the term comes from
> "All components of a transformer (the token embedding, attention heads,
> MLP layers, and unembedding) communicate with each other by reading and
> writing to different subspaces of the residual stream." — Elhage et al.,
> [_A Mathematical Framework for Transformer Circuits_](https://transformer-circuits.pub/2021/framework/index.html)
> (Anthropic, 2021)

That "different subspaces" detail matters: because every layer's output is
just *added in*, nothing forces two layers' contributions to occupy
different directions of the vector. In practice they mostly do — a stream
that let every layer's writes collide indiscriminately would erase
information the next layer needed — but it's a soft, learned separation, not
a hard architectural guarantee. That's precisely what makes the residual
stream interesting to study: it's a shared, additive workspace, not a neatly
labeled set of slots.

## Each block: attention sublayer + MLP sublayer, both additive

A Transformer stacks $L$ identical **blocks**, and every block does the
exact same two-step read-compute-add:

1. **Attention sublayer.** The stream is normalized (LayerNorm — rescales
   the vector so training stays stable; its formula, and RMSNorm's, are in
   [the normalization post](/posts/dropout-and-normalization/)), the
   normalized copy goes into the attention computation, and
   attention's output is added back onto the *un-normalized* stream:
   $x_l' = x_l + \text{Attn}(\text{LN}(x_l))$. What attention actually
   computes there — the $Q$, $K$, $V$ projections, the scaled dot product,
   the softmax — is the entire subject of [the next
   post](/posts/transformer-attention-patterns/); nothing here depends on
   those details, only on the fact that whatever it computes gets *added*.
2. **MLP sublayer.** The (now-updated) stream is normalized again, fed
   through a small feed-forward network — expand to a wider hidden
   dimension, apply a nonlinearity, project back down — and that output is
   added back the same way: $x_{l+1} = x_l' + \text{MLP}(\text{LN}(x_l'))$.
   The MLP's job, roughly, is per-position processing of whatever the
   attention step just mixed in; its internals are a topic of their own.

Both sublayers follow the identical contract: read the stream (through a
normalization step), compute something, add it back. Nothing about that
contract cares *what* the something is — which is exactly why attention and
the MLP can be swapped for other mechanisms in a Transformer variant without
changing anything about the stream itself.

One historical note, because it changes how you should read diagrams of the
original Transformer: the 2017 paper (Vaswani et al.) normalized *after*
each addition — "Post-LN," $x_{l+1} = \text{LN}(x_l + F_l(x_l))$. GPT-2
(Radford et al., 2019) moved the normalization *inside* the branch instead —
"Pre-LN," the version described above — because it gives the stream an
unbroken additive path from the first token embedding to the last block,
with no normalization ever sitting directly in that path. Pre-LN is what
essentially every modern LLM (GPT-3 onward, LLaMA, and this blog's own
inference-serving series) actually uses.

## Why "residual"? Fixing a wall deep networks used to hit

The name isn't decorative. **Residual connections** — the identity shortcut
$y = x + F(x)$ — were introduced by He et al. for image models in
[_Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1512.03385)
(2015), to fix something that looked at first like overfitting but wasn't:
plain deep networks, past a certain depth, got systematically *worse* on
both training and test data as more layers were added. Depth wasn't
learning to be a better function — it was struggling to learn even an
identity function through dozens of stacked nonlinearities.

The residual add fixes this directly, and the reason is a one-line
derivative. For a single block $y = x + F(x)$:

$$
\frac{\partial y}{\partial x} = I + \frac{\partial F}{\partial x}
$$

That leading $I$ is the entire trick. Whatever $\partial F/\partial x$ turns
out to be — even if it's nearly zero, exactly what happens when a
nonlinearity saturates — a full-strength copy of the gradient still passes
straight through the identity term. Stack $L$ such blocks and the network
keeps an unbroken gradient path all the way back to the input, *on top of*
whatever each nonlinear branch separately contributes. A plain deep network
has no such term: its gradient is a product of $L$ Jacobians with nothing
anchoring any of them near 1, so it shrinks or explodes at every layer it
passes through — the classic vanishing/exploding-gradient wall that made
very deep networks nearly untrainable before 2015.

Transformers inherited this trick wholesale: the "Add" in every sublayer's
"Add & Norm" is exactly He et al.'s residual connection, which is also
exactly why a 96-layer model is trainable at all. Depth stopped being the
enemy the moment addition, not replacement, became the update rule.

## From the stream to a prediction: unembedding

After the last block, one more normalization and one more matrix
multiplication turn the stream back into something over the vocabulary.
The **unembedding matrix** $W_U$ projects the final stream vector to one
raw score — a **logit** — per vocabulary entry, and softmax turns those
logits into a probability distribution to sample or argmax from.

($W_U$ is sometimes literally $W_E^\top$ — "weight tying," from
[Press & Wolf, 2017](https://arxiv.org/abs/1608.05859) — reusing the
embedding table backwards instead of learning a second one. It's still
common in smaller models, but most 2024–2026 frontier models use a separate,
untied $W_U$: embedding parameters scale with vocabulary size × dimension,
non-embedding parameters scale with layers × dimension², and at frontier
scale the second term dwarfs the first — so the parameter savings tying
offers stop being worth the constraint.)

Continuing the toy example, take the stream after some number of blocks to
be $x_L = \begin{bmatrix} 0.4 & 1.1 \end{bmatrix}$ and unembed with:

$$
W_U = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 & -1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 & -1 \end{bmatrix}
$$

$$
\text{logits} = x_L W_U = \begin{bmatrix} 0.4 & 1.1 & -0.4 & -1.1 \end{bmatrix}
$$

Softmax turns those four raw scores into a distribution that sums to 1. It
appears in every post on this blog, so it is defined once, here.

### What softmax actually computes

Given a vector of $n$ real numbers $z$ — the logits — softmax exponentiates
each one and divides by the sum of all the exponentials:

$$
\operatorname{softmax}(z)_i = \frac{e^{z_i}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n} e^{z_j}}
$$

Exponentiating makes every entry positive, whatever sign the logit had;
dividing by the total makes them sum to exactly 1. So the output is always a
valid probability distribution, and it preserves order — the largest logit
gets the largest probability. Worked on the toy logits:

$$
e^{z} = \begin{bmatrix} e^{0.4} & e^{1.1} & e^{-0.4} & e^{-1.1} \end{bmatrix}
      = \begin{bmatrix} 1.492 & 3.004 & 0.670 & 0.333 \end{bmatrix},
\qquad \textstyle\sum_j e^{z_j} = 5.499
$$

$$
\operatorname{softmax}(z) = \frac{1}{5.499}\begin{bmatrix} 1.492 & 3.004 & 0.670 & 0.333 \end{bmatrix}
= \begin{bmatrix} 0.271 & 0.546 & 0.122 & 0.061 \end{bmatrix}
$$

Two properties follow directly from the formula and matter everywhere
softmax appears:

- **Only differences between logits matter.** Adding the same constant $c$
  to every $z_i$ multiplies numerator and denominator by $e^{c}$, which
  cancels: $\operatorname{softmax}(z + c) = \operatorname{softmax}(z)$. This
  is why the code below computes `np.exp(logits - logits.max())` —
  subtracting the maximum changes nothing mathematically but keeps $e^{z}$
  from overflowing when logits are large.
- **Scale controls sharpness.** Multiplying every logit by $s > 1$ stretches
  the differences, so the largest entry takes more of the mass; as $s$ grows
  the output approaches one-hot (all mass on the argmax) and the derivative
  $\partial p_i / \partial z_i = p_i(1 - p_i)$ collapses toward zero.
  Multiplying by $s < 1$ flattens it toward uniform. That sensitivity is
  exactly why attention divides its scores by $\sqrt{d_k}$ before the
  softmax — [the attention post](/posts/transformer-attention-patterns/)
  derives the scale it is correcting.

Drag the logits below to see all of this at once — the exponentials, the
normalizer, the probabilities — and use the scale and shift controls to
check the two properties:

`cat` is the most likely next token here — not because of anything
meaningful (these are arbitrary toy numbers), but because that's
mechanically all a prediction is: whatever accumulated on the stream,
projected onto the vocabulary and normalized into probabilities. Every
Transformer, at every step of generation, ends exactly here.

## Reproducing this in code

The embedding and unembedding steps above are real matrices; the "layers in
between" step is deliberately a stand-in, not real attention or a real MLP —
those get the full, verified treatment in later posts. What matters here is
only the shape: read the stream, compute something, add it back, repeat:

```python

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)

vocab = ["the", "cat", "sat", "."]
d_model = 4
W_E = rng.normal(size=(len(vocab), d_model)).round(2)   # embedding table
W_U = rng.normal(size=(d_model, len(vocab))).round(2)    # unembedding (untied)

def toy_layer(x, seed):
    """Stand-in for one block's attention+MLP sublayers combined. The real
    versions are far more structured than a random projection, but every
    one of them has this exact shape: read the stream, compute an update,
    add it back."""
    r = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    W = r.normal(scale=0.3, size=(d_model, d_model))
    return x + np.tanh(x @ W)                            # <-- the residual add

token_ids = [0, 1, 2]                                    # "the cat sat"
x = W_E[token_ids[-1]]                                   # stream at the last position only
print("after embedding:  ", x.round(3))

for layer in range(3):                                   # 3 toy blocks
    x = toy_layer(x, seed=layer)
    print(f"after block {layer + 1}:   ", x.round(3))

logits = x @ W_U
probs = np.exp(logits - logits.max())
probs /= probs.sum()
print("logits: ", logits.round(3))
print("probs:  ", probs.round(3), "-> predicted next token:", vocab[probs.argmax()])
# after embedding:   [-0.7   -1.27  -0.62   0.04]
# after block 1:    [-0.426 -1.148 -1.106 -0.339]
# after block 2:    [-0.808 -1.475 -0.925 -0.608]
# after block 3:    [-1.505 -1.556 -1.035 -0.269]
# logits:  [ 0.207 -1.803  1.463 -1.101]
# probs:   [0.203 0.027 0.714 0.055] -> predicted next token: sat
```

Notice the stream keeps accumulating — it never resets between blocks — and
that the entire prediction machinery at the end (`x @ W_U`, softmax, argmax)
is identical regardless of what ran inside `toy_layer`. That's the whole
point of building the stream this way: everything upstream of the
unembedding can be replaced, extended, or studied independently, because the
interface between "the layers" and "the prediction" is just one accumulated
vector.

## What this post leaves for later

On purpose, this post stays at the floor and doesn't derive anything above
it:

- **What attention actually computes** inside its sublayer — the $Q$, $K$,
  $V$ projections and the scaled dot product — is [the next
  post](/posts/transformer-attention-patterns/) in full.
- **What the MLP sublayer computes** — the expand-nonlinearity-contract
  shape — isn't derived here at all yet; treat `toy_layer` above as a
  placeholder for a topic this notebook hasn't covered.
- **Tokenization** (how text becomes IDs in the first place) is assumed
  here and derived in full in [the byte-pair encoding
  post](/posts/bpe-tokenizer-explained/) — the floor beneath this one.
  **Positional encoding** (how position gets folded into $x_0$) is still
  assumed, not derived — the attention post's own limitations section is
  where it is flagged as unfinished business.
- **LayerNorm's exact formula** is treated as a black box here; all that
  matters for this post is *where* it sits (Pre-LN, inside each branch, not
  in the stream's main path). [The normalization
  post](/posts/dropout-and-normalization/) derives LayerNorm and RMSNorm,
  and what normalizing does to training.

Each of those is a well-defined, separate topic precisely because the
residual stream decouples them — that decoupling is the architectural
payoff this whole post has been building toward.

## Takeaways

- A Transformer's core object is the **residual stream**: one vector per
  token position, starting at the embedding, updated only by **addition**
  at every subsequent layer.
- Every sublayer follows the same contract — normalize, compute, add back —
  regardless of whether it's attention, an MLP, or something else entirely.
  That uniform contract is what lets each piece be studied, replaced, or
  extended independently.
- Residual (skip) connections exist because a plain deep network's gradient
  is a product of many Jacobians with nothing anchoring it near 1; the
  residual add's leading identity term gives every layer an unbroken
  gradient path regardless of what the nonlinear branch does.
- Modern LLMs normalize *before* each sublayer (Pre-LN, from GPT-2 onward),
  not after (Post-LN, the original 2017 design) — Pre-LN keeps the stream's
  main path free of normalization entirely.
- Prediction is mechanical once the stream is built: unembed, softmax,
  sample — the exact same three steps regardless of how many layers or what
  kind of layers produced the final stream value.

## References

- Vaswani et al., [_Attention Is All You Need_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.03762) (2017) — the original Transformer; Post-LN, the encoder-decoder block structure this post's Pre-LN description is contrasted against.
- He, Zhang, Ren & Sun, [_Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1512.03385) (2015) — the residual/skip connection Transformers inherited, and the degradation problem it fixes.
- Radford et al., [_Language Models are Unsupervised Multitask Learners_](https://cdn.openai.com/better-language-models/language_models_are_unsupervised_multitask_learners.pdf) (GPT-2, 2019) — the practical shift to Pre-LN that most modern LLMs still use.
- Elhage et al., [_A Mathematical Framework for Transformer Circuits_](https://transformer-circuits.pub/2021/framework/index.html) (Anthropic, 2021) — the residual-stream framing this post (and this blog) is named after.
- Press & Wolf, [_Using the Output Embedding to Improve Language Models_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1608.05859) (2017) — weight tying between the embedding and unembedding matrices.
The [vLLM](/posts/vllm-architecture-explained/) post ended on a limitation: vLLM's
prefix reuse is block-level — paged blocks matched by a hash of their content —
and each sequence still keeps its own block table, filled in independently of
every other sequence in the batch. SGLang starts from a different premise:
what if the KV cache weren't owned per-sequence at all, and instead every
request's cache lived in *one* shared structure the whole engine could see at
once? That structure is a radix tree, and reusing it well is what SGLang's
scheduler is built around — enough so that the mechanism has its own name,
RadixAttention.

This is the second post in the series that started with
[vLLM's architecture](/posts/vllm-architecture-explained/). The next post
covers [TensorRT-LLM](/posts/tensorrt-llm-explained/), which spent its
first two years taking the opposite approach — compiling a fixed engine
ahead of time instead of scheduling dynamically — before converging on a
PyTorch-native runtime of its own, and a
[final post](/posts/vllm-vs-sglang-vs-tensorrt-llm/) lines all three up
side by side.

## Table of contents

## The problem: prefixes repeat, but per-sequence caching doesn't notice

A huge fraction of real LLM traffic isn't independent prompts — it's the same
tokens showing up at the start of many different requests:

- A chatbot's system prompt is identical across every user's first message.
- Few-shot examples get pasted into every prompt in a batch evaluation.
- A multi-turn conversation's history is a strict prefix of its next turn —
  each new message just appends to what came before.
- Tree-of-thought and self-consistency sampling deliberately fork *one*
  prompt into many parallel completions that share everything up to the fork
  point.

vLLM's paged blocks already reuse a block when its content hash matches
something already cached, which catches the simplest case (a shared system
prompt sitting at fixed block boundaries). But the reuse stops there: each
sequence manages its own block table independently, so nothing in the engine
represents *how much two requests overlap*, and nothing schedules requests to
make overlap more likely to still be resident when it would help. SGLang's
own account of prior work (the RadixAttention paper's related-work section)
makes the same point about earlier reuse schemes: vLLM and a technique called
ChunkedAttention handle simple cases like whole-system-prompt sharing but not
general, multi-level, tree-structured reuse with LRU-style eviction. A
different approach, PromptCache, reuses non-contiguous cached segments but —
by the RadixAttention paper's own account — trades away some accuracy to do
it, reporting an accuracy drop of up to 43% in one of its own settings.

## RadixAttention: one shared tree instead of N block tables

A **radix tree** is a space-efficient generalization of a trie: an edge can be
labeled with a whole *sequence* of tokens instead of just one. SGLang uses one
radix tree, shared across the whole engine, to map token sequences to their
cached KV tensors — stored in the same kind of paged, non-contiguous layout
vLLM uses, just organized as one tree instead of N independent per-sequence
tables. The path from the tree's root to any node spells out a token
sequence; if two requests share a prefix, their paths share the same nodes,
and the KV cache for that shared span exists exactly once.

Here's an illustrative example: three requests share a system prompt, two of
those also share a block of few-shot examples, and each request's own
question is a unique leaf:

When a new request arrives, SGLang walks its prompt tokens down the tree to
find the longest prefix that already exists as a path — that portion's KV
cache is already resident, so only the tokens past that point need a fresh
prefill. The tree also decides what gets evicted under memory pressure: it
uses **LRU eviction restricted to leaves**, and every node carries a
**reference count** that's nonzero while any currently-running request's path
passes through it. A node can only be evicted once its reference count hits
zero — so a shared ancestor like the system-prompt node above survives for as
long as *any* branch below it is still in use, and only becomes evictable
once every request depending on it has finished and it has become a leaf
itself. There's no fixed-size reservation carved out for the cache, either:
cached tokens and the currently-running batch dynamically share one memory
pool, the same pool PagedAttention's block manager would otherwise be
managing alone.

Here's a toy timeline that makes "evictable" and "evicted" concrete, since
they're not the same thing. Request 1 and Request 2 both share the system
prompt and few-shot block; Request 1 finishes first, so its own leaf becomes
evictable, then actually gets reclaimed a couple of steps later. Once
Request 2 also finishes, the shared ancestors become evictable too — but
they're still physically resident, so when Request 3 arrives and happens to
match the same prefix, it gets a cache hit on nodes that were sitting in
"evictable" limbo rather than a fresh prefill:

Notice the system prompt and few-shot node briefly go back to state 1
(protected) at step 7 — that's Request 3 reusing them, not a new arrival
creating them fresh. Request 1's own leaf, by contrast, was evicted at step
5 and stays gone: nothing else needed exactly its continuation, so there was
nothing to reuse.

## Cache-aware scheduling: ordering the queue on purpose

A shared tree only helps if the scheduler actually keeps reusable prefixes
warm. SGLang defines a request's **cache hit rate** as

$$
\text{cache hit rate} = \frac{\text{matched prefix tokens}}{\text{total prompt tokens}}
$$

and instead of admitting waiting requests in plain arrival order, it sorts
the queue to run the request that currently shares the **longest matched
prefix** first. Processing requests in arrival order can thrash the tree —
the working set the GPU is actively touching keeps changing, so branches get
evicted right before they would have been reused. The RadixAttention paper
proves (Theorem 3.1) that longest-prefix-first scheduling is equivalent to a
