depth-first traversal of the radix tree, and that this greedy rule is
provably cache-hit-rate-optimal in the offline case, where the cache is large
enough to hold the workload's whole working set. The authors are upfront that
this doesn't fully hold online: greedily favoring long-prefix matches can
starve requests whose prompts don't overlap with anything already cached,
and the paper leaves a fully fairness-aware version of the policy as future
work.

Two more things make this practical rather than just theoretically nice.
First, the overhead of maintaining the tree is small even when there's
nothing to gain from it: on a ShareGPT workload with zero cache reuse, the
paper measured **under 0.3% overhead** (0.2s of tree bookkeeping against
74.3s of total inference time for 100 requests) — small enough that there's
no real reason to turn it off, even for workloads that don't overlap. Second,
RadixAttention composes with the machinery from the vLLM post rather than
replacing it: it's compatible with continuous batching and paged attention,
and under tensor parallelism each GPU keeps its own sharded copy of the same
tree, needing no extra synchronization since every shard runs the identical
sequence of tree operations.

## What the paper measured

SGLang's own benchmarks (Zheng et al., NeurIPS 2024) report higher throughput
and lower latency across a spread of workloads chosen specifically to
exercise prefix reuse — agent control, logical reasoning, few-shot learning,
JSON-constrained decoding, RAG pipelines, and multi-turn chat — against
Guidance v0.1.8 (llama.cpp backend), vLLM v0.2.5, and LMQL v0.7.3, on
Llama-7B/70B and Mixtral-8x7B, run on AWS EC2 G5 (A10G) and some A100 80GB
instances:

Two things are worth flagging about that number before treating it as
gospel: it's a *range* across roughly ten different workloads rather than
one clean paired comparison — the chart above shows the high end of that
range, not a single controlled measurement — and the vLLM baseline is an
old version, since the paper notes that RadixAttention-style reuse had
already been partially integrated into vLLM by publication time, so the
authors picked an earlier vLLM release specifically to keep the comparison
clean. Like every number in this series, treat it as that vendor's own
dated, self-reported figure, not a current-day, apples-to-apples verdict. On
multimodal models (LLaVA), the paper separately reports up to **6× higher
throughput** against its own HuggingFace Transformers baseline.

The more concrete number, because it comes from one real production
deployment rather than a synthetic benchmark suite: after SGLang had served
LMSYS's Chatbot Arena in production for a month, RadixAttention's live cache
hit rate measured **52.4% for LLaVA-NeXT-34B** and **74.1% for Vicuna-33B**,
cutting Vicuna-33B's average first-token latency by **1.7×**:

## Structured generation: constrained decoding as a first-class feature

Constrained decoding — forcing output to match a JSON schema or a regex —
normally compiles the constraint into a token-level finite state machine
(FSM) and decodes one token at a time even across spans where the *next several
tokens are already fully determined* by the grammar. If a JSON template forces
the literal text `{"name": "` next, a naive FSM still burns one forward pass
per token to emit it, even though the model has no actual choice to make.

SGLang's original fix, described in the paper as a **compressed FSM**
(sometimes called "jump-forward decoding" outside the paper itself, though
that's not the authors' own term), analyzes the FSM ahead of time and merges
chains of forced, single-transition edges into one compressed edge — so a
whole deterministic span gets emitted in one decode step instead of one step
per token. On a toy example, decoding `{"name": "` (a forced 6-token span)
naively costs 6 forward passes; compressed, it costs 1, with forward passes
still spent one-per-token only on the parts the model actually has to
generate (the field values). SGLang's own ablation on a JSON-decoding
benchmark measured the compressed FSM at **1.6× the throughput** of
token-by-token FSM decoding, and found that skipping reuse of the compiled
FSM across requests in a batch — recompiling it per request instead — cost
a separate **2.4×** drop in throughput on top of that.

That compressed-FSM compiler has since been superseded: as of SGLang v0.4
(December 2024), the default grammar backend is
[xgrammar](https://www.lmsys.org/blog/2024-12-04-sglang-v0-4/), which extends
the same idea to JSON schema, regex, EBNF grammars, and "structural tags," and
is claimed to be 3–10× faster at JSON decoding than the compressed-FSM
approach it replaced — the same core insight (skip forward passes across
forced spans), generalized and handed off to a purpose-built grammar engine
as SGLang matured past its original research prototype.

## The programming model: functions, not just prompts

SGLang is also a front-end language, not only a runtime. A program is a
Python function decorated with `@sgl.function`, built from primitives:
`gen` (generate, optionally constrained by a `regex`), `select` (pick the
highest-probability option from a fixed list of choices), `fork` (split the
current prompt state into parallel, non-blocking branches), `join` (merge
forked branches back together), and `extend` (append more prompt content to
a state). What ties this back to RadixAttention: when a program calls
`fork`, every resulting branch starts as a literal copy of the same prefix —
so the shared prefix those branches have in common is exactly the kind of
overlap the radix tree captures automatically. A self-consistency program
that forks one question into several independent reasoning attempts and
`select`s the majority answer is a direct match for the tree from earlier in
this post — the fork point *is* the shared node, and every branch is just
another leaf hanging off it:

A tree-of-thought program that forks one reasoning prompt into eight
parallel completions works the same way. The application code never manages
a cache key itself — it just calls `fork`, and the shared prefix gets cached
for free as a side effect of how the radix tree already works.

Here's that self-consistency program written out in full:

```python

@sgl.function
def self_consistency(s, question, n=4):
    s += "Question: " + question + "\n"
    forks = s.fork(n)                            # n branches, same cached prefix
    forks += "Reason step by step, then answer.\n"
    forks += sgl.gen("answer", max_tokens=200, stop="\n")
    forks.join()

    # select() is sgl.gen(..., choices=...) under the hood: score each
    # candidate against the model and keep the highest-probability one.
    candidates = [s["answer"][i] for i in range(n)]
    s["final"] = sgl.gen("final", choices=candidates)
    return s

state = self_consistency.run(question="What is 17 * 24?", n=4)
print(state["final"])
```

`s.fork(n)` returns `n` branches that all still share the one cached
prefix up to that point; `forks += ...` broadcasts the same instruction to
every branch at once, and `forks.join()` merges them back so `s["answer"]`
becomes a list indexed by branch. Nothing in this program ever names a
cache key — the four branches sharing the prompt up through `Question:
What is 17 * 24?` is exactly the overlap the radix tree from earlier in
this post captures on its own.

## Recent evolution: pushing scheduling and grammar out of the hot path

Two more optimizations landed in SGLang v0.4 (December 2024), and both echo
problems the vLLM post already covered from a different angle. A
**zero-overhead CPU scheduler** overlaps CPU-side scheduling and grammar
work with GPU compute, so Python-side overhead stops competing with the
model loop for time — conceptually the same problem vLLM's V1 rewrite solved
by moving the scheduler into its own process; SGLang's answer is to overlap
the work rather than relocate it. A **cache-aware load balancer** extends
cache-aware scheduling from a single instance to a fleet: instead of
round-robining requests across replicas, it routes each one to whichever
replica already has the best-matching prefix cached, applying the same idea
that made single-instance scheduling effective to multi-replica deployments.

By the time of that same v0.4 release, SGLang had moved well past a research
prototype: the project's own account describes production use serving
trillions of tokens a day, with xAI using it to serve Grok 3 and Microsoft
Azure using it to serve DeepSeek R1 on AMD MI300X GPUs. SGLang joined the
PyTorch ecosystem in March 2025 — an ecosystem listing, not a change of
governance or foundation ownership — backed by contributors from AMD,
NVIDIA, LinkedIn, Meituan, Stanford, UC Berkeley, UCLA, and xAI, among
others.

## Advantages and disadvantages

**Advantages:**

- RadixAttention makes prefix reuse automatic and cross-request, with no
  app-level cache-key management — and its overhead is low enough
  (well under 1% with zero reuse) that there's little reason not to leave it
  on by default.
- Cache-aware scheduling comes with an actual proof of optimality in the
  offline case (Theorem 3.1), not just an empirically-tuned heuristic —
  unusually rigorous for this corner of systems engineering.
- Structured generation is built into the runtime itself rather than bolted
  on by the application, and it composes with RadixAttention — the same
  tree that caches ordinary prompt prefixes also caches the shared prefixes
  that `fork`-based programs create.
- Documents native support across the broadest hardware set in this series
  so far: NVIDIA, AMD, Intel Xeon, Google TPU, and Huawei Ascend
  accelerators, per its current docs.
- Real production adoption at meaningful scale (Grok 3, DeepSeek R1 on
  Azure) is a stronger existence proof than a benchmark suite alone.

**Disadvantages:**

- RadixAttention only recognizes **exact** prefix matches; the paper itself
  lists fuzzy or semantic matching as unsolved future work, so two prompts
  that are nearly identical but diverge one token early share nothing.
- Cache-aware scheduling's optimality proof is an offline-case result — the
  authors acknowledge the online, greedy version can starve requests with
  unique, non-overlapping prompts, and call a fairness-aware version future
  work rather than a solved problem.
- The benefit is workload-shaped: chat, few-shot, RAG, and tree-of-thought
  workloads with heavy prefix overlap see the biggest wins, while a workload
  of genuinely independent, non-overlapping prompts gets little from
  RadixAttention specifically (continuous batching and chunked prefill still
  apply underneath it, same as they do without a shared tree).
- A reference-counted shared tree, a cache-aware scheduler, and a grammar
  compiler are more moving parts to reason about than vLLM's simpler
  per-sequence block table — the same "more machinery" tradeoff the vLLM
  post already noted for its own scheduler/block-manager/cache-engine trio.
- Still a dynamically-scheduled Python/PyTorch runtime under the hood, not
  an ahead-of-time compiled one — see the next post.

## Where this leads

vLLM's story was memory efficiency plus iteration-level scheduling. SGLang's
is: put the KV cache in one shared structure instead of N independent ones,
and build the scheduler and the front-end language around exploiting that
structure — reuse becomes something the runtime does for you, not something
the application has to manage. The next post looks at
[TensorRT-LLM](/posts/tensorrt-llm-explained/), which spent its first two
years abandoning dynamic Python scheduling altogether in favor of compiling
a fixed, optimized engine ahead of time — before a 2025–2026 architecture
pivot converged it back onto a PyTorch-native runtime of its own. The
[comparison post](/posts/vllm-vs-sglang-vs-tensorrt-llm/) puts all three
side by side.

## References

- Zheng et al., [_SGLang: Efficient Execution of Structured Language Model Programs_](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.07104) (NeurIPS 2024) — RadixAttention, cache-aware scheduling, the compressed FSM, and the frontend DSL.
- [SGLang Joins PyTorch Ecosystem: Efficient LLM Serving Engine](https://pytorch.org/blog/sglang-joins-pytorch/) (PyTorch Blog, March 2025) — production adoption (Grok 3, DeepSeek R1 on Azure), governance, and hardware backing.
- [SGLang v0.4: Zero-Overhead Batch Scheduler, Cache-Aware Load Balancer, Faster Structured Outputs, and Multi-Node Tensor Parallelism](https://www.lmsys.org/blog/2024-12-04-sglang-v0-4/) (LMSYS Blog, Dec 2024) — the xgrammar switch, zero-overhead scheduler, and cache-aware load balancer.
- [SGLang Documentation](https://docs.sglang.ai/) — current hardware support and the `gen`/`select`/`fork`/`join` frontend primitives.
The router so far answers one question: is this about serving, yes or no?
The Notebook Assistant actually needs to send each question to one of four
parts of the blog: serving, architecture, agents, or foundations. That
means one probability per route, all four summing to one, and a loss that
scores the whole distribution.

The answers are the **softmax** and **cross-entropy**. They're worth a post
of their own because they are the output layer and the loss of every
language model. A language model is this post's four-way classifier with
128,256 classes instead of four, applied once per token. Everything here
carries over unchanged:
- where the loss comes from (information theory, not convenience);
- why its gradient is again "prediction minus target", the same
  cancellation post 1 found for the sigmoid;
- how to compute it without overflowing;
- how label smoothing changes it;
- what it costs at language-model scale.

The four-way router is the running test throughout. Its mistakes, read from
a 4×4 confusion matrix, show what a bag of words can and can't tell apart.

## Table of contents

## From one sigmoid to four routes

Post 1's router produced one logit $z$ and turned it into a probability
with the sigmoid. For $K$ classes, give the model one logit per class, $z
\in \mathbb{R}^K$, and turn them into a distribution with the softmax. (In
this post $K$ is the number of classes and $H(\cdot)$ is entropy, both
local; they aren't S0's key/value heads and head dimension.)

$$
p_k = \operatorname{softmax}(z)_k = \frac{e^{z_k}}{\sum_{j=1}^{K} e^{z_j}}
$$

The [residual-stream post](/posts/residual-stream-explained/#what-softmax-actually-computes)
works through the softmax with numbers. Two of its properties matter here.
- **Only differences between logits matter.** Adding the same constant to
  every logit changes nothing.
- **The scale sets the sharpness.** Dividing every logit by a temperature
  $\tau$ flattens the distribution for $\tau > 1$ and sharpens it for $\tau
  < 1$. Post 2.2 used exactly this to
  [calibrate the router](/posts/measuring-a-classifier/#can-we-trust-a-probability-of-07).

The sigmoid isn't a separate idea. It's the softmax with two classes. Fix
the second logit at 0, since only the difference matters:

$$
\operatorname{softmax}([z, 0])_1 = \frac{e^{z}}{e^{z} + e^{0}} = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-z}} = \sigma(z)
$$

So the binary router was a two-class softmax all along, with the "not
serving" logit pinned at zero.

Post 3.1 found that a hidden layer only ties the linear router at this data
size, so the four-way router stays linear, on post 2.1's bag of words. $W$
grows from one column to four, one per route,
and the model computes $z = x^\top W + b$ with $b \in \mathbb{R}^4$. Each
route gets its own weights for every word, and the softmax makes the routes
compete for probability.

Why not keep four separate sigmoids, one "is it this route?" classifier
each (**one-vs-rest**)? This comparison uses the four-way training loop
defined in [Four routes](#four-routes) below:

```python

from router_data import split
from softmax_basics import softmax
from router4 import four_way, train

def one_vs_rest(X, y, lam=0.003, steps=4000, eta=0.5):
    """Four independent sigmoid classifiers, one per route."""
    W, b = np.zeros((X.shape[1], 4)), np.zeros(4)
    Y = np.eye(4)[y]
    for _ in range(steps):
        P = 1 / (1 + np.exp(-(X @ W + b)))
        G = (P - Y) / len(y)                            # the same p - y, per column
        W -= eta * (X.T @ G + lam * W)
        b -= eta * G.sum(axis=0)
    return W, b

ce = lambda P, y: -np.log(P[np.arange(len(y)), y]).mean()
rows = []
for seed in range(20):
    ((Xtr, ytr), (Xva, yva), _), _ = four_way(*split(seed))
    Ws, bs = train(Xtr, ytr, lam=0.003)
    Wo, bo = one_vs_rest(Xtr, ytr)
    P = softmax(Xva @ Ws + bs)
    S = 1 / (1 + np.exp(-(Xva @ Wo + bo)))
    rows.append(((P.argmax(1) == yva).mean(), (S.argmax(1) == yva).mean(),
                 np.abs(S.sum(1) - 1).mean(), (S.max(1) < 0.5).mean(), (P.max(1) < 0.5).mean(),
                 ce(P, yva), ce(S / S.sum(1, keepdims=True), yva)))
r = np.array(rows)
se = lambda d: f"{d.mean():+.3f} ± {d.std(ddof=1) * np.sqrt(1 / 20 + 40 / 120):.3f}"
print(f"val accuracy: softmax {r[:, 0].mean():.3f}, one-vs-rest {r[:, 1].mean():.3f} "
      f"(difference {se(r[:, 1] - r[:, 0])})")
print(f"one-vs-rest scores: sum is off from 1 by {r[:, 2].mean():.2f} on average; no route above 0.5 "
      f"on {r[:, 3].mean():.0%} of questions (softmax: {r[:, 4].mean():.0%})")
print(f"val CE: softmax {r[:, 5].mean():.3f}, one-vs-rest rescaled to sum to 1 {r[:, 6].mean():.3f} "
      f"(difference {se(r[:, 6] - r[:, 5])})")
```

```text
val accuracy: softmax 0.621, one-vs-rest 0.624 (difference +0.003 ± 0.016)
one-vs-rest scores: sum is off from 1 by 0.19 on average; no route above 0.5 on 53% of questions (softmax: 33%)
val CE: softmax 0.891, one-vs-rest rescaled to sum to 1 0.885 (difference -0.006 ± 0.011)
```

The two tie on accuracy. The difference is what the scores mean. Four
independent sigmoids can all be low or all be high. Here their sum is off
from 1 by 0.19 on average, and on 53% of questions no route clears 0.5,
against 33% for the softmax. You *can* rescale them to sum to 1, and here
that scores the same as the softmax: validation cross-entropy 0.885 against
0.891, a tie. But then the distribution you use is a fix applied after
training, not the thing training optimized. When exactly one answer is
right, as with a route or the next token, the softmax trains the
distribution you'll actually read. (Per-class sigmoids aren't wrong in
general. They're the right model when several labels can be true at once,
and some large-scale contrastive models use them on purpose.)

## How surprised should the model be?

The binary router was trained on log-loss, $-\log p$ of the true label,
motivated in post 1 as the Bernoulli likelihood. For $K$ classes the same
reasoning gives $-\log p_y$, the negative log-probability of the true class.
It has a second derivation, from information theory, that explains what the
number means and why it's the right loss for predicting text.

**Surprise.** Call $-\log p$ the **surprise** of an event with probability
$p$. A certain event ($p = 1$) has surprise 0. A rare one has large
surprise. The log is the one continuous choice, up to its base, that makes
surprise add up. Two
independent events have probability $p_1 p_2$, and $-\log(p_1 p_2) =
-\log p_1 - \log p_2$. That's also why a dataset's likelihood, a product
over examples, becomes a loss that is a sum over examples. With natural logs
the unit is **nats**; with $\log_2$ it's bits, and $1$ nat $= 1.443$ bits.

**Entropy** is the expected surprise when outcomes follow a distribution $p$:

$$
H(p) = -\sum_k p_k \log p_k
$$

It's the unavoidable uncertainty in the outcome itself. A fair four-way
choice has $H = \log 4 = 1.386$ nats; a certain one has $H = 0$. Shannon
showed it's also a limit on compression: no code for outcomes drawn from $p$
can average fewer than $H(p)$ nats per outcome, and codes over long blocks
of outcomes get arbitrarily close.

**Cross-entropy** is the expected surprise when outcomes follow $p$ but you
predict with a model $q$:

$$
H(p, q) = -\sum_k p_k \log q_k = H(p) + \operatorname{KL}(p \,\|\, q)
$$

The second form is the key. The **KL divergence** $\operatorname{KL}(p \|
q) = \sum_k p_k \log(p_k / q_k)$ is never negative, and it's zero only when
$q = p$. So cross-entropy is the true uncertainty plus a penalty for being
wrong about it. Minimizing it over $q$ can only lower the KL term, because
$H(p)$ doesn't depend on the model. The best possible model predicts the
true distribution, and then it pays exactly the entropy, no more.

With a single labeled example, $p$ is one-hot on the true class $y$, and the
sum collapses to $-\log q_y$. That's the loss we train on, averaged over
examples. In this section $p$ is the truth and $q$ the model. From the next
section on, as in the rest of the series, $p$ is the model's softmax output
and $y$ is the target.

Four models predicting one question whose true route distribution is $(0.7,
0.1, 0.1, 0.1)$ show what the decomposition measures:

```python

def H(p):                  # entropy, in nats
    p = np.asarray(p, float)
    return -(p[p > 0] * np.log(p[p > 0])).sum()

def CE(p, q):              # cross-entropy of model q on true distribution p
    p, q = np.asarray(p, float), np.asarray(q, float)
    return -(p[p > 0] * np.log(q[p > 0])).sum()

truth = [0.7, 0.1, 0.1, 0.1]     # what the answer really is, for some question
for name, q in {"matches the truth": truth,
                "uniform guess":     [0.25] * 4,
                "overconfident":     [0.97, 0.01, 0.01, 0.01],
                "confidently wrong": [0.01, 0.97, 0.01, 0.01]}.items():
    ce = CE(truth, q)
    print(f"{name:18s} CE {ce:.3f} = H {H(truth):.3f} + KL {ce - H(truth):.3f}   "
          f"perplexity {np.exp(ce):.2f}")

print(f"\nuniform over 4 routes: ln 4 = {np.log(4):.3f} nats; "
      f"over a 128,256-token vocabulary: {np.log(128256):.2f} nats")
print(f"val CE 0.874 -> perplexity {np.exp(0.874):.2f}")

# Label smoothing: the best a model can do is to predict the smoothed target,
# which caps the gap between the right logit and the others.
for eps in (0.1, 0.01):
    top, rest = 1 - eps + eps / 4, eps / 4
    print(f"smoothing {eps}: target ({top:.4f}, {rest:.4f} x3), logit gap ln({top:.4f}/{rest:.4f}) "
          f"= {np.log(top / rest):.2f}, loss floor {H([top, rest, rest, rest]):.3f}")
```

```text
matches the truth  CE 0.940 = H 0.940 + KL 0.000   perplexity 2.56
uniform guess      CE 1.386 = H 0.940 + KL 0.446   perplexity 4.00
overconfident      CE 1.403 = H 0.940 + KL 0.462   perplexity 4.07
confidently wrong  CE 4.148 = H 0.940 + KL 3.207   perplexity 63.29

uniform over 4 routes: ln 4 = 1.386 nats; over a 128,256-token vocabulary: 11.76 nats
val CE 0.874 -> perplexity 2.40
smoothing 0.1: target (0.9250, 0.0250 x3), logit gap ln(0.9250/0.0250) = 3.61, loss floor 0.349
smoothing 0.01: target (0.9925, 0.0025 x3), logit gap ln(0.9925/0.0025) = 5.98, loss floor 0.052
```

Read the first four lines.
- **The truth itself** pays 0.940 nats, the entropy. Nothing can pay less.
- **The uniform guess** pays 1.386, which is $\log 4$ no matter what the
  truth is. A four-way classifier scoring worse than 1.386 is worse than the
  uniform guess. With balanced routes like ours, that means worse than
  knowing nothing. (With unbalanced classes, the no-information baseline is
  the entropy of the class frequencies, which is lower.)
- **Overconfident but right**, putting 0.97 on the likely answer, pays
  *more* than the uniform guess. It's right 70% of the time and pays little
  then, but the 30% of the time it's wrong it pays $-\log 0.01 = 4.6$.
  Cross-entropy punishes confidence that the evidence doesn't support.
- **Confidently wrong** pays 4.148. The penalty has no ceiling: as the
  probability on the truth goes to 0, the loss goes to infinity.

**Perplexity** is $e^{\text{CE}}$: the loss turned back into "how many
equally likely choices would be this uncertain?". The uniform guess has
perplexity 4.00 over four routes. A language model reporting perplexity 10
is, on average, as unsure as picking among 10 equally likely tokens. The
four-way router below reaches a test cross-entropy of 0.848, perplexity
2.34: about as unsure as a choice between two or three routes.

## The gradient is still prediction minus target

Post 1 found that the sigmoid and log-loss combine so that the gradient with
respect to the logit is $p - y$. The softmax and cross-entropy do the same,
and the derivation takes three lines.

**The softmax's derivative.** Differentiate $p_i = e^{z_i} / \sum_j e^{z_j}$
with respect to logit $z_j$. For $i = j$ the quotient rule gives $p_i(1 -
p_i)$; for $i \neq j$ it gives $-p_i p_j$. Both cases are one formula, with
$\delta_{ij} = 1$ if $i = j$ and 0 otherwise:

$$
\frac{\partial p_i}{\partial z_j} = p_i(\delta_{ij} - p_j)
\qquad\text{or, as a matrix,}\qquad
\frac{\partial p}{\partial z} = \operatorname{diag}(p) - p\,p^\top
$$

Raising one logit increases its own probability and takes mass from every
other class, in proportion to how much each had.

**The chain rule.** Write the loss for a target distribution $y$ (one-hot or
not) as $\mathcal{L} = -\sum_i y_i \log p_i$, so $\partial\mathcal{L} /
\partial p_i = -y_i / p_i$. Then

$$
\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial z_j}
= \sum_i \left(-\frac{y_i}{p_i}\right) p_i(\delta_{ij} - p_j)
= -y_j + p_j \sum_i y_i
= p_j - y_j
$$

because the target sums to 1. The $p_i$ from the loss cancels the $p_i$ in
the softmax's derivative, and all that's left is **prediction minus target**, one
component per class. The derivation never assumed $y$ was one-hot, so it
holds for any target distribution, including the smoothed targets below.

Three things follow.
- **Every wrong class is pushed down by exactly its own probability.** A
  class the model gave 0.3 gets gradient $+0.3$; the true class gets $p_y -
  1$. The loss spends its effort where the model's mass is misplaced.
- **The gradient sums to zero across classes**, since both $p$ and $y$ sum
  to 1. That matches the shift invariance: moving every logit up together
  changes nothing, so the gradient has no component in that direction.
- **It never saturates when the model is wrong.** If $p_y \to 0$, the true
  class's gradient approaches $-1$, its largest value. Post 1 showed MSE on a
  sigmoid stalling exactly there, because its gradient carried an extra
  $p(1-p)$ factor. The log removes that factor.

For a batch of $n$ examples, stack the gradients as rows: $G = (P - Y)/n$
with $P$ the softmax outputs and $Y$ the targets. Then the rules from
[post 3.1](/posts/backpropagation-by-hand/#backprop-for-a-whole-layer) give
the weight gradient $X^\top G$, the bias gradient as the column sums of $G$,
and the gradient passed back to whatever produced $X$ as $G W^\top$. In a
language model, that last one is where backpropagation into the network
begins.

As in post 3.1, check the formula against finite differences before
trusting it:

```python

def softmax(Z):
    """Row-wise softmax, shifted by the row max so exp never overflows."""
    Z = Z - Z.max(axis=-1, keepdims=True)
    E = np.exp(Z)
    return E / E.sum(axis=-1, keepdims=True)

def cross_entropy(Z, y):
    """Mean of -log p[y], computed from logits with log-sum-exp."""
    Z = Z - Z.max(axis=-1, keepdims=True)
    log_p = Z - np.log(np.exp(Z).sum(axis=-1, keepdims=True))
    return -log_p[np.arange(len(y)), y].mean()

if __name__ == "__main__":
    # The gradient of cross-entropy w.r.t. the logits is p - onehot(y).
    rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
    z, target = rng.normal(size=(1, 4)), np.array([2])
    analytic = softmax(z)[0] - np.eye(4)[2]
    numeric = np.array([(cross_entropy(z + e, target) - cross_entropy(z - e, target)) / 2e-6
                        for e in np.eye(4)[:, None, :] * 1e-6])
    print(f"p - y:  {np.round(analytic, 4)}")
    print(f"largest gap to finite differences: {np.abs(numeric - analytic).max():.1e}")

    # Why the shift matters.
    z = np.array([[1000.0, 999.0, 0.0, -5.0]])
    with np.errstate(over="ignore", invalid="ignore"):
        naive = np.exp(z) / np.exp(z).sum()
    print(f"naive softmax: {naive[0]}   shifted: {np.round(softmax(z)[0], 4)}")
    show = lambda v: "[" + " ".join(f"{x:.2f}" for x in v[0]) + "]"
    with np.errstate(divide="ignore"):
        print(f"log(softmax(z)): {show(np.log(softmax(z)))}   "
              f"log-sum-exp: {show(z - z.max() - np.log(np.exp(z - z.max()).sum()))}")
```

```text
p - y:  [ 0.226   0.1746 -0.6219  0.2213]
largest gap to finite differences: 8.5e-11
naive softmax: [nan nan  0.  0.]   shifted: [0.7311 0.2689 0.     0.    ]
log(softmax(z)): [-0.31 -1.31 -inf -inf]   log-sum-exp: [-0.31 -1.31 -1000.31 -1005.31]
```

(Save it as `softmax_basics.py`; the later code imports it.) The analytic
gradient matches the numerical one to $10^{-10}$, and its four entries sum
to zero. The true class, index 2, is the only negative entry.

## Computing it without overflow

The last two lines of that output are the reason for the two `max` shifts in
`softmax_basics.py`. With logits $[1000, 999, 0, -5]$, `np.exp(1000)`
overflows to infinity, and $\infty / \infty$ is `nan`. Subtracting the
largest logit first gives $[0, -1, -1000, -1005]$: the same softmax, and
nothing larger than $e^0 = 1$.

The loss needs one more step. Computing `softmax` and then `log` can still
fail in the other direction. The shifted softmax gives the last two routes
probabilities around $e^{-1000}$, which underflow to exactly 0, and $\log 0
= -\infty$: the last line's `log(softmax(z))`. If one of those were the true
class, the loss would be infinite and its gradient `nan`. So compute the log-probabilities directly from the
logits with the **log-sum-exp** identity, where $m = \max_k z_k$:

$$
\log p_y = z_y - \log\sum_k e^{z_k} = z_y - m - \log\sum_k e^{z_k - m}
$$

After the shift, the largest term in the sum is $e^0 = 1$, so the sum is
between 1 and $K$. Its log can neither overflow nor be $\log 0$, and the output's last line
shows the result: $-1000.31$ and $-1005.31$, large but finite. That's what
`cross_entropy` in `softmax_basics.py` does, and it's why deep-learning
libraries take **logits**, not probabilities, as input to their
cross-entropy. PyTorch's `F.cross_entropy(logits, target)` applies the
softmax, the log-sum-exp and the loss in one numerically safe step. Its
`target` can be class indices or, since version 1.10, full class
probabilities, which fits a gradient that works for any target
distribution, and it has a `label_smoothing` argument for the next section.
Passing it probabilities from your own softmax is a common bug: it applies
`log_softmax` to them, a second softmax.

## Four routes

The four-way router uses post 2.1's bag of words, with each question labeled
by its route index. Training is post 1's gradient descent with the new
gradient:

```python

from router_data import featurize, ROUTES
from softmax_basics import softmax

def four_way(train, *others):
    """Post 2.1's bag of words, with the route index (0-3) as the label."""
    sets, vocab = featurize(train, *others)
    return [(X, np.array([ROUTES.index(r["route"]) for r in rs]))
            for (X, _), rs in zip(sets, (train, *others))], vocab

def train(X, y, lam=0.0, smooth=0.0, steps=4000, eta=0.5, K=4):
    W, b = np.zeros((X.shape[1], K)), np.zeros(K)
    Y = np.eye(K)[y] * (1 - smooth) + smooth / K        # label smoothing
    for _ in range(steps):
        G = (softmax(X @ W + b) - Y) / len(y)           # p - y, per example
        W -= eta * (X.T @ G + lam * W)
        b -= eta * G.sum(axis=0)
    return W, b

def top_label_ece(P, y, bins=5):
    conf, right = P.max(axis=1), P.argmax(axis=1) == y
    which = np.clip((conf * bins).astype(int), 0, bins - 1)
    return sum((which == k).mean() * abs(conf[which == k].mean() - right[which == k].mean())
               for k in range(bins) if (which == k).any())
```

(Save it as `router4.py`. `router_data.py` is from post 2.1. `top_label_ece` is post 2.2's
calibration error applied to the model's top prediction: how far "80% sure"
is from being right 80% of the time.)

With four classes, the label smoothing in `train` needs its own
explanation. **Label smoothing** replaces the one-hot target with a mixture:
$(1 - \varepsilon)$ on the true class plus $\varepsilon$ spread evenly over
all $K$. With $\varepsilon = 0.1$ and $K = 4$ the target becomes $(0.925,
0.025, 0.025, 0.025)$. Since the gradient is $p - y$ for *any* target, the
model is now pulled toward 0.925, not 1.0, and the effect shows up in two
places (from the last two lines of `info.py`'s output):
- **A loss floor.** The best achievable loss is the smoothed target's
  entropy, 0.349 nats, not 0.
- **Bounded logits.** Matching the target means the true class's logit sits
  exactly $\log(0.925/0.025) = 3.61$ above the others. Without smoothing,
  the only way to reach the target is an infinite gap, so on data it can
  separate, the model keeps growing its weights. That's the failure post 1
  predicted and post 2.1 found. A smaller $\varepsilon$ allows a bigger gap
  and a lower floor, 5.98 and 0.052 at $\varepsilon = 0.01$, and as
  $\varepsilon \to 0$ the gap is unbounded again.

Smoothing is a regularizer on the output rather than on the weights. The
smoothed loss splits into two cross-entropies, $(1 - \varepsilon)$ times the
usual one plus $\varepsilon$ times the cross-entropy against the uniform
distribution, and that second term is a penalty on confident outputs.

The experiment compares four models on post 2.1's 20 random splits, 30
training, 10 validation and 10 test questions per route. It uses post 2.1's
method throughout: differences are paired split by split, with the
Nadeau–Bengio corrected standard error, and the choice is made on
validation cross-entropy:

```python

from router_data import split, ROUTES
from softmax_basics import cross_entropy, softmax
from router4 import four_way, train, top_label_ece

CONFIGS = {"L2 0.003": (0.003, 0.0), "no penalty": (0.0, 0.0),
           "smoothing 0.1": (0.0, 0.1), "smoothing 0.1 + L2 0.003": (0.003, 0.1)}
val, test = {c: [] for c in CONFIGS}, {c: [] for c in CONFIGS}
for seed in range(20):
    ((Xtr, ytr), (Xva, yva), (Xte, yte)), _ = four_way(*split(seed))
    for name, (lam, smooth) in CONFIGS.items():
        W, b = train(Xtr, ytr, lam, smooth)
        P = softmax(Xva @ W + b)
        val[name].append((cross_entropy(Xva @ W + b, yva), (P.argmax(1) == yva).mean(),
                          top_label_ece(P, yva)))
        test[name].append((softmax(Xte @ W + b).argmax(1), yte, cross_entropy(Xte @ W + b, yte)))

base = np.array(val["L2 0.003"])
corrected = np.sqrt(1 / 20 + 40 / 120)                  # Nadeau-Bengio, post 2.1
vs = lambda d: f"{d.mean():+.3f} ± {d.std(ddof=1) * corrected:.3f}" if d.any() else ""
print(f"{'':25s} {'val CE':>7s} {'vs L2':>15s} {'val acc':>8s} {'vs L2':>15s} {'ECE':>6s} {'vs L2':>15s}")
for name, rows in val.items():
    r = np.array(rows)
    d = r - base                                         # paired, split by split
    print(f"{name:25s} {r[:, 0].mean():7.3f} {vs(d[:, 0]):>15s} {r[:, 1].mean():8.3f} "
          f"{vs(d[:, 1]):>15s} {r[:, 2].mean():6.3f} {vs(d[:, 2]):>15s}")
both, smooth = np.array(val["smoothing 0.1 + L2 0.003"]), np.array(val["smoothing 0.1"])
print(f"smoothing + L2 vs smoothing alone: val CE {vs(both[:, 0] - smooth[:, 0])}")

best = min(val, key=lambda c: np.mean([row[0] for row in val[c]]))   # chosen on validation
conf = np.zeros((4, 4), dtype=int)
for pred, true, _ in test[best]:
    for t, p in zip(true, pred):
        conf[t, p] += 1
test_ce = np.mean([ce for *_, ce in test[best]])
print(f"\nchosen on validation CE: {best}. test CE {test_ce:.3f} (perplexity {np.exp(test_ce):.2f})")
print("test confusion, pooled over the 20 splits (rows = true route):")
print(f"{'':14s}" + "".join(f"{r:>13s}" for r in ROUTES))
for i, r in enumerate(ROUTES):
    print(f"{r:14s}" + "".join(f"{v:13d}" for v in conf[i]))
precision, recall = conf.diagonal() / conf.sum(0), conf.diagonal() / conf.sum(1)
f1 = 2 * precision * recall / (precision + recall)
for r, p, q, f in zip(ROUTES, precision, recall, f1):
    print(f"{r:14s} precision {p:.3f}  recall {q:.3f}  F1 {f:.3f}")
print(f"test accuracy {conf.trace() / conf.sum():.4f}; mean recall {recall.mean():.4f}; "
      f"macro-F1 {f1.mean():.4f}")
```

```text
                           val CE           vs L2  val acc           vs L2    ECE           vs L2
L2 0.003                    0.891                    0.621                  0.096                
no penalty                  0.976  +0.085 ± 0.054    0.633  +0.011 ± 0.021  0.154  +0.059 ± 0.033
smoothing 0.1               0.874  -0.017 ± 0.022    0.655  +0.034 ± 0.029  0.106  +0.010 ± 0.033
smoothing 0.1 + L2 0.003    0.907  +0.016 ± 0.014    0.628  +0.006 ± 0.013  0.125  +0.029 ± 0.031
smoothing + L2 vs smoothing alone: val CE +0.033 ± 0.018

chosen on validation CE: smoothing 0.1. test CE 0.848 (perplexity 2.34)
test confusion, pooled over the 20 splits (rows = true route):
                    serving architecture       agents  foundations
serving                 120           29           22           29
architecture             19          113           12           56
agents                   10           10          153           27
foundations              17           19           20          144
serving        precision 0.723  recall 0.600  F1 0.656
architecture   precision 0.661  recall 0.565  F1 0.609
agents         precision 0.739  recall 0.765  F1 0.752
foundations    precision 0.562  recall 0.720  F1 0.632
test accuracy 0.6625; mean recall 0.6625; macro-F1 0.6621
```

The top half is the comparison.
- **No penalty against L2: the metrics disagree.** L2's validation
  cross-entropy is better by 0.085 ± 0.054, and its calibration error by
  0.059 ± 0.033, both suggestive rather than decisive. On accuracy the
  unpenalized model is 0.011 *ahead*, well inside its error. The two metrics
  measure different things. Accuracy only checks the top route.
  Cross-entropy also charges for how confident each prediction was, and
  without a penalty the probabilities run hotter, including on the questions
  it gets wrong.
- **Smoothing and L2 tie.** Smoothing's validation cross-entropy is lower by
  0.017 ± 0.022 and its accuracy is higher by 0.034 ± 0.029. Both are within
  about one standard error, so neither is a real difference at this data
  size.
- **Both together are too much.** Combined, they're 0.033 ± 0.018 worse than
  smoothing alone and tie L2.

Post 2.1 warned that a fixed step count can quietly act as early stopping,
and that an unpenalized model's weights keep growing. Both are worth
checking, along with what each model does on its own training data:

```python

from router_data import split
from softmax_basics import softmax, cross_entropy
from router4 import four_way, train

# 1. Is 4,000 steps enough, or is it quietly early-stopping some models?
# 2. What does each model look like on its own training data?
CONFIGS = {"L2 0.003": (0.003, 0.0), "no penalty": (0.0, 0.0),
           "smoothing 0.1": (0.0, 0.1), "smoothing 0.1 + L2 0.003": (0.003, 0.1)}
rows = {}
for seed in range(20):
    ((Xtr, ytr), (Xva, yva), _), _ = four_way(*split(seed))
    for name, (lam, smooth) in CONFIGS.items():
        for steps in (4000, 16000):
            W, b = train(Xtr, ytr, lam, smooth, steps=steps)
            P = softmax(Xva @ W + b)
            rows.setdefault((name, steps), []).append((
                cross_entropy(Xva @ W + b, yva), np.linalg.norm(W),
                cross_entropy(Xtr @ W + b, ytr),              # hard labels, no penalty
                P.max(1).mean(), (P.argmax(1) == yva).mean()))
corrected = np.sqrt(1 / 20 + 40 / 120)
base = {s: np.array(rows[("L2 0.003", s)])[:, 0] for s in (4000, 16000)}
print(f"{'':25s} {'steps':>6s} {'val CE':>7s} {'vs L2':>15s} {'|W|':>6s} {'train CE':>9s} "
      f"{'mean top p':>11s} {'val acc':>8s}")
for (name, steps), r in rows.items():
    r = np.array(r)
    d = r[:, 0] - base[steps]
    vs = f"{d.mean():+.3f} ± {d.std(ddof=1) * corrected:.3f}" if d.any() else ""
    print(f"{name:25s} {steps:6,d} {r[:, 0].mean():7.3f} {vs:>15s} {r[:, 1].mean():6.1f} "
          f"{r[:, 2].mean():9.3f} {r[:, 3].mean():11.3f} {r[:, 4].mean():8.3f}")
```

```text
                           steps  val CE           vs L2    |W|  train CE  mean top p  val acc
L2 0.003                   4,000   0.891                   10.6     0.131       0.622    0.621
L2 0.003                  16,000   0.891                   10.6     0.131       0.622    0.621
no penalty                 4,000   0.976  +0.085 ± 0.054   21.7     0.008       0.776    0.633
no penalty                16,000   1.068  +0.177 ± 0.078   27.3     0.002       0.815    0.630
smoothing 0.1              4,000   0.874  -0.017 ± 0.022   14.1     0.080       0.616    0.655
smoothing 0.1             16,000   0.876  -0.015 ± 0.025   14.7     0.078       0.613    0.661
smoothing 0.1 + L2 0.003   4,000   0.907  +0.016 ± 0.014    9.0     0.200       0.563    0.628
smoothing 0.1 + L2 0.003  16,000   0.907  +0.016 ± 0.014    9.0     0.200       0.563    0.628
```

- **The unpenalized model was being early-stopped.** Trained four times
  longer, its weight norm grows from 21.7 to 27.3, its validation
  cross-entropy worsens to 1.068, and L2's lead doubles to 0.177 ± 0.078.
  The other three barely move, so for them 4,000 steps is enough.
- **Smoothing bounds the weights, as predicted.** Without any weight
  penalty, its weight norm levels off (14.1, then 14.7), while the
  unpenalized model's keeps climbing.
- **The combination underfits.** Its training cross-entropy is 0.200,
  against 0.080 for smoothing alone, and its average top probability, 0.563,
  is below its accuracy of 0.628: it's less sure than it should be. Each
  method restrains confidence, and together they restrain it too much.

The rule from post 2.1 is to choose on validation and not relitigate, so
the router uses smoothing 0.1, the lowest validation cross-entropy. It's
chosen on validation, but by a margin smaller than its own error. The test
numbers below describe this model, not a claim that smoothing beats L2.

## Reading a 4×4 confusion matrix

A two-class router has four outcomes; a four-class router has sixteen. Post
2.2's binary definitions carry over one route at a time. Treat route $k$ as
"positive" and the other three as "negative":
- **precision** for $k$ is its diagonal entry divided by its *column* sum,
  of the questions sent to $k$, the fraction that belonged there;
- **recall** for $k$ is the diagonal entry over its *row* sum, of the
  questions that belonged to $k$, the fraction that got there.

The bottom half of the output is the chosen router on the pooled test
splits, 200 questions per route.
- **Accuracy is 0.662**, against 0.25 for guessing, and test cross-entropy
  is 0.848. With the same number of test questions in every route, accuracy
  equals the mean of the four recalls exactly: 0.6625 both. **Macro-F1**,
  the unweighted mean of the four routes' F1 scores, also mixes in
  precision, and lands at 0.6621, close by near-coincidence. With unbalanced
  routes the three can differ a lot, and macro-F1 weights a rare route as
  much as a common one.
- **Agents is the easiest route**, with recall 0.765 and precision 0.739.
  Its vocabulary, like *agent*, *tool*, *prompt* and *context*, rarely
  appears in the other routes' questions.
- **The worst cell is off the diagonal.** Of 200 architecture questions, 56
  go to foundations, more than to serving and agents together (31).
  Foundations has the lowest precision, 0.562, because it absorbs about twice
  as many mistakes as any other route: 112 questions wrongly sent there,
  against 46 to 58 for the others.

The same matrix as fractions of each route's 200 test questions, true route
down the side and predicted route along the top. The diagonal is what the
router gets right. The largest cell off it, 0.28, is architecture sent to
foundations:

Why foundations? A look at one split's learned weights and its mistakes:

```python

from router_data import split, tokens, ROUTES
from softmax_basics import softmax
from router4 import four_way, train

tr, va, te = split(0)
((Xtr, ytr), (Xte, yte)), vocab = four_way(tr, te)
W, b = train(Xtr, ytr, smooth=0.1)
for k, route in enumerate(ROUTES):
    top = np.argsort(-W[:, k])[:6]
    print(f"{route:13s} " + ", ".join(f"{vocab[j]} {W[j, k]:+.2f}" for j in top))
print(f"\nbias b = {np.round(b, 2)}, softmax(b) = {np.round(softmax(b), 2)}")
P = softmax(Xte @ W + b)
for i in np.where((yte == 1) & (P.argmax(1) == 3))[0]:
    words = tokens(te[i]["question"])
    print(f"\narchitecture -> foundations: {te[i]['question']}")
    print("  never seen in training: " + ", ".join(t for t in words if t not in vocab))
    for t in dict.fromkeys(t for t in words if t in vocab):
        print(f"  {t:5s} weights {np.round(W[vocab.index(t)], 2)}")
    print(f"  words alone: p = {np.round(softmax(Xte[i] @ W), 2)}   with the bias: p = {np.round(P[i], 2)}")
```

```text
serving       vllm +1.11, batching +1.11, token +0.98, paged +0.96, to +0.95, quantization +0.92
architecture  attention +1.92, unembedding +1.61, residual +1.59, stream +1.29, transformers +1.21, layer +1.17
agents        agent +1.83, prompt +1.63, context +1.47, calling +1.38, rot +1.33, retrieval +1.22
foundations   backpropagation +1.74, confusion +1.29, learning +1.23, gradients +1.23, sigmoid +1.12, optimizer +1.09

bias b = [ 0.13 -0.17 -0.17  0.21], softmax(b) = [0.28 0.21 0.21 0.3 ]

architecture -> foundations: How does rotary position embedding encode distance?
  never seen in training: rotary, position, embedding, encode, distance
  how   weights [-0.7   0.19  0.27  0.24]
  does  weights [ 0.43 -0.2  -0.01 -0.23]
  words alone: p = [0.19 0.24 0.32 0.25]   with the bias: p = [0.22 0.21 0.27 0.31]

architecture -> foundations: How is the embedding table trained?
  never seen in training: embedding, table, trained
  how   weights [-0.7   0.19  0.27  0.24]
  is    weights [ 0.04 -0.24 -0.15  0.34]
  the   weights [ 0.02  0.57 -0.43 -0.16]
  words alone: p = [0.12 0.38 0.16 0.34]   with the bias: p = [0.13 0.31 0.14 0.41]
```

The weights are what you'd hope: each route's strongest words are its own
topic. The two mistakes show what goes wrong. In both, every content word,
*rotary*, *position*, *embedding*, *table*, *trained*, never appeared in the
120 training questions, so the bag of words drops them. What's left is a
few function words, *how*, *does*, *is*, *the*, and the bias. Their
evidence is weak, and the bias decides. In this split the bias itself leans
toward foundations, 0.30 against 0.21 for architecture. On the words alone,
the second question would have gone to architecture, correctly.

The routes are balanced, 30 training questions each, so the bias isn't a
learned prior about how common each route is. It absorbs whatever the word
weights leave over, and which route it favors changes from split to split.
Across all 20 splits:

```python

from router_data import english, split, tokens, ROUTES
from softmax_basics import softmax
from router4 import four_way, train

unsure, sent_to, all_to, unseen = [], np.zeros(4), np.zeros(4), {True: [], False: []}
to_found, moved, right = 0, 0, {True: [], False: []}
for seed in range(20):
    tr, va, te = split(seed)
    ((Xtr, ytr), (Xte, yte)), vocab = four_way(tr, te)
    W, b = train(Xtr, ytr, smooth=0.1)
    P = softmax(Xte @ W + b)
    low = P.max(1) < 0.4                                 # the router is barely guessing
    unsure.append(low.mean())
    sent_to += np.bincount(P[low].argmax(1), minlength=4)
    all_to += np.bincount(P.argmax(1), minlength=4)
    for i, r in enumerate(te):
        unseen[bool(low[i])].append(np.mean([t not in vocab for t in tokens(r["question"])]))
        right[bool(low[i])].append(P[i].argmax() == yte[i])
    # Would they still go to foundations if no route had a head start from its bias?
    found = low & (P.argmax(1) == 3)
    to_found += found.sum()
    moved += (softmax(Xte[found] @ W + b.mean()).argmax(1) != 3).sum()
print(f"test questions with top probability < 0.4: {np.mean(unsure):.0%}, "
      f"right {np.mean(right[True]):.0%} of the time (the rest: {np.mean(right[False]):.0%})")
print("where they go:   " + ", ".join(f"{r} {n / sent_to.sum():.0%}" for r, n in zip(ROUTES, sent_to)))
print("all predictions: " + ", ".join(f"{r} {n / all_to.sum():.0%}" for r, n in zip(ROUTES, all_to)))
print(f"share of words never seen in training: unsure {np.mean(unseen[True]):.0%}, "
      f"the rest {np.mean(unseen[False]):.0%}")
print(f"of the {to_found} unsure questions sent to foundations, {moved} go elsewhere with an even bias")
for w in ["what", "is"]:
    print(f"questions containing '{w}': " + ", ".join(
        f"{r} {sum(w in tokens(q['question']) for q in english if q['route'] == r)}" for r in ROUTES))
```

```text
test questions with top probability < 0.4: 16%, right 40% of the time (the rest: 71%)
where they go:   serving 18%, architecture 23%, agents 22%, foundations 37%
all predictions: serving 21%, architecture 21%, agents 26%, foundations 32%
share of words never seen in training: unsure 33%, the rest 26%
of the 47 unsure questions sent to foundations, 24 go elsewhere with an even bias
questions containing 'what': serving 11, architecture 19, agents 16, foundations 28
questions containing 'is': serving 13, architecture 17, agents 14, foundations 28
```

- **One test question in six is a near-guess**, with top probability below
  0.4. Those questions have more words the router has never seen, 33% of
  their words against 26% for the rest. They're right 40% of the time,
  against 71% for the rest.
- **Near-guesses lean toward foundations**: 37% go there. The router leans
  that way in general, sending 32% of all questions there, and a little more
  when it's unsure.
- **The bias explains about half of it.** Of the 47 near-guesses sent to
  foundations, 24 go somewhere else when every route gets the same bias.
- **Phrasing explains some of the rest.** Foundations questions are more
  often definitions, "What is gradient descent?", and 28 of 50 foundations
  questions contain *what* and 28 contain *is*, more than any other route.
  So *what* and *is* carry a little foundations weight.

Neither is a flaw in the loss or the optimizer. When a question's topic
words are unknown, the router has nothing to go on but phrasing and a bias,
and it does the best it can with them. A bag of words built from 120
questions can't know that *rotary* and *embedding* are architecture words.
Features learned from far more text can, which is what embeddings do (Part
II).

## At language-model scale

Everything above is exactly how a language model is trained. The classes are
the tokens of the vocabulary. Llama 3's has 128K tokens, $V = 128{,}256$ in
the released model's configuration. At
every position of every training sequence, the model produces $V$ logits
for the next token, and the loss is the cross-entropy against the token that
actually came next. Two numbers from this post transfer directly.

- **The starting loss.** A model whose logits all start near zero predicts
  uniformly and pays $\log V = 11.76$ nats per token, as `info.py` printed.
  With small random logits of standard deviation $\sigma$, it pays about
  $\log V + \sigma^2/2$: slightly more, never much less. That makes a
  sanity check for the first step of any training run. A first loss well
  above $\log V$ means the initial logits are too large. One well *below*
  it means the model can see the answer, usually a label leak or targets
  shifted by one position.
- **The gradient.** Backpropagation into the network starts from $P - Y$ at
  every position: the model's next-token distribution minus a one-hot on the
  real next token.

The new problem is size. The loss needs a logit for every position and every
vocabulary entry:

```python
# Memory for the logits of one training batch, at Llama-3-8B's vocabulary.
V, D = 128_256, 4096
for B, T in [(1, 8192), (8, 8192)]:
    n = B * T                                            # token positions
    logits = n * V * 4                                   # FP32 logits
    print(f"B={B}, T={T}: {n:,} positions x {V:,} = {n * V / 1e9:.2f} B logits; "
          f"FP32 {logits / 2**30:.1f} GiB, and the same again for their gradient")
print(f"the hidden states that produce them, BF16: {8 * 8192 * D * 2 / 2**30:.2f} GiB")
print(f"with activation checkpointing, the saved input of each of 32 layers: "
      f"{32 * 8 * 8192 * D * 2 / 2**30:.0f} GiB")
```

```text
B=1, T=8192: 8,192 positions x 128,256 = 1.05 B logits; FP32 3.9 GiB, and the same again for their gradient
B=8, T=8192: 65,536 positions x 128,256 = 8.41 B logits; FP32 31.3 GiB, and the same again for their gradient
the hidden states that produce them, BF16: 0.50 GiB
with activation checkpointing, the saved input of each of 32 layers: 16 GiB
```

Even one sequence of 8,192 tokens has a billion logits, 3.9 GiB in FP32, the
precision the loss is usually computed in, plus the same again for their
gradient. At a batch of 8 such sequences the logits are 8.4 billion
numbers, 31.3 GiB. The hidden states that produced them take 0.50 GiB. A
full set of activations saved for backward is larger still, but with
activation checkpointing ([post 3.1](/posts/backpropagation-by-hand/#why-backward-and-what-it-costs)),
which keeps only each layer's input, the whole 32-layer model saves 16 GiB.
This one intermediate of the loss is then bigger than everything else the
model stores for backward. For smaller models with large vocabularies, the
Cut Cross-Entropy authors find the loss can take an order of magnitude more
memory than the rest of the model combined.

The $p - y$ form is what makes the fix possible. The gradient at a position
needs only that position's logits and its target, so nothing requires the
whole matrix to exist at once. Fused cross-entropy kernels compute logits a
chunk at a time, take the loss and $p - y$ for that chunk immediately, push
the gradient back through the unembedding, and throw the chunk away.
- **Liger Kernel** fuses the final matrix multiplication with the loss and
  processes the input in chunks.
- **Cut Cross-Entropy** goes further: it computes only the true token's logit
  and the log-sum-exp, on the fly, in on-chip memory. Splitting the
  vocabulary into blocks means the log-sum-exp is built up block by block,
  with a running maximum (an "online" log-sum-exp). For Gemma 2 (2B), its
  authors report the loss computation's memory falling from 24 GB to 1 MB.

The math is the same as the four-route router. Only the order in which it's
computed changes, to fit the hardware. That pattern, the same result
computed in an order that avoids storing a large intermediate, comes back
in FlashAttention (Part V), which uses the same running-maximum trick for
attention's softmax.

## Where this leads

- **Post 4.1** is about making gradient descent work on deeper networks:
  initialization, why the output layer's scale sets that first loss, and the
  optimizers, momentum and Adam, that took over from plain gradient descent.
- **Post 8** uses the softmax and cross-entropy over one positive and many
  negatives, contrastive learning, to train embeddings. That's the loss
  behind retrieval.
- **Post 9** trains the first language model on the blog. Its loss is this
  post's cross-entropy at every position, and its perplexity is this post's
  $e^{\text{CE}}$.
- **The same softmax** normalizes attention scores, in the
  [attention post](/posts/transformer-attention-patterns/), and its
  temperature is the sampling knob of Part IV.
- **KL divergence** comes back in Part VII. It's how distillation compares a
  student to a teacher, and how preference tuning keeps a model close to
  where it started.

> [!note] The Notebook Assistant, step 5
> The router now sends each question to one of four routes: a softmax over
> four logits, trained on cross-entropy with label smoothing 0.1 (chosen on
> validation; it ties L2). It gets 66% of test questions right, against 25%
> by chance, at a test cross-entropy of 0.848. Its most common mistake sends
> architecture questions to foundations when their topic words are ones it
> has never seen. Its probabilities are informative but run low: its top
> route averages 0.62 against 66% accuracy on validation, the usual effect
> of label smoothing, which post 2.2's temperature scaling would correct.
> Questions whose top route is below 0.4 are right only 40% of the time,
> against 71% for the rest, so flagging them as unsure is worth doing (with
> the cutoff chosen on validation). Better features, in Part II, are what
> should move the number.

## Takeaways

- The softmax turns $K$ logits into a distribution. The sigmoid is the
  two-class case with one logit fixed at 0. Four separate sigmoids score
  about as well, but their outputs don't form a distribution unless you
  rescale them after training.
- Surprise is $-\log p$, the only continuous choice (up to the base) that
  makes independent events add.
  Entropy is expected surprise. Cross-entropy is entropy plus the KL
  divergence from the truth, so minimizing it drives the model toward the
  true distribution.
- Cross-entropy punishes unjustified confidence: 0.97 on an answer that's
  right 70% of the time scores worse than a uniform guess. Perplexity,
  $e^{\text{CE}}$, reads the loss as a number of equally likely choices.
- The gradient with respect to the logits is $p - y$ for any target
  distribution. It sums to zero, pushes each wrong class down by its own
  probability, and doesn't saturate when the model is wrong.
- Compute the loss from logits with log-sum-exp, never as `log(softmax(z))`.
  Libraries take logits for this reason.
- Label smoothing gives a loss floor and a bounded logit gap, a regularizer
  on the output. Here it tied L2, kept the weights bounded without a weight
  penalty, and made the probabilities run low. Combining it with L2
  underfit.
- Cross-entropy and accuracy can disagree. Read both, and calibration too.
  Check that a fixed step count isn't early-stopping the baseline.
- With topic words unknown, a bag-of-words router falls back on phrasing
  and its bias. Better features, not a better loss, are the fix.
- A language model is this classifier over the vocabulary. Its first loss
  should be near $\log V$, slightly above, never well below. At scale the
  logits can outweigh everything else a checkpointed model stores for
  backward, which fused kernels avoid by never storing them.
