  the average length of any code.
- Cover and Thomas, [_Elements of Information Theory_](https://doi.org/10.1002/047174882X)
  (Wiley, 2nd ed., 2006) — entropy, cross-entropy and KL divergence, with
  the proof that KL is never negative.
- Bridle, [_Probabilistic Interpretation of Feedforward Classification Network Outputs_](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-76153-9_28)
  (1990) — the softmax as a network's output layer, and its name.
- Szegedy, Vanhoucke, Ioffe, Shlens and Wojna, [_Rethinking the Inception Architecture for Computer Vision_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1512.00567)
  (CVPR, 2016) — label smoothing as a regularizer on the output.
- Pereyra, Tucker, Chorowski, Kaiser and Hinton, [_Regularizing Neural Networks by Penalizing Confident Output Distributions_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1701.06548)
  (2017) — label smoothing as a penalty on confident outputs.
- Müller, Kornblith and Hinton, [_When Does Label Smoothing Help?_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1906.02629)
  (NeurIPS, 2019) — label smoothing improves calibration, and makes a
  teacher worse for distillation.
- Grattafiori et al. (Meta AI), [_The Llama 3 Herd of Models_](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.21783)
  (2024) — the 128K-token vocabulary.
- Hsu et al., [_Liger Kernel: Efficient Triton Kernels for LLM Training_](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.10989)
  (2024) — fused, chunked linear and cross-entropy kernels.
- Wijmans, Huval, Hertzberg, Koltun and Krähenbühl, [_Cut Your Losses in Large-Vocabulary Language Models_](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.09009)
  (ICLR, 2025) — cross-entropy without materializing the logits; 24 GB to 1
  MB for Gemma 2 (2B).
- PyTorch, [`torch.nn.functional.cross_entropy`](https://pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.nn.functional.cross_entropy.html)
  (documentation) — takes logits, with a `label_smoothing` argument.
The [vLLM](/posts/vllm-architecture-explained/) and
[SGLang](/posts/sglang-radixattention-explained/) posts covered two engines
that are both, underneath everything else, dynamically-scheduled Python
runtimes — a scheduler decides what runs next, every step, against a live
execution graph. TensorRT-LLM started from a genuinely different premise:
instead of interpreting a model graph at runtime, compile it once, ahead of
time, into a fixed execution plan, and replay that plan on every request.
That was true for most of the project's life. It isn't fully true anymore,
and the story of why is as informative as the architecture itself — so this
post covers both: the ahead-of-time design that made TensorRT-LLM
distinctive, and the 2025–2026 pivot that moved it onto a PyTorch-native
runtime much closer to its two Python-native siblings in this series.

This is the third post in the series that started with
[vLLM's architecture](/posts/vllm-architecture-explained/) and continued
with [SGLang's RadixAttention](/posts/sglang-radixattention-explained/). A
[final post](/posts/vllm-vs-sglang-vs-tensorrt-llm/) puts all three side by
side.

## Table of contents

## Compiling an engine: the architecture that defined TensorRT-LLM

[TensorRT-LLM](https://github.com/NVIDIA/TensorRT-LLM) is NVIDIA's
open-source (Apache 2.0) LLM serving project, first released in October
2023. It's built on top of [TensorRT](https://developer.nvidia.com/tensorrt),
NVIDIA's general-purpose deep learning inference compiler, and it absorbed
the batching techniques of an earlier NVIDIA project,
[FasterTransformer](https://github.com/NVIDIA/FasterTransformer) — that
repo's own README now points readers to TensorRT-LLM for further
development instead. There's no single academic paper to cite here the way
there is for vLLM or SGLang; the closest things are the GitHub repo itself
and NVIDIA's own documentation.

For its first two years, TensorRT-LLM's defining workflow was a genuine
compile step. You'd describe a model using TensorRT-LLM's Python builder
API, then run `trtllm-build`, which:

- **Fused kernels and layers** — combining sequences of operations (a matmul,
  a bias add, an activation function) that would otherwise be separate GPU
  kernel launches into one fused kernel, cutting launch overhead and the
  memory traffic between them.
- **Calibrated precision ahead of time** — choosing and calibrating a
  quantization format (more on this below) as part of the build, not as a
  runtime decision.
- **Baked in a fixed shape profile** — the compiled engine targeted a
  specific GPU SKU and a chosen range of batch sizes and sequence lengths;
  it wasn't a portable artifact the way a HuggingFace checkpoint is.

The output was a `.engine` file: a serialized, hardware-specific execution
plan. Serving a model meant loading that engine and replaying its fixed plan
on every request, with no Python interpreter or dynamic graph traversal in
the hot path:

The trade was explicit: pay a compilation cost once, ahead of time, in
exchange for a hot path with no interpreter overhead and every kernel
already fused and tuned for the target GPU — the opposite bet from vLLM and
SGLang, which pay Python/PyTorch overhead on every step in exchange for
never needing a separate build step before serving a new model.

## In-flight batching and paged KV cache

TensorRT-LLM's own term for continuous batching is **in-flight batching**:
the same iteration-level idea covered in the [vLLM post](/posts/vllm-architecture-explained/)
— sequences in their context (prefill) phase and sequences in their
generation (decode) phase are admitted and evicted from the batch every
step, rather than waiting for a whole static batch to finish together. If
that sounds familiar, it's the identical mechanism under NVIDIA's own name
for it.

TensorRT-LLM also adopted a paged KV cache — a later addition, converging
toward the same block-based memory layout PagedAttention introduced — with
configurable block sizes (8, 16, 32, 64, or 128 tokens). Its chunked-prefill
equivalent is called **Chunked Context**: a long prompt's context phase is
split into chunks and interleaved with other sequences' generation steps,
with one constraint that has no analogue in vLLM's version — the chunk size
must be an integer multiple of the KV-cache block size, since a chunk
boundary that split a block would leave that block only partially filled by
one sequence's context.

## Quantization: precision calibrated ahead of time

Precision calibration was the other pillar of the ahead-of-time philosophy.
Choosing and calibrating a quantization format is handled by a separate
NVIDIA tool, the
[TensorRT Model Optimizer](https://github.com/NVIDIA/TensorRT-Model-Optimizer)
("ModelOpt"), which has since become framework-agnostic — it now also
quantizes models for vLLM and SGLang, not just TensorRT-LLM, which is a
telling sign of how much of the "compile ahead of time" philosophy has
migrated out of TensorRT-LLM specifically and into shared tooling.

### What quantization actually does

Every format below is a variation on the same base operation: **affine
quantization**, mapping a real value $r$ to a small integer $q$ and back
via a scale $S$ and zero-point $Z$ (Jacob et al., 2018 — the paper that
formalized integer-only inference for neural networks):

$$
q = \operatorname{round}(r / S) + Z, \qquad r \approx S(q - Z)
$$

Weights are usually quantized with $Z = 0$ ("symmetric"), since weight
distributions cluster near zero; the scale is just
$S = \max|r| / (2^{b-1}-1)$ for a $b$-bit signed integer. Take five toy
FP16 weights and quantize them to INT8 ($b=8$, so $2^7 - 1 = 127$):

$$
r = [0.8,\ {-1.5},\ 0.3,\ {-0.05},\ 1.2], \qquad
S = \frac{\max|r|}{127} = \frac{1.5}{127} \approx 0.0118
$$

$$
q = \operatorname{round}(r / S) = [68,\ {-127},\ 25,\ {-4},\ 102]
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\hat{r} = qS = [0.803,\ {-1.500},\ 0.295,\ {-0.047},\ 1.205]
$$

```python

r = np.array([0.8, -1.5, 0.3, -0.05, 1.2])
scale = np.abs(r).max() / 127
q = np.clip(np.round(r / scale), -127, 127).astype(int)
dequant = q * scale

print("scale:  ", round(scale, 6))
print("q:      ", q)
print("dequant:", dequant.round(4))
print("max abs error:", np.abs(r - dequant).max().round(4))
# scale:   0.011811
# q:       [  68 -127   25   -4  102]
# dequant: [ 0.8031 -1.5     0.2953 -0.0472  1.2047]
# max abs error: 0.0047
```

The reconstruction is close but not exact — max error **0.0047** here —
because 8 bits can only represent 255 distinct levels across whatever range
$\max|r|$ spans (here, $\pm 1.5$). That rounding error, small as it looks
per weight, is the entire tradeoff every row below is managing differently.

The other half of the payoff is memory, not compute: a parameter that costs
2 bytes in FP16 costs 1 byte in INT8 and half a byte in INT4 — for a
70B-parameter model, that's roughly **140 GB → 70 GB → 35 GB** from the
storage format alone, before any other change:

| Format | Precision | Best-fit hardware |
| --- | --- | --- |
| FP8 | 8-bit float | Hopper, Blackwell |
| INT8 (SmoothQuant) | 8-bit int, activation-smoothed | Ada and earlier |
| INT4 (AWQ / GPTQ) | 4-bit int, weight-only | Memory-constrained deployments |
| NVFP4 / MXFP4 | 4-bit float | Blackwell |

Each row is the same affine idea above, with a different fix for the same
underlying problem: one scale for an entire tensor is too coarse once
weights or activations have outliers.

- **SmoothQuant** quantizes both weights *and* activations to INT8, which is
  harder than weight-only quantization because activations routinely have
  large per-channel outliers that blow out a single scale. Its fix migrates
  the difficulty instead of solving it directly: an offline, per-channel
  smoothing factor shifts scale from activations onto weights before either
  is quantized, so both end up quantizable at INT8 (Xiao et al., 2023).
- **AWQ** and **GPTQ** are both weight-only — activations stay in higher
  precision. AWQ protects the roughly 1% of weight channels it identifies as
  "salient," by *activation* magnitude rather than weight magnitude, with a
  scaling transform applied before quantization instead of storing them at
  higher precision (Lin et al., 2024). GPTQ instead quantizes weights
  column-by-column and uses second-order (Hessian) information to update the
  remaining, not-yet-quantized columns, compensating for the error each step
  introduces (Frantar et al., 2022).
- **FP8** sidesteps the integer round-and-scale scheme entirely — it's a
  floating-point format (e.g. E4M3: 4 exponent bits, 3 mantissa bits), so it
  carries its own built-in dynamic range. In practice it's still commonly
  paired with a per-tensor scale factor (NVIDIA's "delayed scaling," tracked
  from a running history of recent maximum values), because FP8's native
  range alone isn't quite enough for stable training or inference across
  arbitrary tensor distributions.
- **NVFP4 and MXFP4** push the "one scale is too coarse" fix further: instead
  of one scale per tensor, they use one scale per small *block* of values —
  32-element blocks sharing an 8-bit scale for MXFP4 (the Open Compute
  Project's Microscaling spec), 16-element blocks sharing an 8-bit block
  scale plus one additional 32-bit global scale for NVFP4. Smaller blocks
  track local outliers more closely, which is what makes 4-bit floats
  viable at all.

## Multi-GPU: tensor, pipeline, and expert parallelism

TensorRT-LLM supports the same tensor and pipeline parallelism covered in
the [vLLM post](/posts/vllm-architecture-explained/) — sharding layer
weights across GPUs with an NCCL all-reduce combining partial results, or
assigning whole groups of layers to pipeline stages — plus expert
parallelism for mixture-of-experts models, with a ReduceScatter-plus-AllGather
optimization specifically for large MoE models split across many GPUs. One
property survived the entire architecture change discussed below unchanged:
the parallelism degree is fixed when a deployment is configured, not
adjusted per request the way vLLM's scheduler adjusts batch composition —
true of the original compiled-engine flow (baked into the build) and just
as true of the current PyTorch-native runtime (set at process launch).

## Speculative decoding

This is where TensorRT-LLM is the deepest of the three engines in this
series: it supports draft-model speculative decoding, **Medusa**, **EAGLE**
(EAGLE-1, EAGLE-2, and EAGLE-3, the last with disaggregated serving support
in the PyTorch backend), and **lookahead decoding** (a Jacobi-style method
that needs no extra draft model or training at all). The shared idea behind
all of them: a cheap step proposes several candidate tokens at once, and one
expensive forward pass through the full target model verifies all of them
together, accepting whichever prefix of the proposal the target model
agrees with and discarding the rest:

The payoff is that one forward pass through the (expensive) target model can
produce several accepted tokens instead of one, provided the draft proposals
are good enough to have a decent acceptance rate — worse proposals just fall
back toward one accepted token per pass, at the cost of the wasted draft
step.

The accept/reject rule itself (Leviathan et al. 2023; Chen et al. 2023) is
the same regardless of which method proposed the draft: accept token $i$
with probability $\min(1, p_{\text{target}}(x_i) / p_{\text{draft}}(x_i))$,
and the moment one is rejected, throw away every draft token after it and
resample a single replacement from the target model's own (corrected)
distribution. Walking the diagram above with numbers — a draft that scores
progressively less confident against the target model:

```python

def speculative_step(tokens, p_draft, p_target, resample_fn, rng):
    """Leviathan et al. 2023 / Chen et al. 2023 accept/reject rule. One
    target-model forward pass has already scored every draft token; this
    is just the math that decides what survives."""
    for i, (tok, pd, pt) in enumerate(zip(tokens, p_draft, p_target)):
        if rng.random() >= min(1.0, pt / pd):
            return tokens[:i], resample_fn(i)          # reject: drop the rest, resample one
    return tokens, resample_fn(len(tokens))             # all accepted: target gives one bonus token

draft_tokens = ["The", "cat", "sat", "quickly"]
p_draft      = [0.90,  0.80,  0.70,  0.60]
p_target     = [0.90,  0.80,  0.75,  0.20]   # scored by the ONE target forward pass, all at once

accepted, resampled = speculative_step(
    draft_tokens, p_draft, p_target, resample_fn=lambda i: "slowly", rng=random.Random(3)
)
print(accepted, "+ resampled:", resampled)
# ['The', 'cat', 'sat'] + resampled: slowly
```

Tokens 1–3 all pass ($p_{\text{target}} \geq p_{\text{draft}}$ caps the ratio
at 1, an automatic accept); token 4's ratio is $0.20 / 0.60 = 0.33$, so it's
accepted only a third of the time — here it's rejected, "quickly" and
anything the draft proposed after it are discarded, and the target model
resamples one replacement token in its place. Net result: **4 tokens
produced from exactly one target-model forward pass**, the same cost as
generating a single token the ordinary way. That 4-for-1 ratio is the whole
economic case for speculative decoding, and it lives or dies on the draft
method's acceptance rate — the reason TensorRT-LLM ships four different ways
(Medusa, EAGLE-1/2/3, lookahead) to produce that draft.

## The 2025–2026 pivot: from compiled engines to a PyTorch-native runtime

Here's the part of the story that changes how you should read everything
above: TensorRT-LLM does not work this way by default anymore. Per the
project's own release notes and GitHub README:

| Version | Date | Change |
| --- | --- | --- |
| v0.17.0 | Jan 2025 | PyTorch backend added, experimental |
| v1.0.0 | Sep 2025 | PyTorch promoted to the default backend (breaking change) |
| v1.2.0 | Mar 2026 | TensorRT engine backend removed entirely — PyTorch is the sole execution backend |
| v1.2.1 | Apr 2026 | Current stable release |

As of v1.2.0, `trtllm-build`, `trtllm-refit`, `trtllm-prune`, and the
`TrtLlmArgs` configuration type are all gone from the codebase, and
requesting the old backend (`LLM(backend="tensorrt")`) raises a
`ValueError`. The project's own architecture documentation now describes a
`PyExecutor`-based dynamic runtime as the standard path, and files the old
compiled-engine flow under an explicit "Legacy" section. The GitHub README
states it plainly: TensorRT-LLM is "architected on PyTorch," with that
PyTorch-native design meant to let developers "experiment with the runtime
or extend functionality" — language that reads as a direct response to what
the old flow made hard.

One retrospective account of the change (not an official NVIDIA statement,
so treat it as informed color rather than a citable fact) attributes the
pivot to a "conversion tax": every new technique — a new speculative
decoding method, a new attention variant — had to be built twice, once
against PyTorch to validate it and once again against the separate
TensorRT graph-building API to ship it, and that tax slowed the project down
relative to vLLM and SGLang, which only ever had the one PyTorch-native
version to update.

What actually survives of the "compile ahead of time" philosophy today is
much narrower than a `.engine` file: `torch.compile`-based kernel fusion,
plus **piecewise CUDA graph** capture at process startup — a lightweight,
per-process JIT warmup that fuses and replays common execution shapes, not a
separate portable artifact tied to one GPU SKU and shape profile the way
`trtllm-build`'s output was. The idea of paying a cost once to save
repeated work didn't disappear; it shrank from "compile once, deploy the
artifact anywhere" down to "warm up once, per process."

The practical upshot: as of today, all three engines in this series —
vLLM, SGLang, and TensorRT-LLM — are dynamically-scheduled, PyTorch-native
runtimes. The clean "two dynamic engines vs. one compiled one" framing this
post opened with described TensorRT-LLM's first two years accurately, but
it is no longer how any of the three actually work. The
[comparison post](/posts/vllm-vs-sglang-vs-tensorrt-llm/) picks this up from
here.

## Serving layer: trtllm-serve, Triton, and NIM

Three deployment paths coexist rather than one superseding the others.
**`trtllm-serve`** is a standalone, OpenAI-compatible HTTP/gRPC server that
needs no other serving framework, and supports disaggregated prefill/decode
serving — running the compute-bound prefill phase and the memory-bound
decode phase on separate GPU pools, an idea in the same spirit as the
[vLLM post](/posts/vllm-architecture-explained/)'s point that mixing prefill
and decode work in one batch is exactly what chunked prefill has to manage
around. **Triton Inference Server**, paired with the `tensorrtllm_backend`,
remains a good fit when a deployment needs to route across multiple models
or backends on one server rather than serving TensorRT-LLM alone. **NVIDIA
NIM** — prebuilt inference containers — auto-selects an optimized backend
per target GPU and model, and as of NIM's current lineup that backend
selection can land on TensorRT-LLM, vLLM, *or* SGLang: NVIDIA's own
production packaging no longer treats TensorRT-LLM as the only option
inside it.

## What the benchmarks say

NVIDIA's MLPerf Inference v5.1 submission (published September 2025)
reported, for Blackwell Ultra GB300 NVL72 systems running DeepSeek-R1:
**5,842 tokens/sec/GPU** in the offline scenario and **2,907 tokens/sec/GPU**
in the server scenario — roughly **5× the per-GPU throughput of the prior
Hopper generation** on the same benchmark:

Separately, and more precisely, NVIDIA reported GB300 NVL72 delivering
**45% (offline) and 25% (server) higher per-GPU performance** than its own
immediate predecessor, the GB200 NVL72 — a closer generation-over-generation
comparison than the roughly-5×-vs-Hopper figure above, which spans a bigger
hardware jump. Both numbers are NVIDIA's own full-system MLPerf submission —
GPU and software stack together, with TensorRT-LLM as the serving software —
not an isolated "TensorRT-LLM vs. some other engine" comparison the way
Orca's number was for vLLM or SGLang's own paper benchmarks were. Like every
number in this series, treat it as that vendor's own dated, self-reported
figure for its own hardware generation, not a cross-engine verdict.

## Advantages and disadvantages

**Advantages:**

- The deepest speculative-decoding support of the three engines in this
  series — draft-model decoding, Medusa, EAGLE-1/2/3, and lookahead decoding
  all in one project.
- The broadest, most mature quantization tooling, via a Model Optimizer that
  has outgrown TensorRT-LLM specifically and now also serves vLLM and
  SGLang deployments.
- MLPerf-validated performance on NVIDIA's newest hardware generations,
  backed by full-system submissions rather than library-only claims.
- Multiple production-serving paths — a standalone server, Triton
  integration, and NIM packaging — for different deployment shapes.
- The PyTorch-native pivot removed the "conversion tax" that used to slow
  new techniques down, so recent additions (like EAGLE-3) are landing
  faster than the old compiled-engine era allowed.

**Disadvantages:**

- Still effectively NVIDIA-hardware-only — no AMD or TPU path the way vLLM
  and (more broadly still) SGLang document.
- The architecture changed twice in about a year and a half — experimental
  PyTorch backend, to default, to sole backend — which is real churn for
  anyone who built tooling against the old `trtllm-build` flow and has had
  to migrate off it entirely.
- For its first two years, the compiled-engine flow required a build step
  tied to one GPU SKU and a fixed batch/sequence-length shape profile before
  you could serve anything — a real, if now-historical, operational cost
  that was also the direct motivation for walking away from it.
- Being NVIDIA's own project, its integration with the broader open-source
  LLM ecosystem has at times lagged vLLM's, though that gap has narrowed
  since the PyTorch-native pivot.

## Where this leads

TensorRT-LLM's story doesn't end where it started: a genuinely different,
ahead-of-time-compiled architecture spent two years as the clearest
counterpoint to vLLM and SGLang's dynamic scheduling, then converged onto a
PyTorch-native runtime much like theirs — while keeping the deepest
speculative-decoding support and the most mature quantization tooling of the
three as what's left of its original bet on doing more work ahead of time.
The [comparison post](/posts/vllm-vs-sglang-vs-tensorrt-llm/) lines all
three up on the axes that actually still differ: prefix/cache reuse
strategy, quantization tooling, hardware portability, and deployment shape
— not "compiled vs. dynamic," which no longer separates any of them.

## References

- [NVIDIA/TensorRT-LLM](https://github.com/NVIDIA/TensorRT-LLM) — the project repository; no single academic paper exists for TensorRT-LLM the way one does for vLLM or SGLang.
- [TensorRT-LLM Architecture Overview](https://nvidia.github.io/TensorRT-LLM/architecture/overview.html) — the current `PyExecutor`-based runtime and the legacy compiled-engine flow.
- [TensorRT-LLM Release Notes](https://nvidia.github.io/TensorRT-LLM/release-notes.html) — the v0.17.0 → v1.0.0 → v1.2.0 backend-migration timeline.
- [NVIDIA/TensorRT-Model-Optimizer](https://github.com/NVIDIA/TensorRT-Model-Optimizer) — the quantization/calibration toolchain, now framework-agnostic.
- Jacob et al., [_Quantization and Training of Neural Networks for Efficient Integer-Arithmetic-Only Inference_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1712.05877) (CVPR 2018) — the affine quantization scheme (scale, zero-point) every format in this post's table builds on.
- Xiao et al., [_SmoothQuant: Accurate and Efficient Post-Training Quantization for Large Language Models_](https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.10438) (ICML 2023) — migrating quantization difficulty from activations to weights.
- Frantar et al., [_GPTQ: Accurate Post-Training Quantization for Generative Pre-trained Transformers_](https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.17323) (2022) — Hessian-based column-by-column weight quantization.
- Lin et al., [_AWQ: Activation-aware Weight Quantization for LLM Compression and Acceleration_](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.00978) (MLSys 2024) — protecting salient weight channels identified by activation magnitude.
- [TensorRT-LLM: torch.compile and Piecewise CUDA Graphs](https://nvidia.github.io/TensorRT-LLM/features/torch_compile_and_piecewise_cuda_graph.html) — what remains of ahead-of-time compilation under the PyTorch backend.
- [NVIDIA Blackwell Ultra Sets New Inference Records in MLPerf Debut](https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/nvidia-blackwell-ultra-sets-new-inference-records-in-mlperf-debut) (NVIDIA Developer Blog, Sep 2025) — the MLPerf Inference v5.1 GB300 NVL72 numbers.
Every Transformer, from the original encoder-decoder in [_Attention Is All You
Need_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.03762) to a modern decoder-only LLM, is built
from exactly three attention patterns: **self-attention**, **masked
(causal) self-attention**, and **cross-attention**. They share the same
underlying math. This post derives *why* attention takes the shape it does,
then works through each of the three patterns with real matrices, start to
finish.

If it's not already clear what a token embedding is, or what it means for a
layer to "add its output back into the stream," [the residual stream
post](/posts/residual-stream-explained/) is the floor this one is built on —
it covers tokens, embeddings, and the residual stream itself, and stops right
before deriving what attention actually computes. This post picks up exactly
there.

## Table of contents

## Why not just use RNNs?

An RNN builds a representation of token $i$ by folding token $i-1$'s hidden
state together with token $i$'s embedding, one step at a time. That has two
consequences that matter a lot at scale:

- **It's sequential.** Token $n$'s representation can't be computed until
  token $n-1$'s is, so there's no parallelism across the sequence during
  training.
- **Long-range context is indirect.** Information about token $0$ has to
  survive being repeatedly compressed through every intermediate hidden state
  to influence token $n$. In practice it fades.

This exact problem already had a partial fix by the time Transformers came
along. Bahdanau, Cho, and Bengio introduced attention in 2014 as a patch on
top of an RNN encoder-decoder for machine translation — instead of
compressing the whole source sentence into one fixed-size hidden state, let
the decoder look back and weight every encoder position directly. It helped,
but the RNN was still doing the token-by-token folding underneath, so the
sequential bottleneck never went away. Vaswani et al.'s 2017 insight was more
radical: what if the RNN wasn't needed at all, and attention could stand on
its own?

What we'd actually like is for every token's new representation to be a
direct, *learned* combination of every other token's representation —
computed for all positions at once. That's the property self-attention is
designed to have.

## From a similarity score to Q, K, V

Start with the simplest version of "direct combination": say each input token
has a vector representation $D_i$, and we want a new representation $Y_i$
that's a weighted sum of *all* the $D_j$:

$$
Y_i = \sum_{j} \alpha_{ij} D_j
$$

The weights $\alpha_{ij}$ should say how much token $j$ matters to token $i$.
A natural way to measure that is a dot product — $D_i \cdot D_j$ is large
when the two vectors point in a similar direction — followed by a softmax so
the weights for each $i$ sum to 1:

$$
\alpha_i = \operatorname{softmax}(D_i D^\top)
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
Y = \operatorname{softmax}(D D^\top) D
$$

This already has the shape of attention, but it has a limitation: it reuses
the *same* vector $D_i$ for three different jobs at once — deciding what
token $i$ is looking for, deciding what token $j$ offers to be matched
against, and deciding what token $j$ actually contributes to the sum. Those
are three different roles, so give each one its own learned linear
projection instead of reusing $D$ directly:

$$
Q = XW_Q, \qquad K = XW_K, \qquad V = XW_V
$$

- $Q$ (query) — what this token is looking for
- $K$ (key) — what this token offers, to be matched against queries
- $V$ (value) — what this token actually contributes once matched

> [!note] The one idea to hold onto
> Query, key, and value are three different learned *views* of the same token.
> Every attention pattern in this post — self, masked, cross — is the **same**
> $\operatorname{softmax}(QK^\top/\sqrt{d_k})V$ formula; all that ever changes is
> *which sequence* $Q$, $K$, and $V$ are projected from.

Swapping $D$ for $Q$ and $K$ in the similarity score, and $D$ for $V$ in the
weighted sum, gives scaled dot-product attention:

$$
\operatorname{Attention}(Q, K, V) = \operatorname{softmax}\!\left(\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d_k}}\right)V
$$

The one new ingredient is the division by $\sqrt{d_k}$, the key dimension,
and it is there for a reason you can derive in two lines. Suppose the
entries of a query $q$ and a key $k$ are independent with mean 0 and
variance 1. Their dot product is a sum of $d_k$ terms $q_j k_j$, each with
mean 0 and variance 1, so:

$$
\operatorname{Var}(q \cdot k) = \sum_{j=1}^{d_k} \operatorname{Var}(q_j k_j) = d_k,
\qquad \operatorname{std}(q \cdot k) = \sqrt{d_k}
$$

The typical size of a raw score grows with the square root of the key
dimension — about 2 at $d_k = 4$, 8 at $d_k = 64$, 32 at $d_k = 1024$ — for
no reason other than there being more terms to add up. Softmax is sensitive
to exactly that: scaling every input by $s$ sharpens the output toward
one-hot and shrinks its gradient $p_i(1 - p_i)$ toward zero (the [softmax
section of the residual stream
post](/posts/residual-stream-explained/#what-softmax-actually-computes) has
a slider for this). Dividing by $\sqrt{d_k}$ restores unit variance
regardless of $d_k$, so the softmax sees scores of the same typical size
whether a head is 4-dimensional or 1,024-dimensional. A quick check with
20,000 random query–key pairs: the measured standard deviations of
$q \cdot k$ are 2.00, 4.01, 7.96, 16.06 and 31.94 for $d_k$ = 4, 16, 64,
256, 1024 — and after dividing by $\sqrt{d_k}$, all five are 1.00 to two
decimals.

## Self-attention, worked through

Take two tokens with a 2-dimensional embedding each:

$$
X = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}
$$

and three learned projection matrices:

$$
W_Q = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 1 \end{bmatrix}, \quad
W_K = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}, \quad
W_V = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}
$$

Projecting $X$ through each gives:

$$
Q = XW_Q = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 1 \end{bmatrix}, \quad
K = XW_K = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}, \quad
V = XW_V = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}
$$

Note that $Q$ and $K$ must share a dimension for $QK^\top$ to even be
defined — self-attention conventionally sets $d_q = d_k = d_v$, one shared
projection size for all three, though only $d_q = d_k$ is strictly required.

With $d_k = 2$, the similarity scores $QK^\top / \sqrt{d_k}$ are:

$$
\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{2}} =
\begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0.707 \\ 0.707 & 0.707 \end{bmatrix}
$$

Softmax normalizes each row into weights that sum to 1:

$$
A = \operatorname{softmax}\!\left(\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{2}}\right) =
\begin{bmatrix} 0.330 & 0.670 \\ 0.500 & 0.500 \end{bmatrix}
$$

Softmax redistributes each row's scores into non-negative weights that sum to
1 — that's why the heatmap above looks "smoother" than the raw scores one:
softmax always narrows the range compared to arbitrary real-valued scores.
(The formula itself, $e^{z_i} / \sum_j e^{z_j}$, and its two key properties
are derived once on this blog, in [the residual stream
post](/posts/residual-stream-explained/#what-softmax-actually-computes).)
And the output is that weighted combination of $V$'s rows:

$$
Y = AV = \begin{bmatrix} 0.330 & 1.000 \\ 0.500 & 1.000 \end{bmatrix}
$$

Row 0 of $Y$ is 33% token 0's value plus 67% token 1's — token 1 mattered
more to token 0's new representation than token 0 did to itself, purely
because of how $Q$ and $K$ happened to align. That's the whole mechanism:
everything downstream (multi-head attention, masking, cross-attention) is a
variation on this same $\operatorname{softmax}(QK^\top/\sqrt{d_k})V$ computation.

### Multi-head attention

One set of $W_Q, W_K, W_V$ gives the model one "way" of relating tokens. Multi-head
attention runs $h$ independent copies of this — each with its own, smaller
projection matrices — in parallel, then concatenates their outputs and
projects back down with one more learned matrix $W_O$:

$$
\operatorname{head}_i = \operatorname{Attention}(XW_Q^{(i)}, XW_K^{(i)}, XW_V^{(i)})
\qquad
Y = \operatorname{concat}(\operatorname{head}_1, \dots, \operatorname{head}_h)\,W_O
$$

Each head can specialize — one might track syntactic relationships, another
coreference, another local position — and $W_O$ learns how to combine them.

## Masked (causal) self-attention

The encoder can let every token look at every other token — the whole input
is available up front. A decoder generating text one token at a time can't:
if token 2 could attend to token 3, it would be attending to a word that
hasn't been generated yet. So decoder self-attention needs every token to be
blind to anything after it in the sequence.

The trick is to zero out the disallowed positions *before* the softmax, not
after — add a mask matrix $M$ to the raw scores where allowed positions get
$0$ (no change) and disallowed positions get $-\infty$ (so $e^{-\infty} = 0$
after the exponential, and softmax assigns them exactly zero weight):

$$
A = \operatorname{softmax}\!\left(\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d_k}} + M\right), \qquad
M = \begin{bmatrix}
0 & -\infty & -\infty & \cdots \\
0 & 0 & -\infty & \cdots \\
0 & 0 & 0 & \cdots \\
\vdots & & & \ddots
\end{bmatrix}
$$

Extend the running example to three tokens, reusing the same $W_Q, W_K, W_V$:

$$
X = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{bmatrix}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
Q = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 1 \\ 0 & -1 \end{bmatrix}, \quad
K = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \\ -1 & 1 \end{bmatrix}, \quad
V = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}
$$

The unmasked scores $QK^\top/\sqrt{2}$ come out to:

$$
\begin{bmatrix}
0 & 0.707 & -0.707 \\
0.707 & 0.707 & 0 \\
-0.707 & 0 & -0.707
\end{bmatrix}
$$

Every position can see every other position here — nothing above the
diagonal is off-limits yet. Adding the mask sends every entry above the
diagonal to $-\infty$, and softmax turns those into zero weight — each row
can only draw from itself and earlier positions:

$$
A = \begin{bmatrix}
1.000 & 0 & 0 \\
0.500 & 0.500 & 0 \\
0.248 & 0.504 & 0.248
\end{bmatrix}
$$

Reading a single row of that matrix as bars makes the "distribution" part
concrete — token 3's weights are non-negative and sum to exactly 1:

Compare the two heatmaps: the raw scores fill the whole grid, but masking
collapses everything above the diagonal to zero before it ever reaches $V$.
That lower-triangular shape is the signature of causal masking — token 1
only ever attends to itself, token 2 splits its attention between tokens 1
and 2, and only token 3, the last position, is free to attend across the
whole sequence so far.

> [!tip] The signature of causal masking
> A lower-triangular attention matrix *is* causality made visible: query row $t$
> can only draw from key columns $\leq t$. A full, non-triangular matrix in a
> decoder's self-attention means the future is leaking into the present.

Multiplying $A$ by $V$ gives the final masked output:

$$
Y = AV = \begin{bmatrix}
1.000 & 1.000 \\
0.500 & 1.000 \\
0.497 & 0.752
\end{bmatrix}
$$

## Cross-attention

Self-attention (masked or not) always keeps $Q$, $K$, and $V$ coming from the
*same* sequence. Cross-attention is the one place that changes: it's how a
decoder incorporates information from a different sequence — the encoder's
output — while generating its own. The query comes from the decoder; the key
and value come from the encoder:

$$
Q = D_{\text{dec}} W_Q, \qquad K = E_{\text{enc}} W_K, \qquad V = E_{\text{enc}} W_V
$$

where $D_{\text{dec}}$ is the decoder's hidden state and $E_{\text{enc}}$ is
the encoder's output for the source sequence. Everything else — scaled dot
product, softmax, weighted sum — is identical to self-attention; only where
$K$ and $V$ come from has changed.

Take the 3-token encoder output from the masking example as
$E_{\text{enc}}$, and a separate, 2-token decoder state:

$$
E_{\text{enc}} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{bmatrix}, \qquad
D_{\text{dec}} = \begin{bmatrix} 0.5 & 0.5 \\ -1 & 1 \end{bmatrix}
$$

Projecting each through its own weight matrix:

$$
Q = D_{\text{dec}}W_Q = \begin{bmatrix} 1.000 & 0.500 \\ 0 & 1.000 \end{bmatrix}, \qquad
K = E_{\text{enc}}W_K = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \\ -1 & 1 \end{bmatrix}, \qquad
V = E_{\text{enc}}W_V = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}
$$

Note the shapes: $Q$ is $2 \times 2$ (2 decoder tokens) while $K$ and $V$ are
$3 \times 2$ (3 encoder tokens) — cross-attention doesn't require the two
sequences to be the same length, which is exactly why it works for
translation, where source and target sentences rarely have the same number
of tokens. Only $d_q = d_k$ is required here — $d_v$ is free to differ, since
$V$'s width just becomes the output's width. The scores end up rectangular
too, $2 \times 3$:

$$
\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{2}} = \begin{bmatrix} 0.354 & 0.707 & -0.354 \\ 0.707 & 0 & 0.707 \end{bmatrix}
$$

$$
A = \operatorname{softmax}\!\left(\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{2}}\right) = \begin{bmatrix} 0.343 & 0.488 & 0.169 \\ 0.401 & 0.198 & 0.401 \end{bmatrix}
$$

Unlike the causal case, there's no triangular structure here — each decoder
row is free to spread its attention across all three encoder columns, since
nothing needs to be hidden from a query that isn't generating this token
itself.

$$
Y = AV = \begin{bmatrix} 0.512 & 0.831 \\ 0.802 & 0.599 \end{bmatrix}
$$

Each of the decoder's 2 tokens now has a representation that's a learned,
weighted blend of all 3 encoder tokens — the decoder gets to look at the
entire source sequence at every generation step, not just a compressed
summary of it.

## Reproducing this in code

Every number in this post came from one function. Here it is, in full, using
the same $W_Q$, $W_K$, $W_V$ from the three worked examples above — run it
and the printed arrays match every heatmap on this page:

```python

def attention(Q, K, V, mask=None):
    d_k = K.shape[-1]
    scores = Q @ K.T / np.sqrt(d_k)
    if mask is not None:
        scores = scores + mask
    weights = np.exp(scores - scores.max(axis=-1, keepdims=True))
    weights /= weights.sum(axis=-1, keepdims=True)
    return weights, weights @ V

W_Q = np.array([[1, 0], [1, 1]], dtype=float)
W_K = np.array([[0, 1], [1, 0]], dtype=float)
W_V = np.array([[1, 1], [0, 1]], dtype=float)

# self-attention (2 tokens)
X = np.array([[1, 0], [0, 1]], dtype=float)
_, Y = attention(X @ W_Q, X @ W_K, X @ W_V)
print("self-attention Y:\n", Y.round(3))
# [[0.33  1.  ]
#  [0.5   1.  ]]

# masked self-attention (3 tokens): future positions get -inf before softmax
X3 = np.array([[1, 0], [0, 1], [1, -1]], dtype=float)
causal_mask = np.triu(np.full((3, 3), -np.inf), k=1)
np.fill_diagonal(causal_mask, 0)
_, Y3 = attention(X3 @ W_Q, X3 @ W_K, X3 @ W_V, mask=causal_mask)
print("masked self-attention Y:\n", Y3.round(3))
# [[1.     1.   ]
#  [0.5    1.   ]
#  [0.497  0.752]]

# cross-attention: Q from the decoder, K/V from the encoder
D_dec = np.array([[0.5, 0.5], [-1, 1]], dtype=float)
_, Yc = attention(D_dec @ W_Q, X3 @ W_K, X3 @ W_V)
print("cross-attention Y:\n", Yc.round(3))
# [[0.512  0.831]
#  [0.802  0.599]]
```

The only thing that changes between the three calls is which matrices go
into `attention()` — self-attention passes the same sequence three times,
masking adds one array before the softmax, and cross-attention swaps in a
second sequence for $K$ and $V$. That's the entire implementation surface of
every attention pattern in a Transformer.

## Limitations: what attention doesn't give you for free

Three things fall out of the mechanism above that are easy to miss on a
first pass.

**Attention has no sense of order.** Look at the formula again:
$\operatorname{softmax}(QK^\top/\sqrt{d_k})V$ treats the input as a *set* of
rows, not a *sequence* — swap two rows of $X$ and the corresponding rows of
$Y$ swap right along with them, but nothing else about the computation
changes. An RNN gets word order for free, just by processing tokens one at a
time; a Transformer has to be told the order explicitly, via a separate
**positional encoding** added to the input embeddings before they ever reach
$W_Q$, $W_K$, $W_V$. Attention itself is permutation-equivariant — position
has to be smuggled in from outside.

**The cost is quadratic in sequence length.** Computing $QK^\top$ for $n$
tokens produces an $n \times n$ matrix — double the sequence length and the
attention computation (and the memory to store the score matrix) roughly
quadruples. That's invisible at the toy sizes above, and it's exactly the
constraint that motivates [the KV cache](/posts/kv-cache-explained/) and
[paged attention](/posts/paged-attention-explained/) once a real model is
generating thousands of tokens per request. Those posts manage the cost;
[the attention alternatives post](/posts/attention-alternatives/) asks what
happens if you change the mechanism instead.

**Scaled dot-product isn't the only kind of attention — it's the one that
won.** Bahdanau's original 2014 formulation used *additive* attention: a
small feed-forward network scored each query-key pair instead of a dot
product. Vaswani et al. picked the dot-product form instead because it
reduces to matrix multiplication, which GPUs were already extremely fast at
— the two formulations perform comparably in practice, but only one of them
turns attention into hardware Transformers were already built to
accelerate. The $1/\sqrt{d_k}$ scaling exists purely to patch a side effect
of that choice: without it, the standard deviation of a raw score grows as
$\sqrt{d_k}$ (derived above), pushing softmax toward a near-one-hot,
vanishing-gradient regime.

## Putting the three together

A full encoder-decoder Transformer stacks these in a specific order: each
encoder layer applies self-attention (every source token sees every other
source token) followed by a feed-forward network; each decoder layer applies
masked self-attention (so generation stays autoregressive), then
cross-attention into the encoder's output, then its own feed-forward network.
Decoder-only LLMs — GPT-style models — drop the encoder and cross-attention
entirely and rely on masked self-attention alone, which is why "causal" and
"decoder-only" tend to go together in practice.

The projections themselves ($W_Q$, $W_K$, $W_V$, $W_O$) are the only learned
parameters in attention — everything else in this post is fixed math once
those are learned. What they *converge to* during training, and what that
does to inference cost at serving time (KV caching, paged attention), are
their own posts.

## Takeaways

- Every attention pattern in a Transformer — self, masked, cross — is the
  same $\operatorname{softmax}(QK^\top/\sqrt{d_k})V$ computation; only
  *which sequence* $Q$, $K$, and $V$ come from changes.
- Causal masking is additive: add $-\infty$ to disallowed score positions
  before the softmax, and they contribute exactly zero weight afterward.
- Cross-attention is the only pattern where $Q$ comes from a different
  sequence than $K$ and $V$ — that's what lets a decoder read a source
  sequence of a different length than its own.
- Attention has no built-in notion of token order and costs $O(n^2)$ in
  sequence length — positional encodings and the KV cache/paged attention
  exist specifically to patch those two gaps.

## References

- Vaswani et al., [_Attention Is All You Need_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.03762) (2017) — the original Transformer; introduces scaled dot-product attention and removes recurrence entirely.
- Bahdanau, Cho & Bengio, [_Neural Machine Translation by Jointly Learning to Align and Translate_](https://arxiv.org/abs/1409.0473) (2014) — the original (additive) attention mechanism, introduced to fix the fixed-size bottleneck in RNN encoder-decoders.
The [KV cache](/posts/kv-cache-explained/) and [paged attention](/posts/paged-attention-explained/)
posts covered how vLLM manages GPU memory: blocks instead of one giant
contiguous reservation per sequence. That's the memory layer. This post is
about the rest of the machine built on top of it — the scheduler that decides
which requests run *this step*, the batching scheme that keeps the GPU busy
between them, and the distributed-serving layer that spreads a model across
GPUs. Together they're what turns "we don't waste memory" into "we serve
thousands of requests per second."

This is also the first post in a short series comparing how the major
inference engines are put together. The next two look at
[SGLang](/posts/sglang-radixattention-explained/) and
[TensorRT-LLM](/posts/tensorrt-llm-explained/), and a
[final post](/posts/vllm-vs-sglang-vs-tensorrt-llm/) lines all three up
side by side.

## Table of contents

## The problem: static batching leaves the GPU idle

Batching multiple requests together is what makes GPU inference efficient —
one big matrix multiply is far cheaper per token than N small ones. The naive
way to batch is **static batching**: group $N$ requests, run decode steps
together, and don't touch the batch again until every one of them is done.

The problem is that requests don't finish at the same time. Some prompts want
a two-token answer, others want six hundred. Under static batching, a request
that finishes early doesn't free its slot — the batch is fixed until the
*longest* sequence in it finishes, so that slot just sits there, computed on
every step, contributing nothing.

Here's a toy batch of 4 slots with sequences of length 3, 9, 2, and 6 tokens.
The batch can't advance to a new set of requests until the 9-token sequence
finishes, so the shorter ones sit idle (colored 0) for most of the run:

In this toy example, 16 of the 36 slot-steps (44%) are wasted — and none of
that waste shows up until you look at individual sequences, because the batch
as a whole is still "running." Worse, any new request that arrived at step 1
has to wait in a queue until step 9, even though three-quarters of the batch's
slots were free from step 3 onward.

## Continuous batching: one iteration at a time

**Continuous batching** (also called iteration-level scheduling) fixes this by
making the unit of scheduling one forward pass, not one whole batch lifetime.
After *every* step, the scheduler checks which sequences just finished, frees
their slots, and immediately admits new requests from the waiting queue to
fill them — mid-batch, without waiting for anyone else to finish.

Same toy scenario, but now the queue has requests E, F, G, and H ready to go.
The moment slot 1's request A finishes at step 3, request E takes over the
slot for the rest of the run; slot 3 cycles through C, then F, then G:

No idle cells — every slot is generating *some* request's tokens at every
step. (This assumes a steady queue of waiting work, which is the normal case
under real load; if the queue runs dry, slots do sit empty, but that's a
demand problem, not a scheduling one.)

This idea predates vLLM: it was introduced by
[Orca](https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi22/presentation/yu) (Yu et al.,
OSDI 2022) under the name "iteration-level scheduling," paired with
"selective batching" (batching only the operations that tolerate different
sequence lengths in a step, running attention per-sequence). Orca measured a
**36.9× throughput improvement** over NVIDIA FasterTransformer at matched
190ms latency on a GPT-3 175B model:

vLLM adopted iteration-level scheduling and combined it with PagedAttention:
continuous batching decides *which* sequences run this step, PagedAttention
means admitting a new sequence mid-batch never requires a big contiguous
reservation — it just needs one free block.

## The engine loop: scheduler, block manager, workers

Concretely, vLLM's engine is built from a few pieces that repeat this cycle
every step:

- **Scheduler** — owns three queues (waiting, running, swapped) and decides,
  each step, which sequences from `running` continue, which from `waiting`
  get admitted, and whether anything needs to be preempted.
- **Block manager** — the PagedAttention bookkeeping from the previous post:
  tracks free physical blocks and each sequence's block table.
- **Worker(s)** — run the actual forward pass (one iteration of prefill or
  decode) on the GPU(s), using the block tables to gather the right KV blocks.
- **Cache engine** — moves KV blocks between GPU and CPU memory when the
  scheduler needs to swap a sequence out.

An `AsyncLLMEngine` wraps this loop for online serving, and an
OpenAI-compatible API server sits in front of that, handling HTTP requests,
tokenizing prompts, and streaming detokenized output tokens back over
server-sent events as they're generated:

Every arrow back into the waiting queue is what makes this "continuous" —
there's no separate "start a new batch" event; admission happens every step.
Stripped down to just the control flow the diagram shows, one step looks
like this — not vLLM's actual source (the real scheduler also tracks swap
queues and chunked-prefill budgets), but the shape of the decision it makes
every iteration:

```python
def scheduler_step(running, waiting, block_manager):
    for seq in list(running):
        if not block_manager.has_free_block():
            victim = pick_preemption_victim(running)  # default: most recently added
            block_manager.free(victim)
            running.remove(victim)
            waiting.insert(0, victim)                 # re-enters admission like any new request

    while waiting and block_manager.has_free_block():
        seq = waiting.pop(0)
        block_manager.allocate(seq, blocks=1)          # only needs ONE free block to start
        running.append(seq)

    return worker.forward_pass(running)                # one prefill/decode step over `running`
```

Two things worth noticing here that the rest of this post is really about:
admitting a new sequence only ever needs `block_manager` to find **one**
free block, not a reservation — that's PagedAttention paying off inside the
scheduler — and a preempted sequence re-enters `waiting` through the exact
same code path as a brand-new request, which is why the "Scheduling policy
and preemption" section below doesn't need a separate mechanism for it.

## Prefill vs. decode, and why mixing them is tricky

Two very different kinds of work flow through that same loop. **Prefill**
processes an entire prompt in one shot to build its initial KV cache — it's
compute-bound, since it's one big matmul over hundreds or thousands of
tokens. **Decode** generates one token per sequence per step — it's
memory-bound, since attention has to re-read the whole cached history for
comparatively little new compute.

Naively mixing them causes a real problem: if the scheduler admits a new
request with a 4,000-token prompt in the middle of a running batch, that
single prefill can dominate the step, stalling token generation for every
other sequence that was mid-decode. Every request in the batch feels a latency
spike, not just the new one.

vLLM's fix is **chunked prefill**: split a long prompt into fixed-size chunks
and interleave them with other sequences' decode steps, instead of running the
whole prompt at once. Each step has a token budget $B$ (vLLM's
`max_num_batched_tokens`, 512 by default); decode tokens — one per running
sequence — are scheduled first, and whatever budget is left over goes to the
next chunk of a pending prompt:

$$
\text{prefill chunk size} = \min(\text{remaining prompt tokens},\ B - \text{decode tokens this step})
$$

For a 2,000-token prompt arriving alongside 8 already-running sequences with
$B = 512$:

| Iteration | Decode tokens (8 running seqs) | Prefill chunk (new request) | Total |
| --------- | ------------------------------ | ---------------------------- | ----- |
| 1 | 8 | 504 (chunk 1 of 4) | 512 |
| 2 | 8 | 504 (chunk 2 of 4) | 512 |
| 3 | 8 | 504 (chunk 3 of 4) | 512 |
| 4 | 8 | 488 (chunk 4, remainder) | 496 |
| 5 | 9 (new request now decoding too) | – | 9 |

Decode never waits behind the full prompt — it's prioritized every step, and
the big prefill is smeared across four iterations instead of stalling
everyone for one giant one. In vLLM's V1 engine, chunked prefill is on by
default whenever possible, precisely because mixed batches (some sequences
decoding, one or two prefilling) are the normal case under load, not an edge
case to special-case around.

## Scheduling policy and preemption under memory pressure

Sometimes there just isn't a free block for a running sequence's next token —
every physical block is already owned by some sequence. When that happens,
the scheduler **preempts** one of the running sequences (by default, the most
recently added, to protect requests that have been running the longest) and
moves it out of the `running` queue. vLLM supports two ways to do this:

- **Recompute** — drop the sequence's KV blocks entirely. When it's
  re-admitted later, it just reruns prefill on the tokens it already
  generated. Zero memory-transfer cost, but it burns the compute again.
- **Swap** — copy the sequence's physical blocks out to CPU memory instead of
  discarding them, and copy them back over PCIe when it's re-admitted. Saves
  the recompute, costs host-device bandwidth.

vLLM's V1 engine defaults to recompute, since for typical sequence lengths
re-running prefill is cheap relative to a round-trip over PCIe. Either way,
a preempted sequence goes back into the waiting queue and re-enters the same
admission process as a brand-new request — preemption and normal scheduling
share the same machinery.

## Distributed serving: tensor and pipeline parallelism

Once a model doesn't fit on one GPU, vLLM has to split it across several.
**Tensor parallelism** shards each layer's weight matrices (the attention
projections, the MLP) column- or row-wise across $N$ GPUs; every GPU computes
its shard of a layer's math on the same input, and an NCCL all-reduce
combines the partial results before the next layer:

That all-reduce happens *every layer*, so tensor parallelism wants GPUs
connected by something fast (NVLink within a node) — it doesn't scale well
across nodes on ordinary networking. For multi-node serving, vLLM also
supports **pipeline parallelism**: instead of splitting each layer, whole
groups of layers are assigned to different GPU stages, and micro-batches flow
through the pipeline so no stage sits idle waiting on the next. The two
compose — tensor-parallel within a node, pipeline-parallel across nodes — and
vLLM also supports expert parallelism specifically for mixture-of-experts
models, routing each token to whichever GPU holds the expert it was routed to.

## The V1 rewrite: pulling the scheduler out of the hot path

Everything above describes the logical architecture, but where each piece
*runs as a process* has changed. In vLLM's original engine, the API server,
scheduler, and detokenizer all lived in one Python process alongside the
model workers — at high request rates, ordinary Python/CPU overhead
(tokenizing, scheduling, streaming responses) started to compete with the
GIL for time the model loop needed.

[vLLM V1](https://vllm.ai/blog/2025-01-27-v1-alpha-release) (announced
January 2025) splits this into a separate `EngineCore` process, running just
the scheduler and model executor, talking to the API/frontend process over a
ZeroMQ socket instead of sharing a thread with it. The practical effect is
that CPU-side request handling can no longer stall the GPU loop, and the
scheduler treats prefill and decode uniformly under one token-budget policy —
which is exactly what makes chunked prefill vLLM's default behavior rather
than a special mode you opt into.

## Advantages and disadvantages

**Advantages:**

- Fully open-source and Python-native — easy to read, extend, and debug, and
  it tracks new Hugging Face model architectures quickly.
- Broad hardware support beyond NVIDIA: AMD ROCm, Google TPU, and Intel Gaudi
  backends exist alongside CUDA.
- PagedAttention plus prefix caching give it some of the best memory
  efficiency of any serving engine, which is what continuous batching needs
  to actually pack GPUs full.
- Chunked prefill and preemption are handled by the scheduler itself, so
  latency-sensitive and throughput-sensitive workloads share one engine
  without hand-tuned request routing.
- A large, active OSS ecosystem: quantization formats (AWQ, GPTQ, FP8), LoRA
  multi-adapter serving, and structured-output integrations all land in vLLM
  quickly.

**Disadvantages:**

- It's still a Python/PyTorch runtime under the hood. CUDA graphs and
  `torch.compile` claw back a lot of the interpreter overhead, but a
  dynamically-scheduled Python engine has a higher CPU-side floor than a
  compiled, static-graph runtime.
- More moving parts to reason about: scheduler, block manager, cache engine,
  and workers all interact, and tuning `max_num_batched_tokens`, preemption
  mode, and parallelism together is genuinely nontrivial.
- Being general-purpose across models and hardware means it's not as tightly
  co-optimized to one vendor's kernels as a compiled, hardware-specific
  engine — see the TensorRT-LLM post below.
- Its prefix reuse is block-level (paged blocks matching a hash of their
  content), not the radix-tree structure SGLang builds its whole scheduler
  around — see the next post.

## Where this leads

vLLM's story is: memory efficiency (PagedAttention) plus iteration-level
scheduling (continuous batching, chunked prefill) equals a Python-native
engine that keeps the GPU full without giving up flexibility. The next post
looks at [SGLang](/posts/sglang-radixattention-explained/), which pushes
prefix reuse further with a radix tree instead of independent per-sequence
block tables, then [TensorRT-LLM](/posts/tensorrt-llm-explained/), which
spent its first two years taking the opposite approach entirely — compiling
a fixed, optimized engine ahead of time instead of scheduling dynamically in
Python — before converging on a PyTorch-native runtime of its own. The
[comparison post](/posts/vllm-vs-sglang-vs-tensorrt-llm/) puts all three
side by side.

## References

- Kwon et al., [_Efficient Memory Management for Large Language Model Serving with PagedAttention_](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.06180) (SOSP 2023) — PagedAttention and vLLM's original architecture.
- Yu et al., [_Orca: A Distributed Serving System for Transformer-Based Generative Models_](https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi22/presentation/yu) (OSDI 2022) — iteration-level scheduling and selective batching, the origin of continuous batching, with the 36.9× throughput figure.
- [vLLM Architecture Overview](https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/design/arch_overview/) — the LLMEngine/scheduler/worker breakdown and the V1 multi-process design.
- [_vLLM V1: A Major Upgrade to vLLM's Core Architecture_](https://vllm.ai/blog/2025-01-27-v1-alpha-release) (vLLM Blog, Jan 2025) — the EngineCore/ZeroMQ rewrite and default chunked-prefill scheduling.
- [vLLM Optimization and Tuning](https://docs.vllm.ai/en/latest/configuration/optimization/) — `max_num_batched_tokens`, chunked prefill, and preemption-mode configuration.
Three posts, three engines: [vLLM](/posts/vllm-architecture-explained/)'s
scheduler and continuous batching,
[SGLang](/posts/sglang-radixattention-explained/)'s shared radix tree, and
[TensorRT-LLM](/posts/tensorrt-llm-explained/)'s arc from ahead-of-time
compilation to a PyTorch-native runtime of its own. That last post ended by
pointing out something that changes how this comparison has to be written:
as of today, all three are dynamically-scheduled PyTorch-native runtimes.
The clean "compiled vs. dynamic" split this series could have leaned on a
year ago no longer separates any of them. So this post doesn't crown a
throughput winner — cross-vendor benchmark numbers were never apples-to-apples
to begin with, and now the architectures aren't even different enough to
make that comparison meaningful. Instead, it lines the three up on what
actually still differs, and ends with a decision framework.

## Table of contents

## What's no longer a differentiator

All three engines now reach for the same lightweight JIT toolkit instead of
a separate ahead-of-time build step: `torch.compile`-based kernel fusion,
plus CUDA graph capture for common execution shapes. vLLM lists
"`torch.compile` optimizations" and "CUDA graph acceleration" as core
features. SGLang has both automatic CUDA graph capture and its own
`torch.compile` integration — plus a feature it calls **Piecewise CUDA
Graph**, the same name TensorRT-LLM uses for the equivalent optimization it
adopted once it moved off compiled `.engine` files. Two of the three engines
in this comparison independently converged on the same name for the same
idea. None of the three has a separate, portable, ahead-of-time build
artifact anymore — the "compile once, deploy anywhere" bet TensorRT-LLM
originally made didn't survive contact with how fast the other two could
iterate without it.

## Batching strategy

All three admit and evict sequences mid-batch every step rather than
waiting for a static batch to finish — the iteration-level idea from Orca
that the [vLLM post](/posts/vllm-architecture-explained/) covered in depth.
What differs is what else shapes the scheduling decision:

| Engine | Term | Distinguishing detail |
| --- | --- | --- |
| vLLM | Continuous batching + chunked prefill | Preemption (recompute or swap) when memory runs out |
| SGLang | Continuous batching + cache-aware scheduling | Queue ordered by longest-matched-prefix-first, not just arrival order |
| TensorRT-LLM | In-flight batching + Chunked Context | Chunk size must be a multiple of the KV-cache block size |

SGLang is the one that changes the scheduling policy itself around its
cache, rather than treating scheduling and caching as separate concerns.

## KV-cache and prefix reuse

This is where the three genuinely diverge, and it's the closest thing this
series has to a real architectural fork line:

| Engine | Structure | Reuse granularity |
| --- | --- | --- |
| vLLM | Per-sequence block tables (PagedAttention) | Block-level, matched by content hash |
| SGLang | One shared radix tree, engine-wide | Automatic, cross-request, reference-counted eviction |
| TensorRT-LLM | Per-sequence paged KV cache | Block-level, no cross-request tree structure |

vLLM and TensorRT-LLM both reuse cache at block granularity, matched
per-sequence. SGLang is the outlier: RadixAttention makes reuse a property
of one shared structure the whole engine sees, not something each sequence
negotiates independently — the payoff being automatic reuse across
requests that share a prefix, with no application-level cache-key
management, at the cost of only recognizing exact prefix matches (see the
[SGLang post](/posts/sglang-radixattention-explained/) for the reference
counting and eviction mechanics).

## Quantization

All three support the major formats — FP8, INT8, and 4-bit weight-only
formats (AWQ/GPTQ) — and the tooling has converged as much as the
architectures have: NVIDIA's
[TensorRT Model Optimizer](https://github.com/NVIDIA/TensorRT-Model-Optimizer),
originally TensorRT-LLM's own calibration tool, is now framework-agnostic
and also quantizes models for vLLM and SGLang deployments. The practical
difference by this point is less "which formats" and more which hardware
generation each format targets — FP8 on Hopper/Blackwell, INT4 for
memory-constrained deployments, NVFP4 on the newest Blackwell hardware —
which is really the hardware-portability question below wearing a
quantization hat.

## Structured-output support

Constrained decoding shows the same tooling-consolidation pattern as
quantization: a wave of engine-specific implementations converging onto
shared grammar engines.

| Engine | Backend(s) | Note |
| --- | --- | --- |
| vLLM | `auto` — tries XGrammar first, falls back to Outlines | `lm-format-enforcer` also exists; the vLLM team found it unreliable on long-context cases and deprioritized it |
| SGLang | xgrammar (default since v0.4, Dec 2024) | Originally its own compressed FSM (see the SGLang post), since generalized into xgrammar |
| TensorRT-LLM | XGrammar or LLGuidance | No stated default — must be explicitly enabled via a `guided_decoding_backend` config |

vLLM's own numbers for the XGrammar integration: "up to 5× improvement in
TPOT under load" (vLLM Blog, Jan 2025) — another vendor-reported, dated
figure, not a cross-engine claim.

## Hardware and portability

This is the one dimension that hasn't blurred at all:

SGLang documents the broadest hardware support of the three. vLLM covers
the same set minus Huawei Ascend. TensorRT-LLM is NVIDIA-only — the direct
consequence of being NVIDIA's own project, and the clearest, least
contestable differentiator in this whole comparison.

## Deployment complexity

| Engine | Standard path | Multi-replica story |
| --- | --- | --- |
| vLLM | `AsyncLLMEngine` + OpenAI-compatible API server | Official **vLLM Production Stack** — Kubernetes-native, Helm-deployable, with prefix-aware routing across replicas |
| SGLang | Similar single-server story | Its own **cache-aware load balancer** (v0.4, Dec 2024), routing requests to whichever replica already has the matching prefix cached |
| TensorRT-LLM | `trtllm-serve` (standalone, supports disaggregated prefill/decode), or Triton + `tensorrtllm_backend` | **NVIDIA NIM** — prebuilt containers that can themselves select TensorRT-LLM, vLLM, *or* SGLang as the backend per target GPU/model |

vLLM's Production Stack router and SGLang's cache-aware load balancer solve
the identical problem the identical way: route each request to the
replica most likely to already hold its prefix cached. TensorRT-LLM's NIM
packaging solves a different problem — picking a backend at all, from a
menu that now includes its two competitors in this series.

All three converge on the same client-facing surface, too. Point the same
`openai` client at three different ports and the application code doesn't
change at all — everything this post has covered so far is what's running
behind that URL, not what the caller has to know about it:

```python
from openai import OpenAI

engines = {
    "vLLM": OpenAI(base_url="http://vllm-host:8000/v1", api_key="EMPTY"),
    "SGLang": OpenAI(base_url="http://sglang-host:30000/v1", api_key="EMPTY"),
    "TensorRT-LLM": OpenAI(base_url="http://trtllm-host:8000/v1", api_key="EMPTY"),
}

for name, client in engines.items():
    resp = client.chat.completions.create(
        model="my-model",
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": "Say hi in five words."}],
    )
    print(name, "->", resp.choices[0].message.content)
```

Nothing above names a scheduler, a cache structure, or a backend — the
`base_url` is the only line that differs. Everything this comparison has
covered lives entirely on the other side of that URL.

## Ecosystem and governance

Worth being precise here, since these terms get used loosely:

| Engine | Relationship to PyTorch | Named production users (self-reported) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| vLLM | **PyTorch Foundation-hosted project** (announced May 2025) — actual governance, not a listing | Amazon (Rufus), LinkedIn |
| SGLang | Joined the **PyTorch ecosystem** (Mar 2025) — an ecosystem listing, not a governance transfer | xAI (Grok 3), Microsoft Azure (DeepSeek R1 on AMD MI300X) |
| TensorRT-LLM | NVIDIA's own project, Apache 2.0, no foundation affiliation | Validated via NVIDIA's own MLPerf submissions rather than named third-party deployments |

vLLM's relationship to the PyTorch Foundation is categorically stronger
than SGLang's — "officially governed and administered under the PyTorch
Foundation's neutral and transparent governance model," per the
Foundation's own announcement, versus SGLang's ecosystem-directory listing.
By the same May 2025 announcement, vLLM had 46,500+ GitHub stars and 1,000+
contributors; its own year-in-review post put 2024 growth at 14k→32.6k
stars, 190→740 contributors, and 6k→27k monthly downloads.

## Decision framework

- **Choose vLLM** if you want the broadest hardware portability, the
  largest and most formally governed OSS ecosystem (PyTorch
  Foundation), and your workload doesn't have the kind of heavy
  cross-request prefix overlap that would specifically reward SGLang's
  radix tree.
- **Choose SGLang** if your workload has real prefix-sharing structure —
  shared system prompts and few-shot batches, multi-turn chat, or
  tree-of-thought/self-consistency agent programs built on `fork`/`join` —
  where RadixAttention's automatic cross-request cache reuse pays off, or
  if you need hardware support broader than NVIDIA and AMD.
- **Choose TensorRT-LLM** if you're already committed to NVIDIA hardware
  and want the deepest speculative-decoding support of the three (Medusa,
  EAGLE-1/2/3, lookahead decoding) or NVIDIA's own MLPerf-tuned performance
  work, and NVIDIA-only lock-in is an acceptable trade.

## Where this leaves the series

vLLM, SGLang, and TensorRT-LLM started from three different bets: memory
efficiency plus iteration-level scheduling; one shared cache structure the
scheduler is built around; and compiling a fixed engine ahead of time. Only
the middle one still looks like its original bet today — the first
converged toward it from a simpler starting point, and the third abandoned
its founding architecture entirely to land in the same place. What's left
that actually distinguishes them isn't "how they schedule" anymore; it's
what they optimize the shared architecture *for* — SGLang for automatic
prefix reuse, TensorRT-LLM for NVIDIA-specific depth (quantization,
speculative decoding), and vLLM for being the broadly-portable,
formally-governed default. Pick the one whose bet matches your workload.
